- **Measuring a hard landing.** The first version used the suspension's compression speed, which the sound already used for thumps. Driving the trail tests with damage on showed it spikes to 4–18 m/s at 3–8 km/h whenever a rock step rises under a slowly rolling wheel (the ray-cast wheel's ground jumps up in one step), and 18 of the trail tests broke the car. The landing is now measured as how fast that corner of the body was falling when its wheel landed or bottomed (`Vehicle.wheelLandings`), which a step rising under a crawling wheel doesn't produce. Crawling up, over and down every trail and park line with both vehicles measured at most 3.6 m/s; a tyre is cut above 4.4 m/s on rock (about a metre's drop) and a corner bends above 5.6 m/s (about 1.6 m).
- **The engine.** A crash is a sudden change of the body's speed (over 6 m/s in one step) pointing backwards along the body: something hit the nose. Chassis knocks hurt it too, but belly scrapes on the trails measured up to about 4400 N·s, so only knocks over 8000 count (a 50 km/h crash into a wall is about 20000); a crash is not also counted as a knock. Upside down with the engine running costs 6% a second. In Realistic, a flooding stall costs 35% (water drawn into the cylinders).
- **Checked against the trails.** With Realistic damage forced on, the trail, crawl course, park, Crawler Creek, Camel Trophy, POI, tools and wet-weather tests (192) all pass, and the only damage is from the two tests meant to cause it: the Deep Crossing stall and the rollover. The Camel Trophy car's drive round the whole of Crawler Creek now runs with Realistic damage and expects none.
- **Repairs.** A field repair (Mild) or fitting the one spare (Realistic) comes from the tool menu, needs the car stopped and fairly level, and holds it on the handbrake while it runs (6 s and 8 s). Driving into a campsite, or Return to last campsite, puts everything right and the spare back. Changing the setting brings damage within what the new setting allows (Off puts everything right).
- **Showing it.** A message names the part and corner; a small top-down car outline in the status strip colours each corner and the engine amber or red; a prompt offers the repair when stopped; a badly hurt engine smokes from under the bonnet; a flat tyre is drawn squashed. Sounds: a crack and hiss for a cut tyre, a crunch for a bent corner, a knock for engine damage and the stall clunk when it's wrecked.
- **The tool menu** now has eight options (headlights in v0.14, repair now). Laid out on the old 120 px circle, neighbouring options overlapped from seven on (v0.14 shipped with that). The options now sit on an oval that widens until no two overlap, and grows taller instead on a phone held upright, always on the screen (`ringRadii`, tested on desktop and both phone orientations); upright phones get slightly narrower options so seven fit side by side at the bottom.

### v0.16: lit dials at night

- **What was asked.** The user found the dash gauges hard to read at night. With the headlights on, only the needles glowed, and faintly (0.7); the dials' markings were drawn in the same metal material as the bezels and stayed dark.
- **Dash lights.** The markings now have their own material slot (`DialMarks`), dark by day and lit with the headlights, as real dash lighting is wired to the light switch. Its glow is multiplied by each mark's own colour (a small shader change), so the ivory marks glow ivory and the redline and low-oil marks red, from one material. The needles glow brighter (1.3). Both vehicles' dials are built by the same code, so both get it; a test checks the dash has the markings and that they light only with the headlights.
- Screenshot: `docs/screenshots/night/dash-lit.png`.

### v0.17: fixes from the v0.16 playtest

Every item in `docs/playtest-v0.16.md` was worked through.

- **Units and a speed readout (items 1 and 6).** The km/h / mph setting now changes the speed in the status strip and the crawl gauges (×0.621 for mph; `formatSpeed` is unit-tested). The status strip shows speed and gear whenever the crawl gauges don't, so there is always a speed on screen. The dash speedometer stays km/h: it is drawn as a period Australian 40 Series dial, and README says so.
- **Phone top row (item 2).** Upright, the camera buttons drop their labels: "View" without the preset name, and ⟲ for Reset view. With the top row's gaps tightened, the four buttons on the left and the two on the right no longer meet at 375 px wide. The ⛶ button stays where it was; moving it to the pause menu would hide it from the people most likely to want it. An e2e check asserts no two visible touch buttons overlap at 390×844 and 375×667.
- **Welcome screen (item 3).** Focus on Drive uses `preventScroll`, so a phone opens at the top. The pause menu marks its focused button with a class rather than calling `focus()`, so it needed nothing.
- **Tree strikes (item 4).** The headless repro showed the car was not bouncing straight back: hitting the trunk off-centre, Rapier's one-step depenetration turned 18 m/s forwards into 6 m/s sideways and a 3.7 rad/s spin, so the car's forward-axis speed read as reversing, and it then slid down the bank and rolled. Restitution was not the cause (trees use the default 0, the car 0.05, and CCD was already on). The fix models the crumple: when the chassis loses more than 5 m/s of horizontal speed in one step while already moving faster than that, it keeps only 12% of its remaining velocity and 20% of its spin (`crashTuning`). Only a loss of speed counts, so a teleport or a test that sets a velocity directly doesn't trigger it. The impact the damage and dent systems see includes the speed the crumple absorbed, so a nose-first crash still hurts the engine. A headless test drives into the playtest's tree at 30 and 60 km/h and checks the car doesn't reverse faster than 3 km/h, slide off at more than 6 km/h or roll.
- **Tool menu on short screens (item 5).** The ring starts from a quarter of the screen's shorter side (at most 120 px) instead of a fixed 120 px, and grows sideways first, so on a phone held sideways it stays low. The compass, status strip, course hint and crawl gauges fade to 12% while the menu is open.
- **Controls on a phone (item 7).** Opened by touch, Pause → Controls lists the touch controls (Action / Touch, from `touchControlsTable`). The table scrolls inside the card, so Back is always at the bottom.
- **Paper map (item 8).** Scroll-wheel and pinch zoom (up to 4×) about the pointer or the fingers, and drag to move, clamped so the map always fills the sheet. A sheet under 600 px (a phone) opens at 2.5× centred on the car; a desktop opens on the whole map as before. The hint is inside the sheet and names only the controls of the device in use.
- **Pause menu at short heights (item 9).** Below 640 px tall the card's padding and fonts shrink, and if the screen is also at least 700 px wide the main page's buttons sit in two columns. Arrow keys still step through them in order.
- **High Country start (item 10).** River Flat Camp's start moved from (-150, 238) heading 315° to (-152, 254) heading 107°, on the Mine track facing east. Holding W drives along the track past the Camp Rock Course and stays on it (checked headlessly for 8 s, to 60 km/h).
- **Shrub through the bonnet (item 11).** No shrubs within 6 m of any place's start position (`groundCover.spawnClearance`). Shrubs have no collider, so this is only about what the start looks like.
- **Crawler Creek gates (item 12).** `GateCount` (in `src/sim/gateCount.ts`, unit-tested) watches the car's centre: moving between a gate's flags in its direction of travel passes it. Passing a gate beyond the next one counts those in between as missed. The count starts at whichever gate is driven through first, so starting part way round misses nothing; going back through a gate does nothing; a jump of more than 5 m in one step (a teleport or campsite return) crosses nothing. The HUD shows "Gate N / 52" under the compass (the scored run's clock takes its place while a run is on), a soft single bell note per gate, a "Missed gate N" message, and a result card after gate 52. It is kept in the game rather than in `CrawlRuns` because it has no clock or penalties and is about the whole loop rather than one section. Flag hits are not counted: the flags have no colliders, and the playtest asked for the minimal version.
- **Minor items.** Dust is a smoother, smaller sphere (icosahedron detail 2, 0.3–1.3 m, 1.2–2.4 s) whose opacity falls off towards its silhouette, instead of a large flat-shaded 20-sided one. The headlight pool's hard edge came from the beam being bright enough to stay at full white almost to the edge of the cone, so a wider penumbra alone barely showed. The beams now fade across all or most of the cone (penumbra 0.6/0.5/0.45 → 1/0.9/0.85 for low, high and roof), the cones are 4° wider and the intensities about 15–20% lower (700/1700/1800 → 560/1450/1550). Low beam still lights the track ahead and high beam still reaches the trees. View cycling (C) and the gauge toggle (I) count presses per frame, as the pause menu does. The test ground slab is now 3 km across, so `drive-lab`'s 30 s run stays on it (93.6 km/h at the end).

### v0.18: flag touches on Crawler Creek

- **What counts.** A flag is touched when its stake (plus 6 cm for the stake and pennant) is inside the car seen from above: a rectangle as long and wide as the vehicle's body boxes and tyres (`footprintSize`, 4.15 × 1.84 m for the short-wheelbase 4WD, 4.9 × 1.72 m for the Perentie), centred on the car and turned to its heading. Crawler Creek's gates are 3–4 m wide, so driving the middle leaves at least 0.6 m each side. The flags still have no colliders: a real marker flag is a thin stake that bends, and making it solid would stop the car or throw it, so the touch is worked out in `GateCount` rather than by physics.
- **Once per flag per lap.** A car sitting on a flag, or backing up and touching it again, would otherwise count it every step. RC crawler rules often count each touch; one per flag keeps a slow, careful line through a tight gate from piling up points.
- **When a touch belongs to a lap.** The lap starts at the first gate driven through, but the car's nose reaches that gate's flags before its centre crosses the gate, so touches made while no lap is on are kept aside and those on the starting gate are added when the lap starts. In the same way the car's tail can still touch the last gate's flags after its centre has passed, so the lap's result is held until the car is clear of them.
- **Feedback.** A light knock (the chassis knock sound at 0.35), a short "Touched gate 13's red flag" message, the count under the compass ("Gate 14 / 52 · 2 flags", in the accent colour like a missed gate) and the flags in the lap result card.

# Over the Cape

The sections for v0.01 to v0.99 are in [decisions/v0.md](decisions/v0.md). This file goes on from v1.00; a new version's section goes at the end.

## v1.00: ghost car

Steef picked the ghost car from the remaining ideas, to race your best run on the CREB Track (ROADMAP item 3).

**Recording.** Every run records the car's position, its full rotation (so it climbs and leans with the ground) and the wheels' mean suspension travel every 0.25 s of driving time, the run's own clock that stops while paused (`src/sim/ghost.ts`). Restarting from a camp drops the recording after that camp's time, so the trace stays one continuous drive. A run resumed from a saved camp after the game was closed has no record of the driving before it, so it makes no ghost; the finish card says so.

**Which run is the ghost.** The first finish, then any finish quicker than the ghost. It is the quickest run, not the highest score, because the ghost is about racing. The vehicle of that run is kept with it, so a ghost in the Troopy stays a Troopy when you race it in the 40. Test mode can race a ghost but never saves one.

**Saving.** The trace is kept under its own browser key (`overthecape.save.v1.ghost.creb`), apart from the main save, because a long run is several hundred kilobytes (about 30 bytes a pose, 14,400 poses an hour). If storage is full, the finish card says the ghost was not saved and nothing else is affected.

**Drawing.** The ghost is the same detailed vehicle model in one pale blue-white, see-through material with a faint glow so it stays visible at dusk and at night (`src/render/ghostView.ts`). It has no physics: it cannot be hit and drives straight through you. Between poses it is blended smoothly. Once its run is over it waits at the finish.

**How far ahead.** The pause menu has a Ghost cell: how far behind or ahead you are, comparing your time with the ghost's time at the nearest point of its path, and the ghost's total time. The finish card says by how much you beat it, or how far ahead it finished.

**Not done.** No live gap on the driving HUD (the pause menu only), and no setting to hide the ghost. Both are small follow-ups if wanted.

## v1.01: packing before you leave

Steef picked three ideas from Over The Hill: packing before you leave, a map that fills in as you drive, and more hidden finds. This update is the first. Over The Hill buys upgrades with points; for a game meant to be hard, the closer fit is choosing what to carry under a weight limit, so every extra jerry can or spare costs something on the track.

**Packing list.** New Journey has a **Packing** section under Vehicle & difficulty: fuel in the tank (10 L steps from 10 L to a full tank), jerry cans 0–4, spare wheels 0–3 and tyre plugs 0–6, and **Standard load** (the episode's load plus the vehicle's own extras, as before). Planks stay at two: the washout needs them. The list is kept per vehicle in the save (`packs`), and a new run's checkpoint records what it set out with (`packed`), so a restart from camp and the finish score both use it. Continue resumes the saved camp and ignores the list.

**Weights and limit** (`src/data/packing.ts`). Fuel 0.8 kg/L, a full jerry can with its steel can 22 kg, a spare wheel and tyre 30–60 kg by vehicle (100 kg for the Unimog's 1.1 m wheel), plugs next to nothing. Each vehicle may carry its standard load plus a fixed allowance (90 kg on the Camel Trophy car up to 400 kg on the Unimog), chosen so a short car can't take everything but a big tourer can take a lot more. These are game numbers rounded from published wheel and payload figures, not measured.

**Physics.** The tuned masses already carry the standard load, so the vehicle adds only the difference (`Vehicle.payload`, negative when packed light), worked out every step from what is on board, so burning fuel and pouring in cans lighten it again. The body's mass and inertia follow the payload once it changes by a kilogram. Rapier scales spring force with the body's mass, so the springs are held at the tuned mass's rate and a heavy load squats the car. Mud sink is multiplied by (mass ratio)³, between 0.7 and 1.6: about 15% deeper with 100 kg extra on a 1.6 t car. Fuel use and engine heat already follow engine work, so a heavier car burns more and runs hotter on the climbs without a separate rule. The winch's pull stays rated on the tuned mass.

**Score.** "Fuel and kit" now counts what is left out of what was packed: fuel as before against the packed fuel and cans (it used the episode's 70 L, whatever the vehicle), kit as spares plus a third of the plugs against the same of the pack (it was against a fixed two). A standard-load run that uses a plug now loses a few points it didn't before; packing more no longer makes the kit score easier to keep.

**Changed for every run.** Even on the standard load, the car gets lighter as it burns fuel (about 25 kg over a CREB run), which it didn't before.

## v1.02: history and lesson notes in the top right corner

Steef found the lesson card ("Lesson 1 of 4, Low range") at the bottom of the screen covered the track, and the bottom right is where the low-range crawl gauges go. History and lesson notes, and messages, now sit small in the top right corner in every HUD style, on a computer and on a phone. This replaces the v0.90 choice of putting history notes along the bottom.

**Where.** The note card moved into the HUD's top-right stack (`.notices`), under the Discovered card and the messages, so they stack in one column instead of overlapping. On a computer it is 320 px wide; on a phone about half the screen width (260 px at most) with smaller type, and it scrolls if a note is longer than about a third of the screen upright (40% sideways), so it stays clear of the thumb buttons and the prompt above them. The prompts no longer move up for a note.

**Phones keep driving.** A note used to stop the game on a phone and come up as a sheet from the bottom, because there was no room for it beside the buttons. In the corner there is room, so the game no longer stops: the note shows while you drive and closes on a tap or after its reading time, as on a computer. The stop-and-read mode is still in `HistoryCard` (`reading`) but nothing uses it now.

## v1.03: smoother under the trees

Steef: "It's really choppy on mobile among the trees on Bloomfield track also".

**What was measured.** Same set-up as v0.99 (local build, phone-sized window, Low graphics), 250 m before the Bloomfield creeks. Trees were 55% of the frame. Drawing the forest into the 1024 shadow map took about a quarter of the frame on its own: from the sun the canopy is many layers of cut-out leaves. A 512 map cost a third as much. Separately, every 0.2 s the game checked all 45,000 trees' distance to pick their detail level in one go, a regular hitch on a phone's slower processor.

**Fix.**

- Low graphics, the default on a phone, draws the shadow map at 512 instead of 1024. The sun's shadows have a soft edge (radius 3), so side-by-side screenshots at 1024, 768 and 512 look the same. Medium and High keep their sizes.
- Trees are checked a slice each frame, a full pass still every 0.2 s, so there is no single heavy frame. A jump (teleport, restart) still does them all at once.

The whole frame there is 17% faster (944 to 785 ms on the software renderer).

**Not done.** Up close, the cut-out leaves still cost the most: the 250 nearest trees are about 30% of the frame. Making them cheaper without changing how the forest looks needs a different approach (for example a lower draw resolution only while the phone is struggling), left for a later update if this isn't enough.

## v1.04: the map fills in as you drive

The second of Steef's three picks from Over The Hill, where the map starts blank, fills in as you travel and shows question marks near places not yet found.

**Seen ground** (`src/sim/seenGround.ts`). A 25 m grid over the map; every cell within 150 m of the car is marked as it moves (only after it has gone 5 m, so it costs nothing standing still). 150 m shows the track with some country either side but leaves most of the 1.3 km-wide map blank until you take a side track. The grid is kept as bits, base64 in the checkpoint (`seen`, about 2 KB for the CREB), so a restart from camp gets the map as it was there, and a new run starts blank, the same as places found. A save from before v1.04 has no `seen`: the ground round each place found by then stands in for it.

**Drawing** (`src/ui/paperMap.ts`). The base map is drawn as before; over it, blank paper with the seen cells cut out as circles, blurred 3 px so the edge looks pencilled rather than square. The frame (border, title, north arrow and scale) moved to its own layer drawn on top, so the blank paper doesn't cover it. Places found keep their icon and name. A place not found yet, within 250 m of seen ground, shows a pencilled question mark. The orientation lessons count as places, so a few question marks crowd the start of a new run; they clear as you drive the lessons. Test mode shows the whole map.

**Also.** The unit test for the welcome screen's starting choice now expects the packed load added in v1.01.

## v1.05: hidden finds

The last of Steef's three picks from Over The Hill, where crates sit at scenic spots for anyone who looks round.

**Three more finds with no sign or hut** (`src/data/episodes/crebFinds.ts`), at places people stop, left by other travellers: a spare wheel at the end of the Roaring Meg Falls track, a full jerry can at the back of the range lookout's flat, and two tyre plugs at the end of the Wujal Wujal Falls track. They use the existing finds: stop within 7 m and it goes on board, once, kept in camp checkpoints. The spare wheel is the first find that gives a spare; it lies flat on its crate (`src/render/finds.ts`). Each spot was checked for slope (under 15°) so the car can stop beside it. The lookout's crate is closer to the main line than the huts' (the test now asks for 15 m beyond a find's reach, not 30), since the car pulls off the track into the lookout anyway; the falls tracks are dead ends you only drive to see the falls.

**On the map.** The paper map now also puts a question mark near anything still to be loaded once you've seen the ground close by, unless an unfound place beside it already has one. Once the place is found the question mark stays beside its name until the find is loaded, so a hut you passed without stopping still shows something is there.

With the packing list (v1.01), these give a light-packed run a way to make up a spare or fuel on the way, at the cost of the time spent on the side trips.

**Prompt.** The roadside prompt said a find was "by the hut"; it now says "on a crate", which fits the huts and the new finds.

## v1.06: vehicles to unlock

Steef asked for the 40 Series and the Camel Trophy Land Rover to be the only vehicles open at the start, with every other vehicle opened by a challenge that suits it (`src/data/unlocks.ts`).

| Vehicle       | Challenge                                                                          | Why this one                                          |
| ------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------- |
| Hilux         | Reach the Lions Den for the first time                                             | The everyday ute: the first reward                    |
| GQ Patrol     | Winch the bogged Patrol out on the ridge                                           | The stranded Patrol is already on the map             |
| LC79 V8       | Winch the bogged LandCruiser out at Donovan's Creek                                | The stranded 79 is already on the map                 |
| Troop Carrier | Load all five finds (two huts, three hidden) in one run                            | The long-range tourer: going everywhere               |
| Unimog        | Reach the Lions Den without attaching the winch                                    | The truck that goes almost anywhere                   |
| 80 Series     | Section bests under 2:00 up Big Red, 2:00 up Bob's Hill and 3:00 down the concrete | Built for hard tracks                                 |
| Perentie      | Army supply run: tow two fuel drums to the camp at Wujal Wujal                     | An Army task, reusing the Episode 4 trailer and drums |

- **Saved in the save file** as `unlocked`. A save from before v1.06 has nothing unlocked; if its last vehicle is now locked, the title screen falls back to the 40 Series. Continue then carries on from the saved camp in the 40 Series.
- **Locked vehicles** are greyed out under New Journey → Vehicle & difficulty with a "To unlock" list below, and greyed out with their challenge on the pause menu's Vehicle page. Test mode (`?dev`) opens all of them, as it opens every episode.
- **The winch counts from the start of the run.** It is kept in camp checkpoints (`winched`), so restarting from a camp after winching still counts it. Winching out a stranded driver uses the winch, so the Unimog and the rescue challenges need separate runs.
- **Section targets** use the section bests, so they can be met over several runs. The times (173 m of Big Red, 169 m of Bob's Hill, 380 m of the concrete) are first guesses: about 1.4 m/s on the climbs and 2.1 m/s on the descent.
- **The supply run** is a third Start option beside Start and Continue. It is a normal CREB run with the delivery trailer (the Army No. 5 behind a vehicle with a pintle hook, otherwise the box trailer) and two drums. Parking the upright trailer, both drums aboard, in the Wujal Wujal camp and stopping delivers them. After that you can unhitch and drive on. The run, its checkpoints and its finish count as usual. Restarting from a camp keeps the trailer as it was there (`supply` in the checkpoint).
- **Unlocks come as a card** on the HUD while driving. Those earned at the finish (Hilux, Unimog) are listed on the finish screen instead.

## v1.07: tougher tyres, sharp stones and tyres off the rim

Steef found the tyres popped too easily, and asked for random punctures from sharp rocks instead, with let-down tyres less likely to puncture but more likely to roll off the rim in a corner (`damageTuning.tyre`).

- **Tougher.** A rock landing cuts a tyre from 5 m/s of fall (about 1.3 m, was 4.4 m/s) and tears the sidewall from 6 m/s (was 5.4); a hole strike pinches from 4.4 (was 3.6), so a 30 cm washout hit squarely pinches from about 42 km/h instead of 35. (v1.08: spinning on rock still wears through in 6 s; 8 s let a high-range spin up the Roaring Meg ledge go uncut.)
- **Sharp stones.** Each metre a tyre rolls has a small chance of a slow puncture (cause `sharp`): 0.25 per km at 20 km/h on rock, 0.05 on gravel, 0.015 on dirt and clay, none on mud, black soil, sand, grass, water or the concrete strips (the ground says which with `smoothAt`, from the concrete tint). The chance scales with speed, half at a walk and twice at 45 km/h. Measured along the CREB's tracks, only about 550 m of 8 km is rock and 385 m of that is concrete, so rock alone would almost never puncture a run; the gravel and dirt rates make it about one run in three. The vehicle's own seeded random numbers decide, so a run repeats.
- **Let down.** The old setting had soft tyres cut more easily (75% of the usual speed). That is reversed: let down, a landing or hole strike needs 20% more to cut, and sharp stones are a third as likely (0.35×).
- **Off the rim.** With the tyres let down, past 4.2 m/s² of sideways acceleration (speed × yaw rate) the heavier-loaded tyre of each axle (the outside one) has a chance of 0.5 per second for each m/s² over of rolling off its rim: a new fault, `rim`, flat in about a second, that needs a spare (camp mends it). Measured on flat dirt, the 40 Series slides at about 4.7 m/s² (30 km/h with 0.3 steer, or full lock at 15 km/h); 40 km/h with a little steer (0.15) stays at 3.5, so moderate corners are safe.

## v1.08: tyre test fixes after v1.07

- The unit tests found four things v1.07 broke. Spinning on rock wears through in 6 s again (8 s let the high-range spin up the Roaring Meg ledge go uncut). The tyre tests now drop the car 1.5 m (1.25 m no longer cuts). A hole strike now pinches from 4.0 (v1.07 had 4.4, which left the paddock road's potholes unable to pinch either test vehicle at 47 km/h): a 30 cm washout hit squarely pinches from about 38 km/h, against 35 before v1.07. The fast paddock-road run needs a pinch from either vehicle, not both: the Perentie now gets through it uncut. The engine-heat test's stand-in vehicle gained the turn rate and random numbers the damage step now reads.

## v1.09: 80 Series body rebuilt from photographs

The 80 Series came into the game in v0.45 as a recovered draft that had never been compared with a real one. Steef asked for one vehicle to get a full evaluation and redesign with evidence; the 40 had just been done and the 60 Series was being built in another thread, so the 80 was picked. Eighteen Commons photographs (a black Japanese VX from every side, an Australian 1996 GXL front and rear, a late grille, two touring 80s) are now in `reference/vehicles/landcruiser-80-sooty/`, reduced to 2400 pixels wide, with credits in `SOURCES.md`.

- **Measured, then matched by overlay.** The side photograph was calibrated on its two hubs (2,850 mm apart). The studio camera was then placed to match it, and the model's outline drawn over the photo. The old body was a long box: its roof ran flat to a near-upright windscreen whose foot was 0.4 m too far forward, and its nose and upright tail each stood about 0.17 m beyond the real ones, so the skin was 4.70 m long against the photograph's 4.36 m. The new outline matches the roof, windscreen, tail, bumpers and mirror to a few centimetres. `SOURCES.md` lists each measurement next to the constant that copies it, and explains why the model (4.55 m over the factory bumpers) is shorter than the published 4.82 m.
- **New shell.** The bonnet edge rises from 0.62 m at the nose to 0.75 m at the pillar foot. The windscreen is curved in plan and raked from z −0.70 to −0.205. The roof dips to 1.215 m at the header, rises to 1.275 m at the gutters and has a 4.5 cm crown. Above the tail lamps the tail leans back 0.53 m for each metre up, as on the real wagon, with the split tailgate: an upper liftgate with its glass and a lower drop gate. The corners are rounded 7 cm in plan. The flares are superellipses (squarer than a circle, like the wide-body 80's), with the rear flare's tail stopping above the bumper end. The front has two rectangular lamps a side in one chrome surround, the clear corner lamp wrapping round, a deeper grille and the indicators under the lamps. The B and C pillars and the mirror sail are black, and there are louvres behind the rear quarter glass.
- **Panel joins are shared, not overlapped.** The side skins, wing tops, end faces, side frames and roof are built on the same sample rows, so each edge meets the next exactly instead of being hidden by an overlap. Close-ups of every join (roof edges, rear corners, the liftgate and D-pillar, under the doors, the A-pillar and scuttle) were checked before publishing.
- **Cab 15 cm back.** The raked windscreen would have cut through the old dash, so the seat, eye point, wheel, handbrake, dials and warning lamps all move back 0.15 m, with a new dashboard under the glass. The dial check in `tests/sooty80.test.ts` now starts its ray just in front of the wheel, wherever the cab is.
- **Gear refitted to the new body.** The bull bar, side rails and sliders, the snorkel (now up the right windscreen pillar, intake at z −0.256), the roof platform's feet, light bar and awning, and the rear bar and spare all follow the new nose and tail. The headlight beams, driving lamps and light bar lamps, the winch and tow points, and the collision boxes moved with them. The physics is otherwise unchanged.
- **Studio.** `?stock` draws the 80 with factory bumpers and no touring gear, and `?plain` puts it on a white backdrop, so the outline can be compared with a stock photograph.
- **Triangles.** The body is about 97,000 triangles with its touring gear, down from the old draft's 107,000: the shut lines, mouldings, drip rails and windscreen rubbers are light tubes with five or six sides instead of twelve.

## v1.10: the 60 Series wagon

Steef asked for a 60 Series, built to a higher standard than the 40, with as much research as it took. The game's tenth vehicle is an Australian 1985–86 HJ60 Standard (`hj60`): the standard-roof wagon with the 4.0 L 2H diesel six, the five-speed and barn doors, in a solid mid blue of the period, with a bull bar and winch, a rear bar, a gutter rack and a second spare added for the game. Steef chose its unlock: **finish the CREB in the 40 Series**.

- **Research.** Sixteen Commons photographs (CC0, CC BY, CC BY-SA and public domain) are in `reference/vehicles/landcruiser-60/` with credits and the published dimensions in `SOURCES.md`. `research-notes.md` keeps every figure with its source and a confidence mark, including where sources disagree: the Australian axle ratio (3.70 or 4.11; the game uses 4.111), and the rear door, which was barn doors on the Standard and a lift-up tailgate on the Deluxe G. Published: 2,730 mm wheelbase, 1,800 mm wide, 1,830 mm high, 1,475/1,460 mm track, 4,675 mm long on the base bumpers. The model matches the first four exactly; its skin is 4.57 m nose to tail, about 5.10 m over the bull bar and rear bar.
- **Matched by overlay.** `side-left-j60-mod-roof-tent.jpg` was calibrated on its hubs (2,730 mm apart) and the side render laid over it, with the lift taken out. The belt, the top of the glass, both creases, the A, B and C pillars, the door shuts, the window corners, the arches, the nose and the tail match it to a few centimetres. The front and rear three-quarters were compared with the Japanese Standard Roof STD (front left and rear left), the 1981 red J60 (front right), the Australian 1986 FJ60 (rear right) and the square-on US and Dutch fronts. `SOURCES.md` has the camera.
- **Body.** The sides are built in bands that lean in as the real panels do: tucked under below the lower crease, upright between the creases, leaning in above the shoulder crease through the handles and more steeply up the glasshouse to the drip rails. Each band's normals follow its lean, so the creases catch the light. The pressed flares stand out of the sides, the openings inside them follow the photographs' shape, and inner wheelhouses close them. The front is one piece: a crowned bonnet with its shut lines, the cowl and its vent, the black grille with TOYOTA in block letters, two round lamps in square chrome bezels and the indicators wrapping round the corners. The rear corners are rounded in plan. The barn doors split a little right of the middle (the left door is wider), each with its window, and carry the hinges, handles, tail lamps and the plate between them. The roof is crowned, with drip rails along both sides.
- **Details from the photographs.** The quarter window and its vent, the fuel flap on the right rear quarter, the side markers, the guard vent, LAND CRUISER on the front guards, door mirrors on stalks, mud flaps, and white split-rim wheels with lock rings and radial slots on 7.50R16 bar tyres, as on the Standard.
- **Panel joins.** Close-ups of every join were checked before publishing: the front corner and bonnet, the front arch, the A-pillar foot, the sills under the doors, the rear arch, the rear corners, the roof edges and the back of the roof. The bands share their boundary heights, so their edges meet; shut lines are thin dark rods split at the band edges.
- **Cab.** Two grey vinyl buckets and a bench behind, rubber mats, the padded dash with the instrument hood in front of the right-hand driver, a speedometer, tachometer and gauge in it, pendant pedals, the five-speed and transfer levers, vinyl door trims, visors and a headlining. No photograph of a 60's dash under a free licence was found, so the dash follows the published trim and the 40's layout. The wheel rim sits about 0.45 m from the driver's eye.
- **Driving.** 2.4 t with two aboard and the gear, leaf springs at both ends, the 2H's torque curve (241 Nm at 1,800 rpm, 76 kW at 3,500), redline 3,900, the H55F gears, 1.959 low range and 4.111 axles: about 120 km/h flat out. 90 L tank. The diesel six has its own sound settings (`twoHSynthTuning`). The collision boxes follow the body, the bull bar and the rack. Packing: 40 kg for a spare, 150 kg of room.
- **Underneath.** Ladder rails, crossmembers, gearbox, sump and tank; the exhaust runs inside the right frame rail so it stays out of the right rear wheel opening (`tests/hj60.test.ts` checks all four wheel openings are open). The first spare is slung under the back.
- **Triangles.** About 110,000 for the whole vehicle (the body 86,000), against about 91,000 for the 40 and 163,000 for the 80.
- **What still disagrees.** The bull bar hides the lower grille and the front bumper line, which were taken from the stock fronts. The split between the barn doors and the grille's height are judged from three-quarter views: no square-on rear of a stock barn-door car was found. A second batch of photographs (an Australian Standard on split rims, a dashboard, an engine bay) could not be downloaded in this update.

## v1.11: a Batocera build

The game runs on a Batocera PC as a Ports tile. `batocera/` holds a small Electron wrapper and `deploy.sh`, which builds the site and installs it over SSH. Nothing in `src/` changes except the version number.

- **Electron rather than a browser:** Batocera ships no browser, and a Ports entry needs one program that opens full screen and quits cleanly. Electron brings its own Chromium. It needs `libcups.so.2`, which Batocera lacks, so `deploy.sh` takes it from Debian 12.
- **Speed:** on a Ryzen 5 3500U the first drive of the track ran at 11 fps at the default 15 W on Medium, 40–45 fps at 30 W on Low, and about 60 fps at 30 W on Low drawn at 1280×720. So `deploy.sh` sets 30 W for this game, new saves start on Low, and the wrapper sets `devicePixelRatio` to 2/3 from a script added to `index.html`. Storm scenes were not measured.
- **Exit:** Batocera's exit hotkey sends Alt+F4. The wrapper quits on the key's release, because quitting on the press let EmulationStation catch the same key and restart. Holding Select + Start for 1.5 seconds is a backup.
- **Not tested with a physical controller.**

## v1.12: the 60 Series in sandy taupe, with the stripe kit

Steef asked for the 60 to be sandy taupe, with the decals.

- **Paint.** `#A8987F` replaces the v1.10 mid blue (`hj60Paint.ts`). It reads warm beige in sun and grey-brown in shade.
- **The decals** are the mid-1980s factory stripe kit, measured off the red 1984 car in `reference/vehicles/landcruiser-60/side-right-34-60-1984-mod.jpg`, pixel row by pixel row, with the door shut lines as the scale. Along both sides runs a band under the door handles: a brown pinstripe over an orange stripe at its top (0.54–0.55 and 0.50–0.52 m above the body origin), an orange stripe over a brown pinstripe at its bottom (0.31–0.32 and 0.29–0.30), closed at the back 2 cm before the rear corner. The lower lines stop either side of the rear wheel, as on the car. Ahead of the B-pillar three slashes lean forward at the top (0.75 m back for each metre down): an orange one, a wide brown one, and an orange one that opens into an outlined end on the front guard, closed by a narrow orange slash and a brown pinstripe behind the side marker. TOYOTA LAND CRUISER runs between them in small white capitals, reading towards the front on the right and towards the back on the left.
- **Colours.** No source was found for the factory stripe colours on a beige or taupe 60 (the forum pages that might say are blocked here). The kit on the red car is yellow and black; on taupe it is drawn in burnt orange and dark brown, the period's colours for light-brown paint. Each colour is one constant in `hj60Body.ts` if Steef wants it changed.
- **How it's drawn.** Flat shapes 2 mm proud of the upright band of the side, which is flat between the creases, in the paint material so they shine and get dirty with the body. Long pieces are cut into 15 cm lengths so they follow the panel into a dent. The handles, shut lines and the LAND CRUISER script sit over them, as on the car. The letters are boxes on a 10 × 10 grid. The kit adds under 2,000 triangles.
- **Compared** from both sides and with close-ups of the front door, the rear arch and the rear corner, beside the photograph.

## v1.13: smoother under the trees, round 2

Steef, after v1.03: "Still really choppy in trees on Bloomfield".

**What was measured.** A processor profile while the autopilot drove the Bloomfield Track (Low graphics, phone-sized window). Picking which trees to draw took about 4 ms a frame on a desktop, several times that on a phone. three.js rebuilt each tree's bounding sphere from its matrix on every draw, for every tile in view and again for the shadow map. The physics step and the rest of the frame were small beside it.

**Fix.**

- **Faster tree picking** (`src/render/treeCulling.ts`). Trees don't move, so each tree's bounding sphere is worked out once when the map loads (its middle-detail sphere, 20% larger to cover the other two). Each draw only tests those spheres against the view. It fills three's draw list the way `BatchedMesh.onBeforeRender` does in r186; if three is upgraded, check that its private fields still match. 4.3 ms falls to about 0.5 ms a frame. Screenshots before and after are the same, shadows included.
- **Lower resolution only while the graphics struggle** (`src/render/renderScale.ts`). When frames have been slower than about 36 a second for a second and the game's own code takes under 60% of the frame (so the graphics chip is what's slow), the picture is drawn 10% smaller, down to 70% on Low and 80% on Medium. High never changes. Once frames run faster than about 52 a second for 4 s, and at least 10 s after the last step down, it steps back up. It is off while tests drive a fixed number of steps a frame. `?debug` shows the current resolution.

I asked Steef for the `?debug` numbers on his phone, to tell whether the processor or the graphics chip is the limit. Until then, both are covered.

## v1.14: a phone test (`?bench`)

Steef, after v1.13: "Still quite choppy", on a Pixel 10a. The software renderer here can't tell whether the phone's processor or its graphics chip is what's slow, and each fix so far was aimed from desktop measurements. So the phone now measures itself.

`?bench` (`src/debug/bench.ts`) starts once Drive is pressed. The autopilot drives the Bloomfield Track 520 m out from the creeks at 9 m/s, and the frames are timed for 6 s at a time (the first 1.5 s skipped while things settle): as normal, at half resolution, without shadows, without trees, without ground cover and the meadow, then as normal again as a check. Each line gives the median frame rate, the median and 95th-percentile frame time and the game code's time per frame. The box also shows the screen size, pixel ratio and graphics setting. History notes are put away as they come up, and the automatic resolution is off for the test.

**The automatic resolution now steps down sooner.** Steef's `?debug` on the Pixel 10a, in the trees on the Bloomfield bridge approach: 39 fps, 25.8 ms a frame, with the game code at 10.6 ms (sim 1.7). So the graphics chip is the limit, but v1.13 only stepped down below about 36 fps (28 ms), so it never did. It now steps down below about 50 fps (20 ms), still only while the code is under 60% of the frame. It steps back up above about 56 fps. If a step up is slow again within 15 s, it waits twice as long (up to 2 minutes) before the next step up, so it doesn't keep switching back and forth.

The next fix is chosen from the phone's numbers: whatever setting makes the biggest jump is where the time goes.

## v1.15: the phone test at the slow spot, and lower resolution on High

Steef's v1.14 `?bench` result on the Pixel 10a: 411×784 points at a pixel ratio of 2.625, on **High** graphics. Every setting ran at 60 fps (17 ms a frame) with the game code at 11 to 19 ms. The stretch the autopilot drove, 520 m out from the creeks, was not where he sees 19 to 39 fps.

- **The test now parks at the slow spot.** It stops on the track at (111, −773) in the forest south of the Bloomfield bridge, facing along it, where his `?debug` showed 39 fps standing still, and times the settings there. Standing still also keeps every pass on the same view.
- **It also times Medium and Low graphics** (eight passes, about a minute). The setting is changed for the test only, not saved.
- **High can now step its resolution down to 60%** (it never could). On a phone, High draws up to twice the points (pixel ratio 2) with a 4096 shadow map; the shadow map doesn't change with the resolution.

Steef was told to try Medium in the meantime.

## v1.16: quieter place cards on a phone

Steef found the cards at the Bloomfield River bridge annoying on a phone: a large Discovered card and the full history note stacked down most of the right side of the view.

**One card per place.** While a history or lesson note is up, the Discovered card gives way to it (CSS hides `.discovery-card.found` while the note shows), on every screen, because the note already names the place. A timed run's result card still shows.

**Short notes on a phone.** On a touch screen a note opens short: the kind, the place, a lesson's keys, and two lines of text with "Tap to read more". It goes after 7 s if left alone. A tap opens the whole note with the usual reading time (about 200 words a minute, at least 12 s); the × closes it at once. A computer still shows the whole note, because it sits clear of the controls and a click closes it.

**Smaller Discovered card on a phone** (15 px name, 20 px icon), for places without a note.

## v1.17: no High graphics on a phone

Steef's Pixel 10a stayed choppy under the Bloomfield forest on High (20–39 fps, with the game's code taking only 10–14 ms of each frame, so the graphics chip was the limit), and was smooth on Medium. He doesn't need High on a phone.

**On a touch screen, Settings → Graphics offers Low and Medium only, and a phone saved on High draws Medium** (and the menu shows Medium as chosen). The saved setting is left as it is, so the same save on a computer still gets High. `qualityFor(level, touch)` in `src/data/quality.ts` gives the preset as drawn; the game and `?bench` use it. Computers are unchanged.

## v1.19: winching, camp and plank fixes

Five vehicle-simulation bugs, each verified headless before the change.

- **Winching a bogged vehicle no longer puts your own car into reverse.** While hooked to a bogged driver with the throttle off, the simulation held the car by pressing the brake pedal for you. But a brake pedal pressed at a standstill is how the car selects reverse, so it crept towards the bogged vehicle in gear −1 at about 2000 rpm for the whole pull (7.5 m in a headless run). It now pulls the handbrake instead; the hold brake was already on with no pedals pressed. A test winches a bogged car out and checks the winching car stays in gear 0 with no throttle and moves less than half a metre.
- **Back to camp cools a boiling engine, and a switched-off engine no longer loses health to heat.** The camp rest put the engine's health back to 1 but left its temperature where it was, and overheat damage was charged whenever the temperature was over the boil, running or not. A car returned to camp at 1.2 (boiling) with the engine off lost half its engine health in 90 s. Camp now resets the temperature to normal (0.5), and boiling only hurts while the engine runs: off, it just cools. The "repaired at camp" event also fires when only the heat needed putting right.
- **The steered wheel's axle direction had the wrong sign on z.** Rapier steers a wheel by turning its axle (1, 0, 0) about +y, which gives (cos s, 0, −sin s); the roll torque and the push a wheel gives a plank or the fallen tree used (cos s, 0, sin s), the axle of a wheel steered the other way, so at full lock the force handed to a plank pointed up to about 68° off. Confirmed headless: creeping the car along the true axle, the steered fronts resist like the rears (side impulses −53.6 / −53.5 against the rears' −58); along the code's direction they gave only −47 / −32. The direction now lives in one exported `steeredAxle` helper with a test against the controller's own side impulses. The tyre footprint rays had the same mirror in the rolling direction, (−sin s, 0, cos s) instead of (sin s, 0, cos s), and probe the right azimuth now. `STEER_SIGN` was right all along: steer +1 turns the car right.
- **The ground probe sees dynamic things.** The probe behind plank placement and the preview asked only for terrain and static colliders (Rapier wants the filter to include the collider's own group), so planks, the fallen tree, rocks and the bogged vehicle were invisible to it, contrary to its comment: a plank previewed over a placed plank or a rock spawned inside it and popped out. The filter now includes dynamic colliders, and the probing car's own body is excluded through Rapier's `excludeRigidBody` argument (`probeGroundFor(id)`), so the preview never lands on the car's own roof.
- **Stalled-engine drag follows the gearing.** The tuning comment said `engineBrake` was "per unit of total gear ratio, halved", but the code returned a flat `engineBrake × 2` (240 N for the 40 Series) in any gear and range. The comment is the intended behaviour: a dead car should hold better in low range and a low gear, as a real one does on compression. The code now returns `engineBrake × total ratio / 2`, about 220 N in top gear in high range and about 1.4 kN in first gear in low range for the 40 Series (the old flat 240 N was top gear's). A test checks it scales with the range ratio and falls off up the gears.

## v1.20: track and traffic fixes

Six bugs in the world and the track, found by a review of the code and each confirmed before the change.

- **Oncoming traffic drives the track, not the chords between its waypoints.** The traffic plan was built from the authored control points of the ridge and the range top, but the track itself is the spline through them (`densify`): the straight lines between the points stray up to 4.5 m from the centreline, so for 233 of the ridge's 726 m and 122 of the range top's 368 m the centreline was further from the car's line than the 2.3 m at which it stops for you. The car drove the cutting banks and through trees, never stopped for a player in the middle of the track there, and treated a player who had pulled off towards the chord as blocking. `trafficPlans` (`src/sim/traffic.ts`) now builds the plan from the generated track's dense centreline (`world.features.tracks[].poly`, a point a metre), the same line the ground was graded along.
- **Traffic no longer bulldozes what lies on the track.** The car is a kinematic body, which Rapier moves through whatever it meets; driven through a settled rockfall rock it carried it 19 m, a landed trunk 13 m and the fixed fallen tree 11 m, and both traffic tracks host surprise sites. Its collider now collides with the player's vehicle only (`groups(DYNAMIC, VEHICLE)`): rocks, trunks, planks and drums are untouched, and the player's wheels and body still meet it. Known gap: a towed trailer (a `DYNAMIC` member, like the rocks) passes through it too; telling trailers from rocks would need a collision group of their own.
- **Restart from camp resets the traffic.** `Traffic.reset()` clears the cars out and the record of which track ends have sent one; the game removes their bodies and views. Before, a car already sent kept driving to its end, and the same stretch met no traffic again that session.
- **Restart from camp resets discoveries and section times.** `Exploration.reset(checkpoint.discovered)` puts the places found back to what the camp knew, so their notes and cards show again after a restart and the next checkpoint doesn't carry places from the abandoned stretch. `SectionTimes.reset()` drops the attempt under way and the run's list of times, so the finish can't list Big Red twice; the bests stay.
- **The steep shortcut is no longer timed as the concrete.** An attempt was only abandoned beyond `max(span × 1.5, span + 40)` ≈ 382 m from the far end, but the shortcut leaves the concrete at 152 m and rejoins at 341 m of 385, never more than 132 m from the far end, so a shortcut run was kept as a concrete time and counted towards the Sooty 80's 180 s target. `timedSections` now gives each section its track's dense centreline and width, and an attempt is dropped once the car is more than half the width plus 10 m off the line (about 12 m on the concrete: the shortcut gets 50 m away; a car pulled onto the bank, or round a rockfall, stays well inside it). The same rule drops a Big Red attempt taken round the bypass. The chosen design: a fork's short line is its own reward in time; the section time stays the track's.
- **The car's score counts the engine, suspension and tyres only.** `scoreRun` averaged the body panels into the car's 400 points, but nothing in the simulation ever damages a panel (dents are drawn, `condition.panels` is never written), so a wreck still scored 100 for the car. The share is now the average of the three parts that do take damage, so a wrecked car scores 0 and half the tyres gone costs a sixth. Panels can come back into the share once panel damage exists.
- **Tests.** `tests/traffic.test.ts`: the plan follows the spline off the chord, reset sends a car again, and a Rapier scene where the car drives through a rock without moving it and pushes the player's body. `tests/sectionTimes.test.ts`: an attempt that leaves the line is dropped and one that pulls 5 m aside is kept, sections built from a generated track, and reset. `tests/runScore.test.ts`: a wreck scores 0 for the car. `tests/save.test.ts`: exploration reset.

## v1.21: five fixes from a review

Each was found by a reviewer and checked in the code before it was changed.

- **The far haze was mixed in the wrong colour space** (`src/render/fog.ts`). The haze is blended in after three's output colour space, and three converts its own fog colour to match, but the far colour was uploaded unconverted-from-hex: `#A5BAC8` drew as a much darker steel blue, and the night's `#1E283A` near black. Beyond the colour shift the far land was a darker band that didn't melt into the sky, though dusk writes the haze and the sky's ground the same hex. It now goes in as the horizon's sky colour does (`setStyle(..., LinearSRGBColorSpace)`), so the hex is what's drawn. Dusk, night and midday were screenshotted from the range lookout before and after (`scripts/shoot.ts`, which now clicks through the title screen and takes `hour:` and `click:` steps). The hexes were not retuned: midday's far ridges now fade into the sky instead of standing as a blue-grey band, dusk's haze is the same hex as the sky's ground, and at night the far land draws at about (18, 25, 37) under a (30, 41, 62) sky instead of (8, 12, 18), so it is still darker than the sky, as night land is.
- **Rocks, logs and the ground cover paid three's per-instance bounds test and depth sort on every draw.** The v1.13 culling from spheres worked out once was only on the trees. The rock batch (on the CREB 983 rocks, 143 anchor marks and 589 logs, casting shadows) now uses it too, and both it and the cover batch (up to 10,700 instances on Medium, 23,000 on High) skip the sort, which does nothing for opaque materials. The cover keeps three's culling, as it is rebuilt round the camera.
- **Everything drawn from its physics body judders against the car.** The loop draws part way between the last two physics steps, and the car and trailer glide between their previous and current poses, but oncoming traffic, bogged vehicles, fallen trees, loose rocks, dropped drums and placed planks were drawn where their body stood after the last 60 Hz step: on a 120 Hz screen (the Pixel 10a) or at 40 fps they advanced in steps while the camera glided. Every such body now remembers its pose before each step (`src/sim/bodyPose.ts`, snapshotted at the top of `Simulation.step`, and for the traffic in `Game.step`) and is drawn between the two by one shared `followBody` (`src/render/followBody.ts`), which the car and trailer views use too.
- **Changing Graphics in the pause menu left the rain at the old preset's drop count.** The rain is built with a fixed number of drops (7,000, Low scales to 3,150); `setQuality` now builds it again when the particle share changes.
- **The engine sound fired and flared when the engine didn't catch.** The synthesiser's start sequence went crank → catch → run on its own clock, whatever the simulation said, so with an empty tank (or a wrecked engine in realistic mode, or a dried-out flooded engine with no fuel) you heard the starter, the engine fire, rev to about 1,500 and die, while the gauges showed it never ran. Each vehicle's starter time matches its simulated crank time, so at the end of the crank the synth now looks at the running flag: running, it catches and flares as before; not running, the starter turns over a quarter second longer and the sound dies away. A unit test covers the failed start.

## v1.22: vehicle-model corrections from the review

A reviewer measured the LC79, the Troop Carrier, the GQ Patrol, the Perentie and the Army No. 5 trailer against their `SOURCES.md` figures and photographs. Each item was checked again on the studio page before it was changed (before and after renders: the near-orthographic front, side, rear and top views from `reference/vehicles/README.md`, plus a close front of the LC79, close shots of the Troopy's wheel openings and each cockpit), and nothing was reshaped from memory.

- **The studio can show a trailer again.** `scripts/studio/studio.ts` builds a stand-in Trailer for the page; `TrailerView.update()` has read `tyres` since the punctures of v0.5x, so the page threw before `studio.ready` and `studio-shots.ts trailer:no5` timed out. The stand-in now has `tyres` and `drums`. `studio-shots.ts` also takes `STUDIO_CHROMIUM` for a Chromium outside Playwright's cache.
- **LC79 front indicators.** `front-34-single-cab.jpg` and `front-34-double-cab.jpg` show a tall amber unit in each upper outer corner of the black grille panel, outboard of the headlamp with its top level with the lamp bezel; the model had short wide ones in the lower corners, 145 mm below the lamps. They are now 85 × 140 mm amber on a black surround at x ±0.79, centred 40 mm above the lamp centres. The three lower cooling slots stay.
- **The 70 Series cab is 1.79 m wide** (`SIDE` 0.895, was 0.86 on the LC79 and 0.845 on the Troopy) against Toyota Australia's 1,790–1,870 mm. The LC79's flares keep their 0.97 m outer edge over the 1.6 m track and 37 inch tyres, so they now stand 75 mm off the cab instead of 110; the Troopy's lips stand 20 mm off instead of 35. The shared cab interior's floor, firewall and dash grew to meet the doors, and the cowls and the Troopy's bumpers with them.
- **Troopy wheel openings have a flat top.** `side.jpg`, `front-34-b.jpg`, `detail.jpg` and `rear-34-ambulance.jpg` all show a flat run of about 0.4 m along the top with short rounded corners and the sides opening out towards the sill, like the Patrol's; the model cut circles. `archPath` in `troopyBody.ts` now draws that shape (top 0.435 m above the origin, 0.66 m across the flat, 0.92 m at the sill, 0.12 m corners), and the side panel, the front clip, the lips and the wheelhouses follow it.
- **Troopy length.** The reviewer measured `side.jpg` (hubs 800 px for the 2.98 m wheelbase): nose skin 0.62 m ahead of the front axle, tail 1.18 m behind the rear, so 4.78 m of skin, which is what the model has. Its `SOURCES.md` no longer calls the model 200 mm short: the 4,995 mm figure includes the bumpers. The split is off (the model's nose is 90 mm too far forward and its tail 90 mm short); moving `F` and `TAIL` is left for a later pass, because the tow point, the collision boxes, the rack, the barn doors and the fixed windows are placed from their own figures and would all have to move together.
- **Patrol dials and column under the needles and wheel.** `patrolBody.ts` stretches every vertex across by `WIDEN` (1.035) after the interior is drawn, but `VehicleInstruments` builds the needles and the steering wheel from the unstretched cabin data, so the speedo face sat 17 mm, the tacho 12 mm and the wheel rim 15 mm off their needles and column. `addPatrolInterior` now draws the dial faces and the column narrowed by `1 / WIDEN`, so the stretch puts them back where the cabin data says.
- **Perentie body 1.79 m wide** (`SIDE` 0.895, was 0.845) against the 110's 1,791 mm, with nothing hiding it: the wing tops, the headlamp panels, the scuttle and windscreen frame, the canvas tilt (`HALF` 0.905), its rolled curtains, hoops and rack, and the interior's floor, bulkhead and dash rail grew with it.
- **Army No. 5 at 1.55 m overall.** The hubs moved from ±0.62 to ±0.66 (a 1.32 m track) and the rolled lip now stands 85 mm out past the box's sides, so the lip and the tyres' outer faces both come to about 1.55 m, the published figure. The inside box, the arch spacing and the ring-to-tail length were already right. It tips at about 0.68 g loaded now, from 0.64.
- **Photographs relabelled.** The Patrol's `side-right-1989.jpg` is a rear three-quarter from the right, not a profile, so the folder has no right side. The Perentie's `front-county.jpg` is a close-up of a door mirror, A pillar and wing badge, and `side.jpg` is a head-on front of a hardtop behind two military police motorcycles; neither is the angle its name says, so the Perentie's "check the wing intake and lamps against front-county.jpg" line is gone and the Gaps say there is no straight front of a County and no true side. `front.jpg`, `front-6x6-ambulance.jpg` and `side-county.jpg` are the ones to use.

The Unimog and the triangle budget were left for their own updates.

## v1.18: choosing the winch anchor

Steef wanted a way to pick which anchor the winch hooks. A chooser was already there (tool menu → Winch, A/D or the stick to step through the anchors, E to hook), but it was easy to miss and didn't say which anchor was picked.

- **USE / E opens the chooser when two or more anchors are in reach.** It starts on the one the quick hook would have taken, so a second press hooks that one; with a single anchor in reach, USE still hooks it at once. Planks to pick up and a trailer to hitch still come first. The press that opens the chooser is held back for one frame, so it can't also confirm. (Steef was asked whether USE should stay a quick hook instead; this is the recommended choice, built while he decides.)
- **Every anchor in reach gets a faint ring**, the picked one a bright bobbing ring, and a dashed line runs from the winch to it, so you can see where the cable would go.
- **The prompt names the pick**: "2/5 · Tree, 14 m". The driving prompt says "Choose a winch anchor (5 in reach)" when there is a choice.
- **Tap or click an anchor** in the view to pick it, and tap it again to hook it. A tap is a press that moves less than 12 px in under half a second, so dragging the camera still works; it takes the nearest anchor within 64 px of a finger (40 px of the mouse).

## v1.23: no reload mid-drive, and small input and menu fixes

A review of the game loop, the menus and the input found eight small things, fixed together.

- **A deploy no longer reloads the page while Steef is driving.** The installed app opened the cached build, checked for a new one at once and, some seconds later when the new service worker had finished downloading, reloaded the page (vite-plugin-pwa's `autoUpdate`), losing everything since the last camp. The worker is now registered with `registerType: 'prompt'` and without `skipWaiting`, so the new build waits. `src/main.ts` installs it at once while the title screen is up (nothing to lose there) and otherwise hands it to `pendingUpdate` (`src/core/update.ts`): the HUD says a new version is ready, and the pause menu gains **Restart for the new version**, which saves, fades and reloads into it. A build that is never asked for takes over when the app is next opened, as before.
- **Options / Cross to Drive no longer opens the pause menu on the first frame.** The briefing resolved on the press edge while the button was still held, and the input manager's first poll had no previous state, so the held Start read as a fresh press. The first `poll()` now records what is held without counting it (`InputManager.polled`), and `Game.start()` drops presses queued before the loop started (`clearEdges`), so Space or Enter on the briefing no longer toggles the handbrake or confirms anything.
- **The briefing said "Space to release handbrake", but the handbrake is off at the start**, so that press put it on. The hints now say "Space handbrake" (HAND handbrake, □ handbrake). The run still starts with the brake off and the engine off; the first press of the pedal starts it.
- **The tool menu holds the car.** Opening it zeroed the pedals but only set the handbrake in plank or anchor mode, so the car rolled on a slope while the menu was open. The handbrake is on whenever the menu or a tool mode is.
- **II and MAP work while placing planks or choosing an anchor.** The tool took every press, so the two buttons at the top left did nothing. Pause and Map now cancel the tool and open as usual; Esc / Circle still just cancel.
- **The title Settings page on a phone offers Low and Medium only**, as the pause menu has since v1.17, and a save on High shows Medium. `offeredQualities` and `shownQuality` in `src/data/quality.ts` are shared by the two menus; the `?bench` label prints the setting as drawn.
- **Continuing from a camp, the briefing's On board line shows what is on board there** (`briefingLoad` in `src/core/launch.ts`), not the standard load.
- **History notes count down with the game, not the clock.** The 7 s short card and the reading time ran on `setTimeout`, so a note vanished while the game was paused or the map was open. `HistoryCard.tick(dt)` runs from the render loop only while the game is not paused.
- `AudioSystem.start()` added a `statechange` listener on every gesture before the context ran; it now adds one, which removes itself.

## v1.24: fewer triangles in every vehicle, round one

No vehicle changes shape. A reviewer counted each look (body, four wheels and the two carried planks, built the way the studio page builds them) and found every one well over the 62,000 the detailed 40 Series note set as the budget for a complete vehicle: from 83,000 (Hilux) to 154,000 (Camel Trophy and 80 Series; the counts below are after v1.22's body corrections). `scripts/tri-count.ts` now prints that table (`npx vite-node scripts/tri-count.ts`); it sums each mesh's index count over three. The studio renders of every vehicle (the seven views and the cockpit, `scripts/studio-shots.ts`) were taken before and after and laid side by side; the mean pixel difference is under 0.6 of 255 on every body view, and the tread and the bead rings account for the wheel close-ups.

Where the triangles went, and what changed:

- **Every rounded block was 108 triangles.** `VehicleBuilder.block` with a radius used `RoundedBoxGeometry` at one segment, which is a 3 × 3 grid on each face. Light-bar LEDs, bolt heads, brackets and rack slats are drawn with it in their hundreds. It now draws a chamfered box (`chamferBox`: 6 faces, 12 edge strips and 8 corner triangles, 44 in all) whose vertices keep their face normals, so the shading still grades round the edge like a fillet. A block under 20 mm in any direction, or with a radius under 4 mm, is a plain 12-triangle box: nothing that small shows its rounding.
- **Every swept tube was 1,728 triangles.** `continuousTube` (bull bars, rack rails, roll cages, the Unimog's canvas hoops) sampled its path uniformly 72 times at 12 sides, however short the tube. It now puts one ring of quads on each straight and five on each bend, with six sides under 12 mm radius, eight under 25 mm and ten above (`sweptTube`, framed by parallel transport so the quads don't twist). A four-corner bull bar member is now about 400 triangles.
- **The 80 Series recovery boards** (14,300 a board) were two bevelled extrusions with bevelled hand holes and 51 teeth each as rounded blocks. Each is now an unbevelled extrusion with three steps per corner; the top board keeps its 51 teeth as plain blocks and the lower board, whose teeth sit under the top one, has three ridges. 1,450 a board.
- **The Camel Trophy sand ladders** (16,500 a ladder) were extrusions whose bevel ran round all 34 rounded holes. The holes are now square and punched straight through; 800 a ladder.
- **Tread blocks:** 16 lugs a row on every tyre (14 for the Unimog's chevrons) instead of 24 to 30, each lug a little longer along the tyre so the tread stays as dense. The 80 Series' 24 bead-ring bolts a side (1,150 a wheel, never visible) are gone; the ring stays.
