## v1.25: the roll torque keeps its tuned axle

The full test run after v1.19 showed two trailer tests failing that passed before: towing the box trailer, the Perentie and the LC79 tipped it in a full-lock turn at 0.4 throttle. Bisecting the five v1.19 fixes put it on the corrected axle direction in `addRollTorque`, not on the plank push or the footprint rays. Measured headless in that turn: with the true axle the LC79's body roll went 8.6° → 24° → 89° (over on its side) at 36 km/h as the trailer jackknifed, and the 40 Series the same at 20 km/h; with the old direction both stayed at 6°–12° of roll and the trailers followed.

- **`addRollTorque` goes back to the mirrored direction for steered wheels.** The roll tuning (`rollTorqueScale` and the §6.2 roll checks) was calibrated with it, which gives a steered front about half its physical roll torque at full lock. Correcting it without retuning rolls the car in a hard turn while towing. The true axle stays in `pushDynamicGround` (planks, the fallen tree) and the footprint rays, where nothing was tuned against the old sign. The `steeredAxle` helper and its test stay as the record of Rapier's convention; redoing the roll tuning with it is on the roadmap.
- **One knife-edge test now fails: "pinches tyres on the paddock road taken fast".** On v1.18 it passed on a single cut in six fast runs (the 40 Series on the 530–675 m stretch); with the footprint rays sampling along the wheel's true rolling direction that one strike doesn't happen, and neither vehicle cuts a tyre at 47 km/h over the clear stretches. The fix is kept: the question it leaves is whether the potholes should pinch more often, which is a tuning choice for Steef's play test (README, v1.07–v1.08 item), not something to settle by moving a threshold until the test passes.
- **Already red on `main` before v1.19**: the v1.18 deploy's test job failed 14 tests (challenges, creb, crebForks, crebStretches, current, surprises). The deploy itself is a separate job and went through, as it did for v1.19.

## v1.26: the No. 5 keeps its narrow track

v1.22 took the Army No. 5 to its published 1,550 mm by moving the hubs 40 mm out each side (a 1.32 m track) as well as standing the lip out. The unit tests, run after that update went out as CLAUDE.md asks, caught what the wider track did: loaded, in a hard turn at 25 km/h across a 15° slope, the trailer now stayed up, where the No. 5 is meant to go over at a lower speed than the box trailer (its narrow track and high floor are the point of it, v0.49). The hubs go back to ±0.62 (a 1.24 m track, 1.43 m over the tyres) and the published width stays in the rolled lip (±0.775), which is where the figure leaves room for it: the inside box is 1,320 mm and the overall 1,550 mm, so 115 mm a side is pressed side and lip. `tests/trailer.test.ts` passes again (32 of 32); the tipping figure in `src/data/trailers.ts` is back to 0.64 g. `reference/vehicles/army-no5-trailer/SOURCES.md` records both figures.

## v1.27: the Unimog laid out from its photographs

The model's layout was checked against `reference/vehicles/unimog-ul1700l/side.jpg`, `front-34.jpg`, `rear-34.jpg` and `three-quarter-museum.jpg`, before and after, on the studio page, and the side render laid over `side.jpg` with the studio camera set up to match the photograph (on the truck's right, level, 1.3 m above the hubs, 9.75 m out from the near wheel faces, a 45° vertical field of view on a 4:3 frame).

**What `side.jpg` measures** (369 px/m, the hubs 1,420 px apart for the 3,850 mm wheelbase; stations in the vehicle frame, origin mid-wheelbase, the front axle at −1.925): the windscreen pillars stand above the front axle, the cowl at about −2.0 and the roof's front edge at −1.85, so the pillars lean back about 0.15 m over the screen's height; the cab's rear wall at −0.68; the cab's lower front cheeks run forward beside the bonnet to the grille plane at about −2.63, with the front wheel arch cut into them; the bumper face at −2.93 with the towing jaws just ahead; the tray's headboard right behind the cab at about −0.62 and its tail at 3.45. Heights above the ground: the cab's lower edge 1.2 m, waist 1.9 m, roof 2.44 m, the bonnet 1.6 m at the grille rising to 1.8 m at the cowl, the dropsides 1.33–1.86 m, the tilt top 2.9 m (published 2,875 mm). The brief put a "cab front wall" 0.33 m ahead of the front axle; no edge at that station could be found in the photograph, so the pillar sits over the axle as measured, and the cheeks run on to the grille.

**What the model had** (`unimogBody.ts` before this change): the cab from −1.65 to −0.1, a 1.29 m bonnet, the tray from 0.43 with the spare standing in a 0.5 m gap behind the cab, the cab roof 2.72 m above the ground, and the intake stack behind the cab's right rear corner. So the cab was about 0.35 m too far back at the pillar and 0.55 m at the rear wall, the tray a metre too far back, and the cab 0.28 m too tall.

**What moved.** The stations now live in `src/render/unimogLayout.ts`, and the body, the tilt (`unimogCanvas.ts`), the interior, the cabin layout in `src/data/vehicles.ts`, the headlamp positions and the physics boxes in `src/data/unimog.ts` all follow them:

- **Cab** −2.0 (cowl) to −0.65, 2.24 m wide, floor 0.54, waist 1.19, roof 1.76 (2.50 m above the ground with the roof panel). The screen pillars lean back 0.15 m; the door's front shut line continues the pillar's rear edge, the door glass leans with it. The rear wall, its window, the roof hatch, mirrors, grab handles and the aerial move with the cab. The step is behind the front wheel, as in the photographs.
- **Cheeks and bonnet.** One sloping top surface the full width of the cab from the cowl (1.11) to the nose (0.92), its shoulders and nose edge rounded over 0.1 m; the cheeks' side skins are one extrusion each with the front wheel arch cut out (a flattened arch 1.13 m wide at its feet, 0.66 high), the door's lower panel behind, and the wheel well's roof and inner wall inside; the cheeks' front faces either side of the grille with the nose corners wrapped round; the indicators on the cheeks' front faces, louvres above the arches. The grille sits flush between the cheeks at −2.66, the bumper face at −2.94 with its centre line at 0.30 (the photograph's bumper is 0.17–0.41 m above the ground), the headlamps in it; the winch fairlead on the bumper's face (`winchMount` z −3.0, from −3.22).
- **Intake stack** on the right screen pillar: up through the bonnet's right shoulder at the cowl, out past the pillar and straight up it, the head and cap 0.17 m above the roof (`front-34.jpg` and `side.jpg`). The tuning's flood point (`intake`, 2.3 m above the ground) moves to the stack at the same height; the drawn head stands about 0.35 m above it.
- **Tray** −0.58 to 3.45, floor 0.70, dropsides to 1.18 (the photograph's are 0.53 m tall); the tilt and its bows follow the tray's ends and the dropside top.
- **Spare** lying flat on a cradle under the tray's front on the right, behind the cab, where `rear-34.jpg` shows it (centre x 0.6, y 0, z −0.3). The second spare on the left dropside stays. Under the tray, the fuel tank and a stowage box on the left and the battery box on the right follow `side.jpg` and `rear-34.jpg`, the exhaust now out to the left.
- **Interior and cockpit**: the dash under the screen at the cowl, the seats against the rear wall, the eye at (0.5, 1.52, −1.15) (was 1.74, −0.6), the dials, lamps, wheel and handbrake with it.
- **Physics boxes**: the cab, the cheeks and bonnet, the wheel wells' roofs, the tray and tilt, the bumper and the under-tray spare at their new places. The spare's box is thinner than the drawn tyre (its bottom 0.58 m above the ground) so the Unimog keeps the clearance margin its test asks for.

**Against the photograph after the change**, the cab's rear wall, the cowl and the bumper face are within 30–50 mm of `side.jpg`, the grille and the headboard within about 50 mm, the roof within 60 mm. What still disagrees: the axles are drawn like the other vehicles' axles, not as portal axles (v0.11); the headlamps are round behind wire guards where `front-34.jpg` shows square housings with mesh guards; the grille carries the star where the Army truck's is plain mesh; the roof's front corners and the screen are squarer than the real cab's; the cheeks carry one row of louvres where `rear-34.jpg` shows two groups; the overall length over the towing jaws is 6.4 m, as the photograph measures, against the published 5,500–6,100 mm (which must leave the jaws out).

**SOURCES.md** said the model gave the cab a canvas roof; the code has drawn a painted steel roof with a round hatch since v0.31, so the text is corrected and its "in the current model" column updated.

**Cost.** The body is 81,400 triangles after v1.24's cuts (79,200 on `main` before this change, `scripts/tri-count.ts`); the next rounds of cutting are on the roadmap.

**Tests.** `tests/unimog.test.ts` passes (the length comment now says 6.6 m over the jaws and the pintle hook); the headlight, camera, current, trailer and CREB Unimog tests pass except one: "climbs Bob's Hill in the wet in the Unimog in low range with open diffs" stops 112.5 m from the end on `main` before this change as well (checked on a clean checkout), so it is left for a follow-up and not a result of the new layout. The same goes for `tests/current.test.ts`'s "stops the short-wheelbase 4WD on the deep line", which does not involve the Unimog and fails on `main` as well.

## v1.28: notes count down in game time; the gamepad tests seed the first poll

Follow-up to v1.23, from its test results.

- **History notes count down in simulated time.** v1.23 ran the countdown from the render loop's frame time, which on the browser tests' software renderer (seconds a frame, capped at 0.25 s) ran far slower than the game, so a short card was still up when a test expected it gone. `HistoryCard.tick(dt)` now runs from the simulation step, so a note waits while the game is paused and counts the same seconds the game does, in play and in tests.
- **The gamepad unit tests poll once before pressing.** Since v1.23 the first `poll()` only records what is held, so `tests/driveControls.test.ts` and `tests/camera.test.ts`, which pressed buttons on the very first poll, now poll an idle pad first. The physics failures in the same CI run (vehicle, trailer, CREB stretch and current tests) come from the vehicle-model work of v1.19 to v1.27 and are being fixed in those updates.

## v1.29: fewer triangles in every vehicle, round two

Again no vehicle changes shape. The renders of every vehicle at the studio's eight views were compared against v1.26 (and the Unimog against v1.27, which laid it out afresh) as in v1.24; the mean pixel difference is at most 0.12 of 255 on every view. Every vehicle is now under the 80,000 the v1.24 note aimed at, and most are near the 62,000 budget.

- **Rings and rods sized by the part.** `VehicleBuilder.ring` was a 6 × 32 torus (384 triangles) whether it was a wheel rim or a 4 mm bead line; a 32-step ring now needs a radius over 180 mm, a small one has 12 steps, and a tube under 8 mm is four-sided. `rod` has six sides up to 8 mm radius, eight up to 20 mm and ten above (it was ten always). The winch drums alone (14 to 19 rings each) were 5,000 to 7,000 triangles a vehicle.
- **Chains of rods are one tube.** `VehicleBuilder.chain` sweeps one tube through a list of points (`sweptTube`, the same sweep `continuousTube` uses). The LC79's three roof ribs were 20 capped rods each and the Unimog's tilt bows 32; a chain has no caps and no overlap at the joints.
- **Tube bends get segments for their turn.** `continuousTube` gave every bend five segments; a bend now gets one per 18° of turn, so a bull bar's right angle keeps five and the 21-point hoop of a Unimog bow, bending about 8° at each point, gets one at each.
- **LC79:** the roof was subdivided three times over (64 triangles for one) to carry its pressed ribs; twice (16) carries them as well. The honeycomb grille mesh has 30 mm cells in 6 rows of 28, where 20 mm cells in 9 rows of 42 cost 4,500 triangles for ribs no camera in the game resolves.
- **80 Series:** the side skins have a row every 54 mm instead of 27 (46 rows, not 91), which halves the skins and their inner faces. Dents still land on them; a dent now moves vertices 54 mm apart.
- **Unimog:** the canvas tilt's grids are half as fine each way (16 × 30 for the roof, 24 × 6 for each side). Its row below is against v1.27's new layout, which was 104,000 before these cuts.
- **Lamp lenses:** flattened domes of 20 × 8 segments where some were 28 × 12, 32 × 12 or 40 × 20. The Camel Trophy's arch panels follow their arcs with 21 points, not 41.
- **Wheels:** the dished centres' vents, hubs and caps have half the points round (a 48-point disc is 32, a 14-point vent 8, a 32-sided hub 16).
- **Kept as they were:** the HJ60's stripe pieces and its script lettering (together 2,800 triangles now that each stroke is a plain block, and the README's v1.12 check wants TOYOTA LAND CRUISER readable), the block TOYOTA and NISSAN lettering on grilles and tailgates (1,200 to 2,400 a vehicle), and the bevelled panel extrusions, whose rounded edges are the silhouette. The third round planned in v1.24 (fewer, larger lettering blocks) is not needed for the budget and is left out.

## v1.30: vehicle-model defects in the 40 Series, the Hilux and the 60 Series

A reviewer listed defects in the three leaf-sprung bodies, each confirmed in the code and in studio renders. Each was re-checked against the photographs in `reference/vehicles/` before and after the change (studio renders from `scripts/studio-shots.ts` with v1.22's `STUDIO_CHROMIUM` pointing at the container's Chromium, plus near-orthographic front, top and rear views and a low rear view under the tail).

**Shared.** The leaf-spring hanger rods (`src/render/vehicleSuspension.ts`) were at `side * 0.48 - 0.045`, so the left pair stood outboard of its spring and the right pair inboard. Both pairs now sit 45 mm inboard of their spring.

**40 Series** (`src/data/vehicle.ts`, `src/render/vehicleBody.ts`, `vehicleEquipment.ts`).

- **Wheels drawn on the 1.40 m track.** The tyres stood 33 mm outside the guard lips (track 1.45 m with 0.26 m tyres against guards 1.665 m over the lips). The wheels are now drawn on the published 1,400 mm track as 7.50-16 tyres 0.21 m wide (`drawnWheels` in `src/data/vehicle.ts`, read by the wheel builder, the model's wheel pivots, the axle length and the bonnet spare's clamp): the outer faces sit at 0.805 m, inside the lips, as in `front.jpg`, `front-white.jpg` and `rear-34-hardtop.jpg` (which is in fact a front view). Compared in the near-orthographic front and top renders before and after. **The physics wheels stay on 1.45 m with 0.26 m tyres.** Moving them was tried first: with the track at 1.40 m, 21 tests failed in 11 files, because the paddock road's potholes no longer pinched a tyre on the wheel line they were dug for, the Daintree's deep line no longer stopped the car, the Troop Carrier no longer tipped on a gentler slope than the 40, the loaded trailer no longer tipped before the car in its test turn, and the 40 leaned past the Granite Spur limit on The Chute; with the tyres 0.21 m wide the Little Annan at 5:45 pm no longer swept the crawling car away (the tyres' buoyancy is their drawn volume). Re-balancing the world to a new wheel line is a bigger job than this update, so the drawn wheels sit 25 mm inboard of the physics wheels, which nothing in play shows. Steef can ask for the physics to follow if he wants the real track's handling.
- **Spare on the left rear door.** `rear-34-left.jpg` (1982 hardtop) and `rear-34-orange-1977.jpg` show the carrier on the left door; `rear.jpg` is a 1970 tailgate car with a swing-out carrier and does not settle it. No owner's choice was recorded, so the spare carrier moved to the left door, the jerry can and the stamped lower panel to the right door, the door handle to the right door beside the split, and the number plate to the left of the rear bumper under the spare, where the two photographs have it.
- **Length row.** `SOURCES.md` compared the 3.58 m skin with the 3,840.5 mm over-bumper figure; the row now says the stock bumpers account for the difference. The rear overhang could not be checked: both `side.jpg` and `side-1977.jpg` are front three-quarter views, now said so in the file. `TAIL` is unchanged.

**Hilux** (`src/data/hilux.ts`, `src/render/hiluxBody.ts`).

- **Tyres 0.80 m.** `R` 0.375 to 0.40, the size of a 7.00-16 (the SOURCES row said so). The ground is now 0.52 m below the origin; the intake height was already derived from `R`. Compared in the side and wheel renders with `side-1980-4wd.jpg` and `front-34-right.jpg`.
- **Bar lamps and plate.** They floated 12–20 mm ahead of the bull bar; `face` is now −2.328, the beam's bevelled front face.
- **Under-tub spare.** It passed through the frame rails and two crossmembers at frame height. It now hangs below the rails on its two cradle bars (centre y −0.205, bottom 0.22 m off the ground), as a slung spare does; the collision box follows.
- **Tub length: checked, not moved.** The length row compared the skin with the over-bumper figure; the model is 4.70 m to its rear step bumper against 4,690–4,730 mm published, so the row now says that. Scaling `side-1980-4wd.jpg` on its wheels (the tyre diameters give the perspective), the cab back reads 1.75–1.9 m behind the front axle and the tailgate 3.7–4.0 m, against 1.76 and 3.83 in the model, so the 2.0 m tub is inside what the photograph supports and `TUB_FRONT`, `CAB_BACK` and `FRONT_FACE` stay. `rear-34-right.jpg` is too close and too far round to scale. A true profile of a 4WD long bed would settle it.
- **Arch lips.** The rolled bead round each wheel opening (torus tube 0.03) is now a 12 mm lip, a few millimetres proud of the skin, as the pressed edge in `front-34-right.jpg` and `rear-34-right.jpg`.

**60 Series** (`src/render/hj60Gear.ts`, `src/data/hj60.ts`).

- **Under-body spare.** It sat at frame height through the rails and the two rear crossmembers. It now hangs below the rails on its cradle (centre y −0.22, bottom 0.2 m off the ground), the carrier bolt through its centre; the collision box follows. Checked in the low rear render against `rear-34-left-n-hj60v-std-1987-barn-doors.jpg`, where the spare shows under the bumper.
- **Front collision box.** The winch's roller fairlead stood 56 mm ahead of the box. The bull-bar box now runs to z −2.46, just past the fairlead ring.

**Hilux centre of mass.** The bigger tyres lift the truck 25 mm, and with it the centre of mass, which put it on its side in its 50 km/h full-lock test turn. `com.y` came down 25 mm (0.28 to 0.255) to keep it about 0.78 m off the ground, as tuned; the Hilux test now expects 0.80 m tyres.

**Tests.** With the 40's physics unchanged, the driving tests are as on main. Two fail there already and are not touched here: the Surprises test (the 40 Series through the Roaring Meg rockfall) and the Bloomfield Track creek test (the Land Rover stopped in the current).

**What still disagrees.** The 40 Series' rear overhang and door position (no true side photograph); the Hilux tub length within about 0.1 m (no square-on side of a 4WD long bed); the 40 Series' 1,400 mm track is taken from the defect list and is not yet backed by a catalogue scan in `SOURCES.md`; and the 40's physics wheels stay 25 mm outboard of the drawn ones.

## v1.31: the unit tests are green again

The "Lint and unit tests" job on `main` had been red on every push since v0.70 (the deploy waits only for the build, so nothing noticed). On v1.18, 14 of 688 tests failed, the same 14 locally and in CI. They had two roots.

**Fords were cut a creek's depth too low at the water's edge (v0.88).** The ford ramp measured its height from the creek's centre-line bed (water − depth) instead of the bed at the water's edge, where the carved channel meets the bank at the water level. So just outside the water every ford was cut to about a creek's depth below the channel's edge: a trench under the water's edge that a wheel dropped into on the way in and out (0.90 m at the Daintree, where the authored line is at most 0.65 m deep; 0.6 m at Paddock Road's north creek, which v0.88 measured as "a 0.3 m / 36° bank"). The ramp now starts at the water level and rises at the creek's bank slope, cutting a road above it down to that bank and filling one below it up to the creek's own bank fill (at most 0.35 m above the water). The fill fades out over the outer three quarters of the 8 m ramp, because the Bloomfield Track reaches the Little Annan 2 m below its water level 10 m out and a full fill met it with a 1.7 m wall. Fixed by this: the Daintree line's deepest water (0.65 m, test limit 0.85), the Little Annan bar against the ford (0.28 m against 0.55 m), the short-wheelbase 4WD's wheel water on the bar (under 1 m), the rockfall site on the Roaring Meg climb (the approach starts at the creek), and the paddock and Little Annan crossings in the fork and stretch tests. The "no hidden wall at the water" test still passes.

**The wet clay climbs have needed the winch since v0.70.** `creeps Big Red on the crown`, the seven `drives big-red in the wet` tests and the Unimog's open-diff Bob's Hill fail on v0.73 as well, so the bisect that blamed v0.74 was wrong: the churn from v0.70, kept at full strength in v0.71 because the owner wants the game difficult, is what stops them. Crawling flat out with crawl control on (which floors the pedal whenever the car is under its target speed), the pinches cut up under the wheels and the car stalls; without churn the same crawl reaches the top of Big Red in 126 s. Easing the pedal does not help: below walking pace on clay the wheels count as spinning at any pedal above a third, and at that pedal the car does not climb. This is the game as designed, and the owner answered it in v0.87 with four marked rocks ahead of each of Big Red's pinches, "within the 30 m cable of a stall, so USE hooks one and IN pulls the car up the climb". So the test driver now does what a player does: stuck, it winches to the rock or tree ahead (`WinchRecovery`, as the short-wheelbase drives already did), and those tests accept the winch. Big Red in the wet (on v1.30's physics): short-wheelbase 4WD 146 s with 2 pulls, Perentie 145 s / 2, Hilux 133 s / 1, Unimog 124 s / 0, Troop Carrier 140 s / 2, Patrol 139 s / 1, LC79 161 s / 4 (limit 300 s, at most 4 pulls). The Unimog with open diffs on Bob's Hill: 117 s with 1 pull (at most 2). Bob's Hill with both lockers still goes without the winch in every vehicle (104–106 s), and the gutter line, high range and open diffs still stall.

The driver (`tests/helpers/drive.ts`) learned four things on the way, each something a player does; without them the climbs came down to luck, with one vehicle or another ending up on the mud verge beside the rock it was pulled to, hooking the same rock over and over, or sliding back down the pinch:

- Sliding backwards down a greasy climb, it brakes (both pedals and the handbrake: the brake pedal alone below walking pace means reverse) until it stops, instead of spinning the wheels all the way down and churning the clay as it goes. Rolling back counts as stuck.
- On the cable it steers hard for the line (look-ahead 3 m), because a rock 4 m beside the crown otherwise drags the car onto the mud verge.
- It hooks nothing it is already beside, measured from the winch mount (that pull only drags it sideways), and tries a rock at most twice.
- Stalled again straight after a pull, it hooks the next anchor after 2 s instead of 6.
- Stuck with nothing to hook (beside the rock it was pulled to, the next one just out of cable reach), it backs off for 2.5 s rather than digging in, which also brings that rock back into reach for its second pull. Without this the LC79 sat spinning beside the rock at 123 m for half a minute and churned the crown to nothing.

Driving at the winch's pace (pedal 0.6) and holding the cable until the anchor was abeam were tried and dropped: at full pedal the car outruns the cable on easy ground and lets go, and released only abeam it was towed 9 m off the Big Red bypass onto a side slope and rolled.

**Other tests moved.** The Rock Steps in high range: the car bounces up the steps on spinning wheels and after 45 s is 3.6 m short (6.9 m on v0.73; v0.77's slower dirt churn gave it a little more grip on the approach). The test now asks that it still be short of the top (by more than 2 m) after three times as long as low range took, rather than by 5 m. The Bloomfield Track's fast creek: stopped in it, the short-wheelbase 4WD is still carried into the hole below, but the Perentie, a tonne heavier, lifts too little to be swept; the current walks it 0.7 m sideways while it stands there and it drives out once it moves again. Before v0.88 it only seemed to be swept: a weir on the exit bank held it in the water while the current pushed it along. The test now expects that.

**A fifteenth, from main.** v1.25 left "pinches tyres on the paddock road taken fast" red on purpose: since the footprint rays sample along the wheel's true rolling direction (v1.19) neither vehicle cuts a tyre at 47 km/h on those clear stretches, and whether the potholes should pinch more often is a tuning choice for the play test, not a threshold to move. The test now keeps to what the game does (a steady 30 km/h cuts nothing, fast runs get through, at most a couple of cuts between them) and the tuning question stays open on the README's v1.07–v1.08 item.

**Probes.** `scripts/ford-probe.ts` prints ground, water and depth along a track; `scripts/climb-lab.ts` drives a track with the test driver and logs where it stalls; `scripts/fork-lab.ts` and `scripts/stretch-lab.ts` do the same for the fork and stretch drives.

## v1.32: controller fixes for menus

Pointing at a tool chose it, but nothing on screen showed which one. The in-game display's glass panel style (`#ui[data-hud] .paper`) outranked `.tool-option.selected`, so the orange highlight never appeared on the glass display styles. The selected rule now also matches under `#ui[data-hud]`.

- **Frame rate on Batocera:** the wrapper no longer sets `disable-frame-rate-limit`. The owner reported the game felt better after the change, but this was not a controlled comparison.
- **Menus and the left stick:** every menu now takes the left stick as well as the D-pad, one step per push (`MenuStick`, the same rule as the touch joystick). During driving these presses only reach the winch anchor choice. The stick is stepped on the first poll but not counted, under v1.23's rule: a stick already held when the pad is first seen latches without sending a press, so it cannot confirm the briefing by itself.
- **Up and down order on New Journey:** rows followed the order the groups were listed in code, not the screen, so Down jumped from Back past Start and Drive. Rows now sort by screen position, and Up/Down steps through an option group that wraps over several lines. Entering such a group from outside still lands on the chosen option.

## v1.33: how wet the track is, rolled per run

Roadmap item 4, the last of the original eight. The CREB closes in the wet and reopens about April, so a June run can meet the same track dried out hard or still letting go of the wet. Each run now rolls one of three states and the briefing says which.

**Everything scales off `reopened`, which is the game as it was.** v1.32's authored creek levels, bog depths and `crebWeather.ts` storm are that condition's numbers, so `stormFor` and `mapForCondition` hand back the very same objects for it and a just-reopened run plays exactly as v1.32 did. A test holds that (`toEqual` on the storm, `toBe` on the map). The other two are multipliers off it, which is why there is no fourth "as authored" state.

**The roll comes from the run's seed** (`rollTrackCondition`), through a seed of its own derived from it, so adding or moving a surprise does not change the weather. The checkpoint already keeps the seed, so a restart from a camp finds the same day with nothing new saved, and `?seed=N` repeats a day. Weighted to the track as June usually is: just reopened 0.4, dry 0.3, wet 0.3.

**The storm always comes; its strength scales.** The alternative was letting a dry roll skip it, which was turned down: the afternoon's rising creeks are what the episode is built around, and the Little Annan at the end is tuned for them. So `stormFor` keeps the timeline's shape (the same hours, the same HUD notes) and scales the rain amounts, capped at steady heavy rain, along with `risePerHour` and `maxRise`.

**What each condition moves.**

- **Clay grip** through `ground.before`, the wetness the day starts at (0.25 dry, 0.7 just reopened, 0.9 wet). Grip already follows wetness per surface through `wetGripLoss`, so nothing in the vehicle or the surfaces changed.
- **Creek depth and current** through a new `creeks.standing` on `WeatherTimeline`: metres every creek stands above its authored level before a drop falls. `weatherAt` adds it to the rise, so the current follows through `flowPerMetre` as it does in the storm, and `prepareFlood` counts it when deciding how far up the banks to prepare. Only the wet roll uses it (0.12 m). **Dry does not lower the creeks:** the authored levels are the just-reopened ones, and a negative standing rise would drop the water below beds and fords that the depth tests and the v1.31 ramp work are pinned to. A dry day is drier through grip, bogs and a weaker storm instead.
- **Bog depth** through `mapForCondition`, which returns the map with each track's `rough.bogDepth` and `rough.sumpDepth` scaled (0.55 dry, 1.35 wet). Scaling the map before the world is built keeps the condition out of terrain generation: the hollows are found in the same places by the same seeded search, only dug deeper or shallower, and the tracks, creeks and everything built from them are the same objects.

**Where it is read.** `Game.create` rolls it from the checkpoint's seed, builds the world from the scaled map and hands the Game an episode whose `storm` is the scaled one, so every later reader (the runtime weather, the HUD notes, the dev panel) follows the roll without knowing about conditions. Replays, which pick their own time and weather, skip all of it.

**The briefing is told after the fact.** It is written before the world is built, but a new run's seed is only settled inside `Game.create` (`startCheckpoint`), so the condition cannot be known in time. `main.ts` calls `loading.trackCondition` once the Game exists, which prepends the label to the conditions line and the sentence to the story, both still on screen behind the Drive button. The welcome screen's one-line story stays the episode's general description, because at that point no run exists yet.

**Also in this version.** The e2e suite could not launch a browser in a cloud container: the project pins Playwright 1.56.1 for the Chromium build 1194 that used to be preinstalled, and the image now ships 1243, so every test failed before running any game code. CI does not run e2e, so nothing noticed. `playwright.config.ts` now takes `executablePath` from `CHROMIUM_PATH`, as `scripts/studio-shots.ts` already does, and falls back to Playwright's own browser.

## v1.34: harder crossings

The owner found the crossings too easy: you could drive straight across them, and taking them fast cost nothing.

**The Daintree is deeper and faster off the bar.** The straight line across the middle went from 1.05 m to 1.2 m deep, and the river from 2.6 to 2.9 m/s. The bar line is unchanged (still about 0.6–0.8 m), so weaving over it in low range still gets across, and going straight lifts the car sooner and carries it off.

**Speed through water now floods the engine.** Before, the bow wave could at most double the water's depth at the intake, and water thrown in by it filled the flood timer at the same rate as still water. A crossing takes one to three seconds, so even a fast run never reached the 10 s stall. Now:

- The bow wave can reach 1.5 times the water's depth (`bowMaxOfDepth`), so it reaches a 0.9 m intake from a 0.45 m creek.
- While only the bow wave reaches the intake, the flood timer runs `1 + 0.7 × (m/s over 2.5)²` times as fast (`bowFloodPerSpeed2`). Water still over the intake without a bow wave counts at the normal rate, so stopping or crawling in deep water floods as before.

Measured headless with the short-wheelbase 4WD on the track lines: through the small creeks (Bloomfield middle, Donovan's) at about 19 km/h the timer reaches about 3 s (nothing happens), at about 25 km/h about 7 s (the engine sputters), and at about 31 km/h it passes 10 s and stalls. The Little Annan at 3 pm at 16 km/h reaches about 8 s and gets across; at 22 km/h it stalls.

**The Little Annan ford is now closed late.** At 5:45 pm a steady 16 km/h through the main ford stalls the engine, where before it was the way across. Crawling is still swept away, so late in the storm the shallow crossing downstream is the way over (its test still passes for both vehicles). `tests/storm.test.ts` now expects that, and checks the steady drive still crosses at 3 pm.

## v1.35: a manual gearbox (controller only)

The owner asked how to make the game better for players who like 4WD challenges and driving sims. The biggest gap was the drivetrain: every vehicle drove as an automatic, shifting itself through a torque converter, though only the 40 Series really is one. This update adds a manual gearbox as a setting; the transfer case is next (`docs/ROADMAP.md`, "Sim-audience follow-ups"). The plan called it v1.33; v1.33 and v1.34 shipped first, so it is v1.35.

**One setting, `gearbox`:** Automatic (the default, the game as it was), Manual (you pick the gear, the clutch is worked for you) and Manual with clutch (a clutch too, and the engine can stall). Saved in `Settings`; an old or invalid save reads as Automatic.

**Controller only (the owner's decision).** The row is shown on the title and pause Settings pages only while a gamepad is connected and the device is not a phone or tablet (`showGearboxSetting`). The keyboard and touch always drive automatic and no keyboard shift keys were added. The game's effective mode is the setting while a pad is connected (`InputManager.gamepadConnected`, from `poll()`), otherwise Automatic (`Game.syncGearbox`, every frame), so a keyboard or phone session is never stuck without gears. Shifting is sequential only: **D-pad right** up, **D-pad left** down (R ← N ← 1 ← 2 …). There is no direct gear selection. In Automatic the D-pad still zooms the camera; in the manual modes it shifts and only scroll and pinch zoom. ○/B held is the clutch; its jack role applies only when the car is on its side, and its menu role is unchanged.

**Automatic is untouched.** `updateDrivetrain` branches at the top on `req.mode`; with no mode (every existing caller and test) or `'automatic'` it runs the code exactly as before, and the new `DriveRequest` fields are optional. No vehicle was retuned: the converter numbers stay for Automatic, and each data file's comment now calls it "automatic mode's coupling".

**Per vehicle `gearbox.kind`.** The 40 Series is `'automatic'`: in the manual modes it keeps its converter and the player holds its gear (a 1-2-D lever), with no clutch and no stall. Every other vehicle is `'manual'` and drives through a dry clutch whose capacity is `gearbox.clutchTorque`, by default 1.8 × the peak of the torque curve.

**Clutch maths** (pure, in `drivetrain.ts`, inside the existing four substeps). With engagement `e`, `tLock = combustion − friction` and `capacity = clutchTorque × e`: within 60 rpm of the gearbox side and with `|tLock| ≤ capacity` the clutch locks (`rpm = turbine`, transmitted `tLock`); otherwise it slips and passes `capacity` towards the slower side, and the plates are not allowed to overshoot each other in a substep. Engine braking on overrun falls out of this. No speed governor in the manual modes: the redline cut limits the revs. Fuel and load are computed from combustion exactly as before.

- **Clutch pedal:** `e = smooth01((0.75 − pedal) / 0.5)`, the bite between 25% and 75%. Most pads report ○/B as a plain on/off button, so letting it go would drop the clutch in one frame; engagement therefore rises at most 2.5/s (and falls at once). Letting it out at idle with the wheels held still stalls within a second; with some revs it pulls away (both are tests).
- **Stall:** in "Manual with clutch", engine running, `e > 0.5` and the revs below 0.55 × idle, or below 0.35 × idle at any engagement, stops the engine. `DrivetrainState.stalled` is set for that step (a field, so the return type stayed a number); the Vehicle switches the engine off, lights STALLED (`clutchStalled`), and the sim emits `engine: 'stalledLugged'` for the toast and the stall sound.
- **Auto-clutch** (Manual, and precision crawl in either manual mode): out in neutral or during a shift; in once the gearbox side turns at 1.3 × idle; between, `(rpm − 1.05 × idle) / idle`, so revs above idle feed it in; and it lets go if the revs would drop below 0.9 × idle, so it never stalls. It rises at most 4/s.
- A stalled engine's drag on the wheels (`engineBrakeForce`) is scaled by the engagement: none in neutral or with the clutch in.

**Gears and pedals.** `gear` keeps its meaning (−1 reverse, 0 first) and `neutral` was added beside it, so every reader of `gear` still works and the HUD shows N, R or the number (`gearLabel`). A shift takes 0.35 s with the drive out. In the manual modes W/R2 is throttle in the selected gear and S/L2 only brakes; reverse is a gear. Changing range keeps the gear the player picked. The hold brake and hill-start assist are unchanged.

**Starting.** With a clutch the pedal starts the engine only in neutral or with the clutch held. A press in gear without the clutch sets `Vehicle.startBlocked` for the first step of that press only, and the sim emits `engine: 'clutchToStart'` once per attempt, for the toast. A vehicle arrives in neutral in the manual modes (`Simulation.addVehicle`), and switching to a manual mode with the engine off puts it in neutral, so the first press always starts it. The start prompt says to hold the clutch when it would otherwise be refused.

**Sound.** In the manual modes every change of gear, into or out of neutral included, clunks; in Automatic it still clunks only on changing direction, so the auto's own shifts stay quiet as before.

**Not done.** Shifting in "Manual with clutch" does not require the clutch (no grinding or missed gears); a rev-matching model was more than this step needed. Phones and the keyboard have no way to shift (a roadmap item).

**The Little Annan ford is now closed late.** At 5:45 pm a steady 16 km/h through the main ford stalls the engine, where before it was the way across. (v1.36: it is right on the edge; with the rough banks it can just get out as the engine dies, so the test now checks it floods to sputtering rather than which way it falls.) Crawling is still swept away, so late in the storm the shallow crossing downstream is the way over (its test still passes for both vehicles). `tests/storm.test.ts` now expects that, and checks the steady drive still crosses at 3 pm.

## v1.36: rough creek banks

The owner found the way into and out of the crossings too smooth.

**Why they were smooth.** `fordRamp` clamps the track at a ford to the bank's plane (`water + out × bankSlope`). That removes the step at the water's edge that v1.31 fixed, but because the clamp runs after the track's roughness is added, it also wiped out every bump, pothole and washout on the bank. Every ford was a perfectly even ramp.

**Rough banks on top of the ramp.** `bankRoughness` adds back, after the clamp, a rut scoured under one wheel line and then the other (it swaps about every three metres, starting on a side set by the crossing) and rocks from noise at about 1.4 m. Both are `CreekDef.bankRough` metres, 0.22 by default, so the wheel lines differ by up to about 0.4 m. It fades in over the first two metres from the water, so the water's edge stays a slope with no hidden step (the v1.31 lip test still holds), and out at the top of the ramp.

**Steeper small creeks.** The creeks laid by `creekAcross` (the paddock creeks and the three Bloomfield Track creeks) have banks at 0.45 instead of 0.35 (24° instead of 19°).

**Mud on the exits was tried and dropped.** Short black-soil bogs at the top of six more exit banks stopped the locked crawl at paddock-creek-south and the Bloomfield middle creek outright, even at 0.28 m, and the bog rules want creek-bank bogs deeper than 0.3 m with a marked rock in cable reach. The rough banks and steeper slopes alone keep the crawl tests passing, so this version ships those; exit mud would need its own tuning pass.

## v1.37: the clutch gearbox option shelved

The owner tried **Manual with clutch** (v1.35) and found a controller button a poor clutch: it is on or off, with nothing to feather, and the thumb that holds ○/B is also the one on the D-pad changing gear. The option comes out for now.

**What the player sees.** Settings → **Gearbox** offers **Automatic** and **Manual** only (`GearboxSetting` and `GEARBOX_SETTINGS` in `src/sim/vehicle/drivetrain.ts`). A save that had Manual with clutch (`'clutch'`) loads as Manual; anything else unknown still loads as Automatic. ○/B is no longer the clutch: it is the jack when the car is rolled and Back in menus, as before v1.35. The clutch row is gone from the Controls table, and its binding and glyph from `src/data/controls.ts`.

**Manual starts in any gear.** v1.35 started a manual gearbox only in neutral or with the clutch held. With no clutch button, Manual could never meet that in gear, so Manual now starts on the pedal in any gear (the clutch is already worked for the player). The "clutch in, or neutral" prompt and the `clutchToStart` toast remain only for the sim-level `'clutch'` mode, which no setting reaches, and they no longer name ○/B. The stall toast now reads "Stalled: press R2 to restart".

**What stays.** The clutch maths in the drivetrain (Manual's auto-clutch is built on it), the sim-level `GearboxMode` `'clutch'`, `VehicleInput.clutch` (no device sets it for now) and `tests/manualGearbox.test.ts`. The option returns with steering-wheel and pedal support, which gives it a real clutch pedal.

**Tests.** `tests/save.test.ts` covers the two settings and the `'clutch'` → Manual load; `tests/engineStart.test.ts` adds that Manual starts in 1st without neutral (the `'clutch'` sim test is unchanged); `tests/driveControls.test.ts` checks no controls row mentions a clutch, no button is bound to one, and holding ○/B in Manual leaves `clutch` at 0.

## v1.38: the crawl lesson gets a step to crawl

The third orientation sign says "walk the wheels up the step", and there was no step. The rock band on the bench was a `rockySteps` entry — a rise added to the track's own height profile — of 0.42 m at 175 m along Upper Daintree Road. The profile is written into a heightfield sampled every metre and blended in over ±0.6 m either side, so 0.42 m came out as a 9° ramp on a climb already running 5–7°. There was nothing to climb, only rock-coloured ground.

**A `steps` ledge instead of a `rockySteps` rise.** `TrackStepsDef` was already in the engine, and nothing used it: it builds a solid box across the track with a vertical face, leaning with the graded road and reaching below the ground under its whole footprint. That is the shape the sign describes, and it cannot be smoothed away, because it is a collider rather than terrain. The band is now `steps: [{ from: 175, to: 175, spacing: 1, rise: 0.35 }]` — one ledge, not a staircase — and the two boulders either side are unchanged.

**0.35 m.** The lowest part of every vehicle in the game sits about 0.40 m up (the Perentie's transfer case is 0.41 m, the Hilux's 0.40 m), so a 0.35 m face is one the belly clears and the tyres have to be walked up. The ledge's top runs 2.4 m along the track, which the engine works out from the local grade; on this bench the road only climbs about 0.1 m in that distance, so there is a drop off the back as well. That is what a rock band across a bench does, and taking the far side at a crawl is the same lesson as the near one.

**It shows as rock.** `rockySteps` painted the ground round them rock, and `steps` did not, which would have left granite standing out of red clay. The painting now covers both, at each ledge and 2 m past it, so the landing is rock too. The ledge boxes are bevelled 0.12 m like the crawl-trail granite, so the band reads as rock rather than a kerb.

**Surprises keep off it.** `nearObstacle` in `surprisePlan.ts` already kept rockfalls and fallen trees 50 m clear of a `rockySteps` rise; it now does the same for a `steps` ledge, so a surprise cannot land on the lesson.

Driven headless over 150–200 m, the Perentie, the short-wheelbase Land Rover, the Hilux, the Camel Trophy car and the Unimog all walk up it, slowing from 1.6 m/s to about 1.1 m/s at the face.

**Still open.** Roaring Meg's rock ledge has the same problem: its note calls it "a step of rock about a metre high" and its `rockySteps` rise of 1 m comes out as a 24° ramp. The granite pinch on the Helenvale knoll is five more of them. Both are left for a later version.

## v1.39: pin how wet the track is, and what the browser tests found

**v1.33 made the browser tests non-deterministic, and this fixes it.** A new run's seed is random (`newRunSeed`), so rolling the day from it meant the e2e tests met a different track on every run — and they pin `?surprises=off` for exactly that reason. `m6-audio` caught it: it expects a kookaburra and got a magpie. The run it launched was a different day.

`?track=dry|reopened|wet` pins the condition, beside `?seed=N` and `?surprises=off`, and every e2e entry URL now asks for `track=reopened`, the just-reopened day their expectations were written against (seed 1 would not have done: it rolls dry). `m6-audio` passes again. The parameter is not only for the tests: driving the same stretch on a dry day and then a wet one is the way to feel what v1.33 did, and the README's checklist asks for that.

**The e2e suite had not run in a cloud session for some time.** v1.33's `CHROMIUM_PATH` made it possible again, and the first full run was 33 passed, 9 failed. Eight of the nine fail identically on v1.32, so they are not regressions, and they are on the roadmap with their names. The clearest is `devMode:4`: at half throttle the car covers 1.9885 m where the test wants more than 8 m, the same figure to four decimals on both versions, so something in that test or in test mode has been wrong for a while rather than drifting. `episode:103` fails on v1.32 and passes here after a 20-minute run, so it is flaky, not fixed.

CI runs lint, the unit tests and the build, not e2e. That is why none of this showed up in a deploy.

## v1.40: the crawl band is broken bedrock, not one clean slab

v1.38 gave the crawl lesson a step, and it was still easy in the dry. The reasons were in the numbers. Rock is the grippiest surface in the game (1.1, above dirt's 1.0), so the band held better than the road either side. The ledge was one flat-topped box square across the track, so both front wheels stepped up together and all four stayed loaded. And 0.35 m against 0.8 m tyres is a bump. Height was the only thing resisting, and height alone is weak.

**Built from `rockStep` stones instead.** `courseKit.ts` already had the piece: a band of bedrock as a `ridge` stone, a riser at its downhill edge and a tread flatter than the hill running back into the slope, which Granite Spur and the park course use for exactly this. The `steps` ledge box is gone and `upper-daintree-road` now carries `crawlBand`, five of them with the two boulders.

**Split pairs, with the low half changing sides.** Each pair is two ledges of about 2.4 m, one either side of the centreline, at different heights and leaning different ways, so one front wheel climbs before the other and the car twists on it. The first pair is low on the left (0.22 m) and high on the right (0.38 m); the second, 3 m further on, is low on the right (0.24 m) and high on the left (0.36 m). The easy way across is therefore a diagonal that has to be steered while the car is already articulated, which is the point. A full-width 0.26 m lip at 179.4 m finishes the band onto the bench.

**Nothing stands over 0.38 m**, because a vehicle's lowest point is about 0.40 m up (the Perentie's transfer case 0.41 m, the Hilux's 0.40 m). A picked line clears; a bad one grounds.

**It is a line now, not a bump.** Crawling the band in low range with open diffs, full throttle: down the centre the Perentie tilts 14.5°, pitches 10.7° and never drops below 1.35 m/s; hugging the right it tilts 16.2°, pitches 15.9° and stops for two seconds before it gets through. The short-wheelbase Land Rover tilts 20°. With both lockers every vehicle in the game crosses it.

**The engine changes from v1.38 go with the ledge.** The ledge box's bevel and the rock painting for `steps` are reverted, since nothing uses `steps` again. In their place, the ground is painted rock under a `ridge` stone — bedrock coming up through the track, unlike a boulder sitting loose beside it — and `nearObstacle` in `surprisePlan.ts` keeps rockfalls and fallen trees 50 m clear of a ridge stone rather than of a `steps` ledge.

**Still open**, as in v1.38: Roaring Meg's rock ledge is a 1 m `rockySteps` rise that comes out as a 24° ramp, and the granite pinch on the Helenvale knoll is five more of them. Both would suit the same treatment.

## v1.41: the 80 Series reference, as far as licensing allows

The roadmap asked to compare and correct the 80 Series against real Sooty MkII photographs. **That is blocked, and not on effort.** No photograph of Shaun Whale's car exists under a licence that allows redistribution in this repository: searched again in November 2026 across Wikimedia Commons and the open web, it appears only in 4WD 24/7 videos, magazine features and retailer pages. `reference/vehicles/README.md` only permits CC BY, CC BY-SA, CC0 or public domain, so none of those may be committed, and the model cannot be checked against the car it is named after.

What could be done without pictures was done.

**The written specification is now recorded.** [MY4X4's build page](https://www.my4x4.info/sooty) is text, so it can be cited: a 1993 80 Series with the 1HD-FTE factory turbo diesel and an MTQ CT 26, King Springs with Superior drop arms and an adjustable panhard, General Grabber X3 tyres on bead locks, an ARB air locker **on the rear only**, a Dominator winch, a Custom RV bull bar with two 6 inch Livid lights, a 22 inch light bar, a reinforced roof rack, and scrub rails with custom sliders. It gives no lift height and no tyre size, so Repco's 3 inch and 35 inch figures stand as the only sources for those.

**Three places the game's car disagrees with it**, now written into `SOURCES.md` rather than left implicit: the game gives every vehicle both diff locks where Sooty has the rear only (that is the shared control scheme, not a decision about this car, so it is left); the snorkel is in Repco's rundown and in the model but not in MY4X4's list; and the rear bar with the swing-away spare is likewise in one source and not the other. Neither source is a photograph, so neither of the last two can be settled now.

**The factory body was re-checked.** `side-left.jpg` has a calibrated studio camera recorded for it (`studio.view(-4.87, 0.648, 0.121, 0, 0.648, 0.121, 40.5)` at 1600 × 1000 with `?stock&plain`), so the outline can be laid over the photograph. Re-rendered and compared: nothing beyond the deviations already listed under "Check these first" — the flare openings enlarged on purpose to clear the 35s, the front flare reaching a little further forward below the bumper, and the rear flare's tail 4 cm long. No body change was made, because none was warranted.

**Nothing in the game changed**, so there is nothing to drive for this version. To go further the owner would have to supply photographs he owns or may redistribute, or decide to drop the Sooty framing and call the car a built-up 80 Series.

## v1.42: Roaring Meg's ledge and the Helenvale granite, built the same way

The two places v1.38 and v1.40 left open. Both were `rockySteps` — a rise added to the track's height profile — and both came out as ramps, because the profile is written into a heightfield sampled every metre and blended in over ±0.6 m. Roaring Meg's note promised "a step of rock about a metre high"; what was there was a 24° ramp. Helenvale's five rises of 0.4–0.55 m barely showed above a slope already running 17–28°. Both are now `rockStep` ridges, as the crawl lesson is.

**Roaring Meg: five ledges, no terrain rise.** The 1 m `rockySteps` is gone and the band carries the climb itself: 0.42 and 0.50 m split across the track at 205–206 m, 0.38 and 0.46 m at 208–209, and a 0.36 m lip across the width at 211. The low half changes sides between the pairs, so one front wheel climbs before the other and the easy way up is a diagonal. The note now says what is there: about a metre of rock in three ledges with a lip to each.

**Keeping the 1 m rise under the ledges did not work.** The first attempt left it in place and set the stones on its face. The Perentie hung up dead at 207.9 m with both lockers in and never moved, and at a rise of 0.45 m the Hilux hung up at 208.1 m: the ramp's steepest part and the ledges were in the same three metres. Dropping the rise to zero and making the stones taller gives the same metre of climb, in steps a car can walk up one axle at a time.

**Helenvale: five split pairs on the knoll.** The slope does most of the work there, so the risers are lower than Roaring Meg's — 0.24 to 0.38 m, paired at 1451, 1463, 1476 and 1489 m with a 0.30 m lip at 1501. The `rockySteps` are gone; the five big boulders either side and the three loose rocks on the treads stay, because the note describes them.

**Measured.** Crawling in low range at full throttle with **open** diffs, the Perentie cannot climb Roaring Meg on any line: it stops at 203–206 m and slides back. With both lockers it goes up at about 1.1 m/s, the short-wheelbase Land Rover tilting 31.7°. Every vehicle in the game crawls both bands with the lockers in. High range at 2 m/s still fails Roaring Meg and cuts tyres, which is what the stretch test asks of it.

## v1.43: the 40 Series' second spare leaves the bonnet

The second spare on the short-wheelbase 4WD lay flat on the bonnet, clamped through the hub (`bonnetSpare` in `src/render/vehicleEquipment.ts`). From the driver's seat it sat in the middle of the windscreen. `front-34.jpg` shows the bonnet clear, so the spare was game kit, not the factory bonnet.

**Where it went.** It now lies flat on the roof rack that was already there, over the recovery boards and left of the canvas roll. Centre at x −0.24, z 1.02: left of the vehicle's centre so the tread stays about 3 cm inside the left rail, and far enough back that the whole tyre is behind the driver's eye (z 0.44). The same centre clamp holds it. The plate sits 6 mm above the boards, thick enough that the lower hub is inside the plate rather than in the timber, and a spine in the 60 mm slot between the boards carries the plate from the rack deck, so the spare still has something under it when the boards are laid on a crossing.

**Why there, and not on the deck.** The boards run the length of the basket on the left and down the middle, and the toolbox and the canvas roll take the right. A 0.76 m tyre will not sit on the slats without going through one of those, and none of them moved. Standing the spare upright at the back of the rack would clear them, and would also stand about a metre above the roof on a short hardtop. Flat in the basket is how the Patrol, the 60 Series and the LC79 already carry a second spare.

**What did not move.** The body, the door spare (still on the left rear door, as `rear-34-left.jpg` and `rear-34-orange-1977.jpg` show), the jerry can, the boards, the roll, the toolbox, the bull bar and the collision boxes. The bonnet spare was already outside those boxes; this one is too. It is drawn only.

Compared in the studio before and after, from the driver's seat, the front, the front-left and above. The bonnet is clear, the spare is inside the rack rails, and it does not meet the boards, the roll, the toolbox or the roof.

## v1.44: the workflow, trimmed

The first phase of `docs/PLAN-efficiency.md`. Nothing in the game changes; what changes is how long it takes to check and ship one.

**Prettier walked the photographs.** `prettier --check .` took 23 s, and 8 s of that was spent under `reference/` and `research/` on 18 small Markdown and JSON files, because it has to list every directory to find them. They are reference material, not code; `reference`, `research` and `batocera` are now in `.prettierignore`. Lint fell from 53 s to 28 s here.

**The build type-checked the tests.** `npm run build` ran `tsc --noEmit` over `tsconfig.json`, which includes `tests`, `e2e` and `scripts`: 1,196 files, none of which ship. The build now checks `tsconfig.app.json` (`src` and `vite.config.ts` only), and the full check is `npm run typecheck`, which CI runs beside lint. `incremental` is on for both, with their build info under `node_modules/.cache`, so a second local run takes about 2 s. The build fell from 29 s to 17 s. The Playwright server command uses `vite build` alone for the same reason.

**CI pulled 314 MB twice a run.** The repository pack is 314 MB and 313 MB of it is the reference photographs, which nothing in the code, the tests or the build reads (the only mentions in `src/` are doc comments pointing at them). Both jobs in `deploy.yml` now use a sparse checkout: `src`, `tests`, `e2e`, `scripts` and `public` for the checks, `src` and `public` for the build. Cone mode keeps the root files (configs, `index.html`, `package.json`) in either case.

**Docs that said the wrong thing.** The roadmap said trailers stay in test mode only. They do not: the Army supply run (v1.06) tows one and unlocks the Perentie, so the trailer code is not dead and the roadmap now says so. The README's layout listed a `docs/NEXT.md` that does not exist. SPEC.md still presented Episodes 2–4 as the game; a note under §1 and §2 records their removal in v0.73.

**What was not done.** The photographs themselves stay full size in history; shrinking them is a separate decision for the owner, since they are the vehicle reference. Phase 2 (the unit tests) follows.

## v1.45: quicker unit tests

The second phase of `docs/PLAN-efficiency.md`. Nothing in the game changes. The unit tests took about ten minutes, and on CI that was one runner working through 85 files after lint, with `tests/creb.test.ts` on its own for most of the time: here it took 433 s of the 561 s run while the other two workers finished early.

**Drives that were meant to fail ran out the clock.** `runLeg` in `tests/helpers/drive.ts` stopped early only when the car reached the end of the path. A test that expects a car not to get up a hill (high range on Big Red, open diffs on Bob's Hill) therefore drove its full 150 s every time, long after the car had stalled. `runLeg`, `driver().drive` and `driver().stretch` now take an optional give-up: once the car has gained no new ground along the path for that long it stops, and the result says `gaveUp`. Time on the winch cable does not count. It is off by default, so every test that expects a car to get through still drives the whole limit.

**20 s, and why.** `GIVE_UP_AFTER` is 20 s. Before choosing it, every drive that expects `done === false` was run to its full limit with the timer only watching, recording the longest the car sat without new ground before it got going again. For the converted drives the worst was 14.3 s (the short-wheelbase 4WD in Crawler Creek's Rock Garden); on the CREB climbs it was 12.8 s (the Hilux in high range on Bob's Hill) and on the CREB stretches 1.8 s. So no converted drive ever gained ground after a 20 s wait: giving up there ends each drive in the same place it would have reached anyway. With the give-up on, the converted drives stop after 31–97 s instead of 50–200 s, and each still meets its other checks (the washout leaves 20.8 and 21.5 m to go against 18; Roaring Meg in high range still cuts three tyres; high range at the paddock creek still bogs 9.1 m short).

**Where it is used.** CREB: the 24 high-range and open-diff climbs, the Unimog's three, the Camel in high range on dry Big Red. CREB stretches: the washout without planks, Roaring Meg in high range, the Bloomfield clay in high range and with open diffs, the Helenvale granite with open diffs, the paddock creek in high range. Ironbark Park's deep Mud Pits with open diffs, and Crawler Creek's Rock Garden with open diffs. **Not** used where the car does get going again after a long wait: the harder Axle Twisters with open diffs (47 s and 40 s waits), Boulder Gully and The Chute (up to 49 s), so those keep their full limit. Big Red's left-gutter line has its own loop and was left as it was.

**One world per file, and the long files split.** `creb.test.ts` built a second CREB world for the puddle check, because `addPuddles` changes the ground in place. It now fills the shared world and puts the water, surface and tint arrays back afterwards. `tests/world.ts` has a memoised `crebWorld()` that the five CREB files use (with the forks pool each file still builds its own, so this saves only within a file). `creb.test.ts` is now three files: `creb.test.ts` (the map, Big Red, and the other vehicles up Big Red), `crebClimbs.test.ts` (who stops on the wet clay, and the Unimog on Bob's Hill) and `crebLegs.test.ts` (the eight wet legs, and the other vehicles up Bob's Hill). `crebStretches.test.ts` is two: the Bloomfield Track and creek crossings moved to `crebStretches2.test.ts`. Test names and assertions are unchanged; the list of test names before and after is identical. `steepest` moved to the drive helpers so both stretch files can use it.

**CI runs the tests on three runners.** `deploy.yml` now has a `Lint and type check` job and a `Unit tests` job with a three-way matrix running `npm test -- --shard=N/3`, so the shards start beside lint rather than after it. The deploy path is unchanged and still waits only for the build.

**Measured** on this 4-core container, three workers, both runs in the same session: before, 561 s wall (745 tests, all green), slowest files `creb.test.ts` 433 s, `crebStretches.test.ts` 163 s, `parkLines.test.ts` 123 s, `challenges.test.ts` 97 s. After, 518 s wall (745 tests, all green), slowest files `creb.test.ts` 121 s, `crebClimbs.test.ts` 120 s, `parkLines.test.ts` 115 s, `crebLegs.test.ts` 109 s, `challenges.test.ts` 103 s. The converted tests: the 24 wet-clay climbs 182 s to 101 s, the Unimog's 22 s to 12 s, the Bloomfield knolls 58 s to 40 s, the Mud Pits 22 s to 9 s. Locally the gain is small because the three workers were already busy; what changed is that no file is a quarter of the run any more, which is what lets the CI shards share it. On CI before: the one checks job took 12 min 19 s, 10 min 55 s of it the tests.

**What was left.** Vitest's `--shard` splits files by a hash of their path, not by how long they take. With these files the shards get about 545, 600 and 225 s of test time, so the third finishes early; a custom sequencer that balances by duration would even them out, but CI has no timings to balance by without keeping a cache. The machine's timings also vary by 20–30 % between runs here (Ironbark Park took 175 s in one run and 115 s in the next), so the numbers above are one run each.

## v1.46: a quicker start

The third phase of `docs/PLAN-efficiency.md`. Nothing about driving changes; the game reaches the first frame sooner and no longer stalls on the way.

**Rapier's WebAssembly is a file of its own.** `@dimforge/rapier3d-compat` inlines its 3.0 MB WebAssembly as 4.1 MB of base64 inside the game's script, which the browser must download, parse and decode before compiling any of it. The plan suggested swapping to `@dimforge/rapier3d` with `vite-plugin-wasm` and `vite-plugin-top-level-await`. A smaller change does the same job: the compat package already ships the binary (`dist/rapier_wasm3d_bg.wasm`, byte for byte the inlined one), and its internal init takes a URL, which it fetches with `WebAssembly.instantiateStreaming`. Only the compat wrapper's `init()` ignores arguments and hands over the base64. `rapierWasmAsFile()` in `vite.config.ts` rewrites that one expression (`<decoder>.toByteArray("AGFzbQ…").buffer`) to the file's URL, for the browser build only (`apply: 'build'`), so Vite emits `assets/rapier_wasm3d_bg-<hash>.wasm`. If a future Rapier changes the expression the build fails rather than quietly shipping the base64 again. No new dependency. Node (`vitest`, `vite-node scripts/*`) and `npm run dev` still use the package as published, inlined bytes and all, so the tests and scripts are untouched. The service worker now precaches `wasm` too (`globPatterns`); 3.0 MB is under the 8 MB `maximumFileSizeToCacheInBytes`, so the game still works offline after one visit.

**The download starts on the title screen.** `src/main.ts` asks for the game script when the browser is first idle after the title appears (`requestIdleCallback`, `setTimeout` without it), keeps the promise and awaits it in `play()`. Once the script has arrived it starts Rapier's load too (`Game.preload()`), so the WebAssembly streams and compiles while the player reads the menus. A failed prefetch is caught (no unhandled rejection) and forgotten, both for the script and in `loadRapier()`, so pressing Drive asks again and shows the error panel as before if it fails then. The title still never waits for any of this: `e2e/launch.spec.ts` now holds the engine's request back and checks the menus work, and that the download does start in the background, instead of checking that it never starts.

**Shaders are compiled before Ready.** After the world is built, `Game.precompile()` poses the car and the chase camera as the first frame will, builds the ground and trees round them, and calls `renderer.compileAsync(scene, camera)`, then draws one frame under the loading screen (the label reads "Preparing shaders"). Where the browser has `KHR_parallel_shader_compile` (desktop and Android Chrome) the compile runs off the main thread and the frame is cheap; where it does not, the one frame does the linking there, behind the loading screen, instead of in the first second of driving, and it also covers the shadow map's own shaders, which `compileAsync` skips. In a test build 29 of the 30 programs the first 40 frames use exist at Ready; before, none did.

**Dusk adds the beams to the same car.** Dusk used to dispose the car's model and build it again with two spot lights, which re-ran the body build (measured in Node on this container: 180 ms for the Unimog, 240–460 ms for most, 740 ms for the short-wheelbase 4WD and 1,010 ms for the HJ60) and then recompiled every lit material in the scene for the new light count on the next frame. `VehicleModel.setBeams(on)` now adds or removes the beams' group on the existing model (0.1–1.6 ms), and `GameRenderer.showLights()` hides the stall: it compiles every program for the new light count with the lights visible, hides them again at once, waits until those programs report ready (`isReady()`, the parallel compile extension), then shows the lights. Frames in between draw without the beams, so the lamps reach the ground a fraction of a second after the toast. The other ways were rejected: three.js leaves invisible lights out of the count, so `visible = false` from the start would still recompile when they show; keeping both spot lights in the scene all day at `intensity = 0` keeps the count fixed but makes every lit pixel in a daylight frame evaluate two spot lights (and the full lighting for the ground in their cones) for lamps that are off. The ghost and the stranded and oncoming cars never had beams and are unchanged; the constructor's `beams` argument now just calls `setBeams(true)`. Dawn (taking the lights away) and a vehicle swap at night still compile on their next frame as before; a swap already hides behind its fade.

**Measured** with Playwright on the container's software renderer (SwiftShader), the low preset, a fresh browser each run, against `vite preview`, timing from pressing Drive on the welcome screen to the briefing's Drive button ("Ready"). Throttled means 1.6 Mbit/s down and 150 ms latency through the DevTools protocol; `vite preview` does not compress, so the throttled runs move the raw bytes.

|                                                    | before (v1.45)           | after (v1.46)                                         |
| -------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------ | ----------------------------------------------------- |
| game script                                        | 5,700 KB (2,058 KB gzip) | 1,591 KB (444 KB gzip)                                |
| Rapier WebAssembly                                 | inlined                  | 3,082 KB file (1,169 KB gzip), precached              |
| Drive to Ready, full speed                         | 21.5 s                   | 21.3 s without the warm-up frame, 33.6–36.7 s with it |
| Drive to Ready, throttled, Drive pressed at once   | 42.5 s                   | 37.3 s                                                |
| Drive to Ready, throttled, 15 s on the title first | 43.9 s                   | 38.9 s                                                |
| network's share (throttled minus full speed)       | about 21 s               | about 3–5 s                                           |
| shader programs compiled at Ready                  | 0                        | 29 of 30                                              |
| first frame after Drive                            | 8.6–14.4 s               | 6.7–8.2 s                                             |
| dusk, main thread spent rebuilding the car         | 180–1,010 ms             | under 2 ms                                            |

How to read it: on this 4-core container world generation is about 20 s of CPU, and the network now hides almost entirely behind it, because Rapier loads in parallel and the script is a quarter of the size. SwiftShader has no `KHR_parallel_shader_compile` and compiles each shader again on its first draws, so here the warm-up frame costs about 12 s at the loading screen and the first frames after Drive are still slow; on a GPU with the extension (the owner's Chrome) the warm-up is mostly in parallel and the first frames should draw at once. Every frame on SwiftShader takes 1.5–3.5 s, so the dusk frame times here are noise (before 5.1, 7.8, 4.5 s; after 1.8, 5.7 s); the CPU figure above is the one that holds.

**Left.** The one program that appears after Ready is made by something drawn only once driving starts; finding it would take a GPU trace. Dawn could warm its programs the same way as dusk if the clock ever runs through a night.

## v1.47: the knoll play area

The owner asked for the stretch before the Daintree River to be a place to warm up for hours before the CREB, with many different obstacles round the hill the village road climbs: a hard rock climb, a drop like the Gunshot, a steep descent, a switchback climb, a long bog with high sides and articulation.

**Where.** The village road climbs over the knoll (`village-knoll`, 19 m) on its west side; its ramp, crawl step, locker pinch and winch bog are the control lessons, so it was left alone. The knoll is too small and too taken up by the road to carry every line, so a higher top (`knoll-east-top`, a 25 m dome, 88 m radius) joins it on the east, with a 12 m flat summit where the lines meet. Its centre is 100 m from the road, so nothing the road stands on moved. Everything is in `src/data/episodes/crebKnoll.ts`.

**The loop.** The Knoll track leaves the village road at its second waypoint, on the flat before the low-range ramp, and ends where the river crossing starts, so a player can drive out of the village, play, and carry on across the river without coming back. It follows the ground and has no mud: a procedural bog on it would add to the deep-hole count `crebStretches.test.ts` pins.

**The lines.** Each leaves the loop or the summit and ends on the other, so the loop is always the way round:

- _Rock climb_: the foot follows the ground; the top 40 m is a pitch of about 26–28° with ledges of 0.32–0.5 m, split down the middle so one front wheel climbs first (the crawl band's pattern, taller). The short-wheelbase 4WD gets up in low range with both lockers, pitching to 44°.
- _Switchbacks_: four legs across the east face and three hairpins of 7 m radius. On a 25 m top the legs run nearly along the contours, so the hairpins took the climb (up to 21°, at full lock), and the short-wheelbase 4WD slid to a stop in the top one. Each hairpin is graded evenly from 15 m before it to 25 m after (`even`), so the turns are tight but not pinches.
- _Side slope_: round the west flank at 20–24° of lean for about 60 m, then down the knoll's east flank to the loop by the river. It first climbed the west shoulder to the summit, but that was 22–27° straight up while still leaning, and the autopilot nearly rolled it. The track keeps all the hill's lean (`camber: 1`); `cant` tops it up where the flank is gentler and takes some off where it is steeper, and takes the knoll's own flank (27° the other way) back to about 20°. It is hard-packed dirt, not clay: on wet clay at that lean both the short-wheelbase 4WD and the Troop Carrier slid off the flank; on dirt both get round, rolling to 34°.
- _The drop_: the ground is pinned at the lip and drops about 2.3 m in the next 2.5 m (about 45° over the 1 m grid), with a band of rock across the lip standing 0.7 m higher, so the nose goes over a face of about 3 m. It is a `ridge` stone with its riser on the downhill side, the crawl band's rock step turned round. A shallow mud hole lies at the foot. The short-wheelbase 4WD crawls off it in low range, pitching nose-down to 63°, and drives out.
- _Steep descent_: straight down the north face, about 30° at the top, with the Ironbark park's erosion ruts.
- _Axle twisters_: fourteen holes, 0.5 m deep, under alternate wheels with a 0.3 m mound beside each, as in the Ironbark park's harder line. Dug level across (`camber: 0.1`), so the twist is the holes' and not the hillside's.
- _Bog trench_: cut 1.4 m below the ground with banks at 1.6 rise per metre, and four deep bogs end to end over about 50 m (deepened in v1.48). The banks are well over 1.5 m above the bog floor, so `creb.test.ts`'s "no track runs in a trench" check skips this one track. `crebKnoll.test.ts` checks its walls instead.

**Junctions are pinned.** Each line's end on the loop is pinned to the loop's graded height there (`joinLoop`, measured). Unpinned, a line graded about half a metre above the loop left a narrow ridge across it where they met, and the autopilot stalled on the one below the steep descent. `crebKnoll.test.ts` checks the profiles agree and the loop has no hump at each junction.

**Checked by driving** in the wet with the autopilot, low range and both lockers: the short-wheelbase 4WD round the loop and along every line (the bog trench with one winch to a marked rock on its bank, since `map.bogs` plant no trees), and the Troop Carrier on the rock climb, switchbacks, side slope and drop.

**Not a forced route.** None of the lines is on the route, the timed sections or the scoring. The arrival notes in `creb.ts` say what each wants.

## v1.48: the bog trench's bogs made deep enough

v1.47's unit tests found one failure after it shipped: `creb.test.ts` holds every hand-placed bog on the map to the road bogs' rule, deep (sticky black soil) and more than 0.6 m, with a marked winch rock within 16 m, because `map.bogs` plant no trees of their own. Two of the bog trench's four bogs were 0.45 and 0.55 m, and its marked rocks stood ahead of the bogs rather than beside them.

- The trench's bogs are now 0.62, 0.66, 0.72 and 0.64 m, and its four marked rocks stand on the bank beside each bog, alternating sides. The short-wheelbase 4WD still gets through in the wet with low range and both lockers, now with two pulls on the winch instead of one.
- The mud hole below the drop stays a shallow splash (0.35 m, not sticky): a winch bog straight after a 3 m drop would be unfair. The test skips that one by id, with a note.

## v1.49: a steadier frame rate

The fourth phase of `docs/PLAN-efficiency.md`. Nothing about driving changes; the picture should look the same, drawn with less work. This container has no graphics chip (Playwright runs on SwiftShader, seconds a frame), so every figure below is main-thread time and allocation in Node on the real CREB map, or draw calls and triangles from `renderer.info`, which SwiftShader reports correctly. GPU time is the owner's to see with `?debug` on the phone and desktop.

**The frame cap.** `GameLoop` drew on every `requestAnimationFrame` while physics steps at 60 Hz, so a 120 Hz phone drew everything (colour and shadow pass) twice per step and `RenderScale`, which counts a frame under 17.8 ms as fast, never lowered the resolution: it just ran hot. A refresh is now skipped when it ran no step and under 15 ms has passed since the last drawn frame, or ran a step and under 9.5 ms has passed (two steps on neighbouring refreshes happen when the clock's phase drifts; 9.5 ms is above a 120 Hz frame and below a 90 Hz one, so 90 Hz screens still draw every step). The game is handed the time since the last drawn frame, so its clocks keep time; the sim and draw timings in `?debug` are per drawn frame. Tests that drive fixed steps, a paused game (the menus still animate) and `?bench` draw every refresh. `tests/loop.test.ts` drives the loop with fake refreshes:

| screen          | 60 Hz | 90 Hz | 100 Hz | 120 Hz | 144 Hz | 165 Hz | 240 Hz |
| --------------- | ----- | ----- | ------ | ------ | ------ | ------ | ------ |
| draws/s before  | 60    | 90    | 100    | 120    | 144    | 165    | 240    |
| draws/s, capped | 60    | 60    | 60     | 60     | 60     | 60     | 60     |

(10 s of refreshes with 0.2 ms of jitter; physics 60 steps/s in every case.) A strong desktop on a high-refresh monitor may want the smoother in-between frames back, so pause menu → Settings → **Frame rate** has **60** (the default everywhere) and **Screen** (every refresh), saved with the other settings.

**Ground cover is culled from spheres.** The cover `BatchedMesh` (10,790 instances on Medium, 23,426 on High) used three's per-instance culling, which rebuilds every instance's sphere from its matrix each draw. `fillTile` now writes each instance's world sphere (its geometry's sphere, scaled by the larger of its scales and grown by the centre's distance off the axis so any turn fits) into a `Float32Array` by instance id, and the batch uses `cullTreesBySphere` as the trees do. In Node, under the Bloomfield forest at (111, −773), one cull went from 0.42 to 0.05 ms on Medium (5,082 live instances) and from 0.90 to 0.13 ms on High (13,296). The cover casts no shadow, so that is once a frame. Drawn instances: 937 before, 938 after (the spheres are a little generous); in the browser the cover's triangles at the same spot were 63,570 before and 63,660 after, and identical at the start.
