**Meadow uploads only what it placed.** Each 8 m cell the camera crossed set `instanceMatrix.needsUpdate`, which uploads all 10,000 instances (640 KB), and ran `computeBoundingSphere()` over every blade. It now marks `count × 16` floats for upload, keeps its temporaries at module level, and sets the bounds from the cell's centre and radius and the lowest and highest ground it placed on (checked in Node against every blade over 300 cells: all inside). Per cell: 181 KB uploaded on Medium (2,830 blades), 494 KB on High (7,714); main thread 1.1–1.7 ms → 0.9–1.0 ms on Medium and 2.7–3.3 ms → 2.1–2.5 ms on High.

**The course hint searches five times a second.** The HUD searched every crawl trail's and route-hint track's 1 m polyline every frame (14 route-hint tracks with 6,944 points, the Bloomfield track alone 2,294; this map has no crawl trails), allocating an object per segment and arrays for the filter and sort. `CourseHintCache` (in `hudState.ts`) keeps one search for 200 ms; the search skips everything when the map's track-distance grid says the car is over 3 m from any track's edge, and `segmentDistance` can fill a scratch object. Driving 1,000 frames along the Bloomfield track in Node: 0.09–0.20 ms and 5.5 KB of garbage a frame before, 0.012 ms and 0.24 KB after; 40 m off the track 0.15 ms before, 0.0002 ms after. The hint shown is the same at every one of those 1,000 points (unthrottled comparison); it can appear up to 0.2 s later.

**Smaller per-frame garbage.** `TerrainMesh.update` keeps its lists between frames, caches each node's children, walks with a method instead of a closure, keeps the drawn nodes in an array (clearing a `Set` allocates a new table every time) and evicts only after a build: 44 µs and 11.5 KB a frame → 11 µs and 0.34 KB. Particles move a dead particle's numbers without a closure or arrays, use fixed shape and fade functions, and upload only live instances, nothing at all while a pool stays empty: an idle frame went from three full-buffer uploads per pool and 0.33 KB to no uploads and 0.05 KB. `Lighting.follow` 0.34 KB → 0. The chase camera's two vectors and the plank view's `Set` are kept between frames. The HUD compares before writing the run texts, range, drive buttons (text and `aria-pressed`), course hint, planks and engine state, as its neighbours already did.

**Left out: splitting the props mesh by 256 m cell.** The merged mesh of every marker post, ledge, washout wall, granite stone and bridge timber is 10,986 triangles (11,146 in the whole props group), about 22,700 a frame with its shadow pass, against 810,000–1,113,000 for the whole frame at the three places measured (Daintree Village start, the Bloomfield forest, Roaring Meg) on Medium. Splitting it could save at most about 2% of the triangles, for 5–15 more draw calls in each pass, so it stays one mesh.

**Measured in the browser** (SwiftShader, Medium, `window.__game.stats()` and `breakdown()`), before → after: start 465 calls / 1,113,165 triangles → 466 / 1,113,283; Bloomfield forest 262 / 810,737 → 261 / 810,849; Roaring Meg 266 / 821,689 → 265 / 821,341. The draw is unchanged, as it should be: the wins are the second draw a 120 Hz phone no longer makes and main-thread time and garbage, not triangles.

## v1.50: signs at both ends of the knoll's lines

The owner kept arriving at the wrong end of one-way lines in the knoll play area: v1.47 signed only each line's start. Each line now has a sign at both ends (`endSign` at end 1 as well as end 0, in `crebKnoll.ts`). The start names the line and, where it has one, its direction; the far end says which way it goes:

| line          | start                 | far end                    |
| ------------- | --------------------- | -------------------------- |
| Rock climb    | Rock climb — up, hard | Rock climb — up only       |
| Switchbacks   | Switchbacks — up      | Switchbacks — either way   |
| Side slope    | Side slope            | Side slope — either way    |
| The drop      | The drop — down, hard | The drop — down only       |
| Steep descent | Steep descent — down  | Steep descent — down only  |
| Axle twisters | Axle twisters         | Axle twisters — either way |
| Bog trench    | Bog trench — winch    | Bog trench — either way    |

The rock climb, the drop and the steep descent are marked one way because the ledges, the lip and 30° of wet clay are not meant to be driven the other way. The rest can be driven both ways. `crebKnoll.test.ts` now checks for a sign within 14 m of each end.

## v1.51: dead episode fields gone, and the docs split

The fifth phase of `docs/PLAN-efficiency.md`. Nothing about driving changes.

**What was dead, and how that was checked.** `rescue` (Episode 3's bogged stockman, with its `RescueDef`), `delivery` (Episode 4's drums to park at the goal) and `lastLeg` (a mission line after the rescue) were fields on `EpisodeDef` that only the removed episodes set. `src/data/episodes/creb.ts` defines `storm`, `deadline`, `timedSections`, `traffic`, `stranded` and `surprises` and none of the three, and `episodeOrder` is `['creb']`; `grep` found `delivery` set only by `tests/delivery.test.ts` and the other two set nowhere. Gone with them: the stockman's stuck vehicle and his look, the `rescued` handler's non-stranded branch, `EpisodeRun.rescued` and the goal gate on it, the delivery condition in `EpisodeRun.check` (which no longer takes the trailers), the mission line's rescue and last-leg cases, `Checkpoint.rescued`, the "Not without the stockman" / "Park the upright trailer here" nag at the goal (it could not fire on the CREB), `rescued` in the test hook's `episode()`, and the "Next episode" button after a finish (with one episode it always showed **Main menu**; `toMainMenu` and `save` lost their episode argument, which only that button passed). In all, 123 lines out of `game.ts`, `episode.ts`, `episodes.ts`, `testHook.ts` and `save.ts`, 22 in; 67 of them in `game.ts`.

**Kept.** `StuckVehicle`, because the stranded drivers use it (its comments now say so), and all trailer code, because the Army supply run tows a trailer and unlocks the Perentie. Its start and arrival check moved out of `game.ts` into `src/sim/supplyRun.ts` (`hitchSupplyTrailer`, `supplyDelivered`, and `deliveryArrived` from `episode.ts`) so a unit test can reach them. Test mode's trailer buttons (`devTrailer`) are untouched. The checkpoint's `delivery` (the trailers and drums as they were at the camp) stays too: the supply run needs it.

**Old saves.** The save parser already rebuilt each checkpoint field by field, so an old checkpoint's `rescued` is now simply not copied; a save from before this version loads as before. A unit test writes `rescued: true` into a saved checkpoint and checks it loads without it.

**The supply run's first tests.** `tests/delivery.test.ts` tested the `delivery` field through a stand-in CREB episode. It now tests the supply run: the box trailer behind a ball hitch and the No. 5 behind the Unimog's pintle, each with the two drums; delivery (the Perentie unlock) only with both drums on a parked, upright trailer inside Wujal Wujal's camp, and not with one drum, a moving or tipped trailer, the camp moved, or no camp; and `supply` surviving the save. Its other tests (trailer round-trips, spilled drums, floating drums, punctures) stay as they were.

**`docs/DECISIONS.md` split.** At 490 KB it was too big to read whole. The v0.01–v0.99 sections (about 1,360 lines, 266 KB) moved verbatim and in order to `docs/decisions/v0.md`. This file keeps the inherited 4WD Simulator notes, a line saying where v0.x is, and v1.00 on (now 236 KB). No README, SPEC or ROADMAP link pointed at a section anchor. CLAUDE.md step 2 now says a new section goes at the end of this file and that pre-v1.00 history is in `docs/decisions/v0.md`.

**README's "What to test" trimmed.** It had 167 boxes back to v0.02. It now keeps the last ten versions (v1.42 to v1.51), and the 157 older ones moved verbatim to `docs/TESTED.md`, where an unchecked box is an unverified check, not a known fault. Two of the ten were dropped as no-ops: **v1.41** (reference notes only, nothing in the game to test; the request for Sooty photographs is in ROADMAP) and **v1.44** (a workflow change with nothing to drive; its quicker CI is checked again by every later run). v1.35's "Manual with clutch", shelved in v1.37, is in `TESTED.md` with the rest of the older checks. ROADMAP's line about real-device checks names both files.

**Scripts.** `scripts/tree-lab.ts` (drive into a tree near River Flat Camp, from 4WD Simulator's test world) was referenced nowhere, so it is deleted. `scripts/vehicle-shots.ts` is referenced by `reference/vehicles/README.md`, so it stays and README's project layout now describes it.

## v1.52: five pieces out of game.ts

The sixth phase of `docs/PLAN-efficiency.md`: five chunks of `src/core/game.ts` moved into their own files, each a straight move with no change to what the game does. `game.ts` went from 2,073 lines to 1,820.

| Chunk                                                                                                                                                                                        | Now in                                                             | Lines   |
| -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------ | ------- |
| Sim events to toasts and sounds (surprises, trailer events and bumps, range, engine, diffs, handbrake, lights, damage, repairs, knocks)                                                      | `src/core/simFeedback.ts`, `wireSimFeedback(sim, hud, audio, ctx)` | 143     |
| Oncoming traffic: the `Traffic` plans, each car's kinematic body and model, its toasts, the per-step pose snapshot and placing, the per-frame draw, and clearing them on a restart from camp | `src/core/trafficCars.ts`, `TrafficCars`                           | 92      |
| Anchor picking: the 0.4 s count of anchors in reach, USE opening the chooser with two or more, and the tap or click that picks one on screen                                                 | `src/core/anchorPicker.ts`, `AnchorPicker`                         | 84      |
| Trailer and drum views: keeping a view for each trailer and loose drum, drawing them each frame, where an unhitched trailer was left, and test mode's hitch                                  | `src/core/deliveryViews.ts`, `DeliveryViews`                       | 104     |
| The HUD's episode block: title, mission, clock, deadline line (the grace time once dark), and the dial's hours                                                                               | `episodeHud(run, episode)` in `src/core/hudState.ts`               | 28 more |

**What stayed in `Game`.** The `tool` and `rescued` handlers stay beside the rest of the run's state, because they set `winched` and free a stranded driver, which feed the checkpoint and the unlocks. The pointer-down listener that brings the touch controls back stays in `Game`; `AnchorPicker` adds its own listener after it, so the order is the same. `Game.devTrailer` is a one-line pass-through, because `window.__game.setTrailer` and the dev panel call it. The unused public `Game.traffic` went into `TrafficCars.traffic`; nothing outside reached it. None of the five turned out tangled: the most any takes is six constructor arguments (the sim, the scene or camera and canvas, the player's vehicle id and a getter for the vehicle, and a toast callback, since the HUD is built after the trailer views and the traffic).

**Tests.** `tests/simFeedback.test.ts` wires a bare event bus to a fake HUD and a fake audio system and checks the toasts and sounds for a puncture, other vehicles' events (none), bumps and dents (sound only), a tyre plug, running dry with and without jerry cans, a lugged stall naming the controller's button, engine start and flood stall, range, diffs, handbrake, lights, precision crawl, and a trailer tipping and bumping. `tests/episodeHud.test.ts` checks the episode block before dark, after dark (grace time, urgent) and in a replay. The full unit suite runs after the first two moves and again after all five, and the browser suite once at the end; a failure there is fixed in a follow-up update.

## v1.53: the 40 Series ute

The owner asked for a ute version of the 40 Series already in the game, "a copy of the current shorty with a tray like this, and a longer wheelbase", sending two pictures of Shaun Whale's barn-find HJ47 farm truck from the 4WD 24/7 Cape York trip. The game's new vehicle is `ute40`, the **40 Series ute**: the short-wheelbase 40's cab, face, 4.2 L petrol six and three-speed automatic on the HJ47's 2,950 mm chassis, with a single cab and a steel farm tray, unlocked by **finishing the CREB in the Hilux**.

- **Built from the 40, not from scratch.** `src/render/vehicleBody.ts` now exports its front clip, windscreen, side and plan helpers and its constants, and its livery takes an optional bonnet colour. `ute40Body.ts` draws the 40's front and screen inside a builder transform that moves the whole cab 0.325 m forward with the front axle (`UTE_CAB_SHIFT`), then adds the cab's short sides behind the doors, rounded rear corners, a back panel with a 1.0 × 0.35 m rear window, the 40's roof cap cut square over the cab, a two-seat version of the 40's interior, and the gear. Nothing about the 40's own body or its photographs was reshaped: `shortie` and `camel` draw exactly as before (their bonnet colour defaults to the body colour).
- **The tray and the gear, from the owner's pictures.** A 2.4 × 1.8 m steel tray with its deck 0.90 m off the ground on runners and crossmembers, 0.26 m drop sides and a tailgate with hinges and latches, a tube headboard to 2.16 m with upright bars and a mesh plate, the red high-lift jack strapped to its right upright and an amber beacon on its top rail, a tarped load lashed down with two ropes on the right of the deck, the two recovery planks along the left, the second spare lying flat behind the load, the first spare slung under the tray behind the axle, mud flaps, a rear bar with tail lamps, plate, shackles and the tow ball. At the front a three-hoop steel bull bar with the winch and roller fairlead, three round spotlights on the centre hoop (they come on with high beam), the plate and the UHF aerial on its left end, a snorkel up the left screen pillar to a ram head above the roof, big door mirrors on arms. Chalky white cab and tray, the bonnet bare rust (a flat rust brown with four darker and lighter patches: the game has no textures), the split rims painted a rusty tan, a made-up plate.
- **Compared.** The studio renders (front, side, rear, both three-quarters, top, wheel and cockpit) were put beside the owner's two pictures before and after each change. The plate and the UHF aerial were moved to the vehicle's left to match the front picture. Still different from the pictures: the real truck's tyres are a size bigger than the 40's 7.50-16s, its headboard is a little lower, and its bonnet rust is mottled rather than flat. No Commons photograph of a stock HJ47 was committed (this container cannot download Wikimedia originals), so the cab-back position and the tray height are read off the owner's pictures by eye; `reference/vehicles/landcruiser-45-ute/SOURCES.md` lists what is published, what is measured in the model and what is estimated.
- **Physics.** The 40's tuning spread with a 2.95 m wheelbase, 1,900 kg, inertia scaled for the length, collision boxes for the cab, tray, headboard, load, bars and the under-tray spare, the winch on the bar and the tow ball behind the tray, the snorkel's intake at 1.62 m, a little more brake and anti-roll. The cab layout is the 40's moved forward with the cab (`shifted` in `src/data/vehicles.ts`). 54,700 triangles against the 40's 58,200. `tests/ute40.test.ts` checks the wheelbase and the shared drivetrain, the unlock and save, the open wheel wells, the deck under the planks, the dial faces at the shifted dash, 60 km/h in 7 to 12 s, the 30° climb in low range and a full-lock turn at 40 km/h; it uses about 20 L/100 km on the fuel test's light road.

## v1.54: joystick side

The owner asked for the option to swap the joystick's side on mobile. Settings (the title page and the pause menu) has a new **Joystick side** row, **Left** (the default) or **Right**. It shows only on a touch screen, next to **Phone driving**.

- **What moves.** On the right, the stick and the button cluster (HAND, 4WD, TOOLS, USE, the winch and UPRIGHT buttons, and GAS and BRAKE in Steer and pedals) trade sides. `TouchControls.setStickSide` puts `stick-right` on the controls and `touch-swap` on the body. The top-left row (II, MAP, LIGHTS, full screen), the compass and the dials stay where they were, because they are not under a thumb.
- **What follows the buttons.** The HUD prompts ("Attach winch") sit just above the buttons, so they move to the left with them, left-aligned. Held sideways the crawl gauges also sit above the buttons, so they move to the left too; held upright they stay at the top right.
- **Saved.** `Settings.touchStickSide` (`'left' | 'right'`) is saved with the other settings; a save without it loads as left. The Controls page on a phone says "bottom left" or "bottom right" to match.
- **Not changed.** The stick's size, dead zone and how it drives are as before; so is the button order inside the cluster. If the swapped layout needs the pedals in the other order, that is a follow-up.
- **Tests.** The save test checks the default and a round trip, and a browser test on a phone checks the stick and the buttons trade sides, that `stick-right` is set and cleared, and that the stick on the right still gives throttle.

## v1.55: the MQ Patrol hardtop

Steef asked for a couple more period vehicles and left the choice to me. The first is an Australian 1980–83 Nissan MQ Patrol (the 160 series) short-wheelbase hardtop (`mq`): the round-lamp car before the 1983 MK facelift, with the SD33 3.3 L indirect-injection diesel six, the four-speed, a part-time two-speed transfer and leaf springs on both live axles, in a dusty period mid blue with the white side stripe (my choice, in the spirit of the bright, stripe-kitted MQs Nissan sold here), with a bull bar and winch, a rear bar, a gutter rack and a second spare added for the game, and the first spare on a swing-away carrier on the rear door. Its unlock: **finish the CREB in the GQ Patrol**, the car that replaced it.

- **Research.** Ten Commons photographs (CC BY, CC BY-SA) are listed in `reference/vehicles/nissan-mq-patrol/SOURCES.md` and `manifest.json` with credits and the published dimensions; `research-notes.md` keeps every figure with its source and a confidence mark. Published: 2,350 mm wheelbase, 4,070 mm long, 1,690 mm wide, 1,880 mm high (the Australian Redbook figures for the 1981 SWB; Wikipedia gives 4,105 and 1,840), 7.50R16 tyres, SD33 70 kW at 3,600 rpm and 216 Nm at 1,800. Not found anywhere: the track (1,400 mm read off the photographs), the gear ratios and the transfer's low ratio (enthusiast figures, flagged unverified), the fuel tank (80 L assumed). **No photograph is committed:** this container was answered with HTTP 429 by `upload.wikimedia.org` for every original (the block the 60 Series work hit in v1.10); only 1,920 px thumbnails came through, which `reference/vehicles/README.md` forbids committing, so the model was built from those working copies and the manifest lists the originals to download.
- **Compared.** The Spanish-built 1983 SWB hardtop (front and rear three-quarters, the same round-lamp body) for the silhouette, the arches, the hardtop's seam and windows, the rear door and lamps; the 1985 LWB wagon square on for the grille panel, lamps, valance and indicators; the 1981 NZ ute for the bonnet and guards from the right; the Dutch 1986/87 hardtops (the facelift's front, the same body behind the A-pillar) for the roof, rear quarter and door carrier. The studio renders at the README's near-orthographic cameras were measured back against the published figures: roof 1.885 m, side skin 3.98 m long, door bottom 0.44 m off the ground, 1.69 m over the lips. No square-on side photograph of a stock car exists under a free licence, so there is no overlay yet; that is the first job once the originals are downloaded.
- **Body.** Flat upright sides below and above the belt, the bonnet and guard tops on one level with the door belt line, squared wheel openings with pressed lips, a near-upright flat screen, the hardtop's roof a step above the cab's with drip rails along both, a single rear door with its window, hinges on the right, upright amber-red-clear lamps on the corners, and the stripe cut into short pieces so it follows a dented panel. NISSAN on the grille and the rear door and PATROL on the doors are stroke capitals drawn in code.
- **Tuning.** 2,150 kg with two aboard and the gear; the SD33 curve peaks at 216 Nm and is governed past 4,000; four-speed 3.592 / 2.246 / 1.415 / 1.00 on 4.375 axles with a 2.0 low range; 80 L tank. It reaches 60 km/h in about 14 s, climbs the 30° test slope in low range, and stays under 20° of tilt in the full-lock turn at 40 km/h. Fuel on the light-road check sits inside the 9–17 L/100 km band in `tests/fuel.test.ts`. Engine sound: an SD33 tuning derived from the 2H's, busier and knockier, with a slow 24 V crank.
- **Wiring.** `mq` in the vehicle id, order (before the GQ), looks, packing (40 kg spares, 110 kg of room), unlocks and the finish check in `game.ts`; `tests/mq.test.ts` covers the geometry, the cab, the unlock and the driving checks. The whole vehicle is 62,700 triangles.
- **The ute's under-tray spare, fixed.** v1.53 hung the 40 Series ute's first spare below the chassis rails, where it showed under the rear bar almost on the ground. It now lies flat under the deck between the tray's runners, below the crossmembers, with its bottom level with the chassis rails and 0.5 m off the ground, and its collision box moved up with it.

## v1.56: browser test results for the game.ts extractions

A docs-only follow-up to v1.52 (Phase 6 of `docs/PLAN-efficiency.md`), which shipped before its browser tests finished, as CLAUDE.md allows. Nothing in the game changes, so README's "What to test" gets no entry (as with v1.44).

**Unit tests.** 767 of 767 passed locally after the first two moves (with the new `simFeedback` test), and CI's three unit-test shards passed on the merged v1.52.

**Browser tests on v1.52.** 33 passed and 9 failed in 57 minutes. Eight of the failures are on ROADMAP's known list: `devMode:4`, `episode:15`, `launch:40`, `launch:147`, `m4-tools:15`, `night:4`, `touch:234` and `touch:280`. The ninth, `vehicles:13` (changing to the Land Rover from the pause menu), timed out waiting for the engine after the swap. Run alone it passed on v1.52 (1.8 minutes) and failed the same way on v1.51, so the extractions did not cause it. It predates them and is flaky, and it is now on ROADMAP's list with a likely cause: the test starts the engine while `restartFromCamp`'s fade is still running, and the fade ends by switching the engine off. `m5-ui:35`, on the list, passed.

## v1.57: a real rock climb

The owner, on the knoll's rock climb: "This isn't a rock climb. It is a couple of rocks on a track", and then "it should be like a hill climb made entirely of rocks." The line was 136 m of graded rock-coloured track with eight ledges and two loose rocks in its top 30 m; the scattered boulders beside it were decoration. With open diffs the short-wheelbase 40 got up it.

**What real rock climbs have.** Trail write-ups of Australian hill climbs (Beer O'Clock Hill, Bob's Hill, One Tree Hill, The Boulders at Menai) and US rock-crawling trails (Bone Crusher, 7 Waterfalls, Noesser's Nasty Notch, Little MC) share a pattern: rock from bottom to top with nothing smooth between, stair-step ledges, an off-camber slab, a V-notch between rock walls where the wheels ride the sides, loose rock between the ledges, and the biggest ledge near the top.

**The new line, bottom to top** (metres along it, all stones on the same track as before, in `src/data/episodes/crebKnoll.ts`):

- **Broken granite, 5–30 m:** rows of low bedded rocks every 1.7 m, three then two across the track and staggered, 0.14–0.32 m proud, so every wheel climbs something and none runs clear.
- **The staircase, 32–55 m:** a ledge every 3.5 m, 0.26–0.40 m, full width and split in turn, with two loose rocks on the treads.
- **The off-camber slab, 58–70 m:** one 12 m whaleback across the track rolled 13° to the left.
- **The V-notch, 72–80 m:** two 7 m whalebacks either side, 0.55–0.6 m high, sloping in 15°, so the wheels ride their flanks. A first try (0.75 m, 18°, 0.1 m closer) stopped the 40 Series ute even locked; this is the one that lets it through.
- **Bedrock, 81–86 m:** low flat slabs (0.03–0.05 m) in two lanes, so no smooth track shows between the notch and the pitch.
- **The pitch, 86–114 m:** the old split ledges, each 0.04 m taller, and four loose rocks; then **the crux** at 118 m, one full-width ledge 0.48 m high.
- **Banks:** two rows of boulders along both sides from the loop to the summit, the inner at 2.7 m, the outer bigger at 3.9 m, so the line reads as a rock gully.

Rubble placed between the notch and the pitch stopped the ute (its long wheelbase was still in the notch while its front met the rubble and the first ledge), so it was taken out. Flat slabs laid over the pitch's treads as well, so the whole top showed rock, stopped both 40s on the ledges even at 0.03 m thick (a slab edge in front of a riser makes it a double step), so the pitch keeps the bare rock surface between its ledges. A 0.55 m crux stopped the ute; 0.48 m lets it through. The scripted drives are sensitive to small changes, so the margins are not large.

**Drives** (scripted, low range, crawl): the short-wheelbase 40 gets to the top with both lockers and stops at the notch with open diffs; the Troop Carrier and the 40 Series ute get to the top with both lockers. Before, the old line went with open diffs. `tests/crebKnoll.test.ts` now checks both. The note, the map label and the route hint describe the new line.

## v1.58: the Range Rover two-door

The owner asked for a couple more period vehicles, in keeping with the game, and left the choice open. The first is an Australian-delivered early-1980s two-door Range Rover (`rangie`): the original two-door body with the black grille, bumpers and floating-roof pillars of the 1981–84 cars, the 3.5 L Rover V8 on carburettors, the LT77 five-speed with the LT230 transfer (full-time four-wheel drive with a centre diff lock), coils at both ends, in Lincoln Green with a tan cabin, with a tubular roo bar and winch, two driving lamps on the bar, a towbar, a gutter rack and a second spare added for the game. It unlocks by **finishing the CREB in the Camel Trophy Land Rover**, the other starter vehicle, as the 60 unlocks from the 40.

- **Research.** Fourteen Commons photographs (CC0, CC BY and CC BY-SA) are credited in `reference/vehicles/range-rover-two-door/SOURCES.md` and `manifest.json`, with the published dimensions and `research-notes.md` keeping every figure with its source and a confidence mark. **None of the files is committed:** `upload.wikimedia.org` answered HTTP 429 to every download from this container's address, over several hours, while the Commons pages loaded; only thumbnails of 1,920 px or less came through, which the folder README does not allow committing. The model was built against those working copies, and the manifest carries the original URLs so the files can be fetched from a machine Wikimedia does not throttle. Published: 2,540 mm wheelbase, 4,470 mm long, 1,780 mm wide, 1,780 mm high (CarsGuide's Australian 1983 listing), 1,486 mm track, 190 mm clearance, 86 L tank, 92.5 kW and 258 Nm (automobile-catalog's 1981/1983 two-door). The model matches the first five; its skin is 4.31 m from the bonnet lip to the tailgate, 4.48 m over the bumpers and 4.98 m over the roo bar and tow ball. The LT77 ratios, the 1.192 high range and the 3.54 axles are enthusiast figures and are marked so; only the 3.32 low range has a catalogue source.
- **Matched by eye at a calibrated camera.** The side heights (waist crease 1.01 m, belt 1.27 m, glass top 1.70 m, doors 0.60 m, bonnet 1.03–1.09 m, arch tops 0.885 m) were read off the blue 1981 car's side photograph with the 0.75 m tyre and the wheelbase as the scale, then the render was checked at the studio's near-orthographic side camera against that car and the 1974 Australian car; the front against the square-on 1981 and 1975 cars; the rear against the 1975 and 1984 two-doors. The overhangs, the screen rake and the rear glass (drawn upright) are judged, not measured, and `SOURCES.md` says so.
- **Body.** Built the way the 60 was: side skins in bands that lean with the real panels (upright below the waist crease, tucked under at the sills, a little in above the crease, more up the glasshouse), with the glasshouse band black so the roof floats on the black A- and D-pillars; the clamshell bonnet is one shallow slab from its lip over the grille to the scuttle with a raised rail along each edge and RANGE ROVER in stroke capitals on the lip; the black grille panel holds fifteen slats, the round lamps in square surrounds and the amber-over-clear indicators; the front wings below the crease are one block round the engine; the tail has the lower tailgate with its letters and plate between the tall amber-clear-red lamps, the rear glass in the black upper band, a black valance and the bumper. Doors, quarter glass, shut lines, the push-button handles, the wing badges, the D-pillar vents, door mirrors, mud flaps and inner wheelhouses as on the other vehicles.
- **Gear and cabin.** The roo bar is three tube hoops on a folded pan with the winch and fairlead, as the green Australian car carries; its two driving lamps are the look's `roof` lamps and come on with high beam. The first spare stands in the load bay (the factory place), the second lies on the rack with a canvas bag, a jerry can and the planks. Rostyle wheels: a silver dished disc with five slots, five nuts and a plain cap (full-time drive, so no free-wheeling hubs). Tan vinyl buckets and bench, the square binnacle with two big dials, the transmission handbrake on the tunnel.
- **Tuning.** 2,150 kg loaded on long, soft coils (0.27 m travel) with light anti-roll, so it leans more than the 60 but passes the 40 km/h full-lock test; the V8's curve peaks at 258 Nm at 2,500 rpm and gives 92 kW at 5,000; idle 750 with the automatic coupling's capacity raised to 115 so the converter does not drag the idle down; 86 L tank, 0.27 efficiency (a carburettor V8 is thirsty: `tests/fuel.test.ts` allows 12–22 L/100 km). A new `roverV8SynthTuning` in `src/data/audio.ts` takes the 40's petrol six to eight cylinders in two banks with a lower idle and a 5,200 limiter.
- **Triangles.** The whole vehicle (body, four wheels and the planks) is about 55,000 triangles, under the 80,000 limit and below the 60's 78,000.
- **Tests.** `tests/rangie.test.ts` checks the published figures, the unlock and the save, the coils and open wheel wells, the dials in front of the driver, the idle, 0–60 km/h in 6–12 s, the 30° climb in low range and the full-lock turn; `tests/fuel.test.ts` has its consumption band.

## v1.59: shared plates, badges and jerry cans

The seventh and last phase of `docs/PLAN-efficiency.md`. Nothing in the game looks or drives differently: the same small parts that were copied from body to body are now drawn by one file, `src/render/bodyKit.ts`.

**What moved.**

| Part               | Was                                                                                                                         | Now                                                                                                                            |
| ------------------ | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ |
| Stroke font        | four `GLYPHS` tables (`perentieBody`, `lc79Body`, `hiluxBody`, `troopyBody`) and the Camel plate's own table                | one `GLYPHS` (digits, the dash and the letters the plates and badges use); where a body's letter differs it passes an override |
| Lettering loop     | a loop in each plate, both TOYOTA badges and the Camel plate                                                                | `strokeText(b, text, opts)`: centred on the plate, or from a starting corner running either way along x                        |
| White number plate | three identical `plate()` functions (Hilux "RN-036", LC79 "VDJ-79", Troop Carrier "HZJ-75"), each with its own `plateWhite` | `numberPlate(b, x, y, z, facing, text, ink)` and `PLATE_WHITE`                                                                 |
| Army plate         | `armyPlate` in `perentieBody.ts`, imported by the Unimog                                                                    | the same function in `bodyKit.ts`                                                                                              |
| Jerry can          | the Hilux's and the Perentie's (body, two diagonal ribs, handle, cap)                                                       | `jerryCan(b, x, y, z, { colour, zone, width, ribX })`; the Hilux's strap and the Perentie's holder stay in their files         |

**Kept as they were.** The two TOYOTA badges are not quite the same: the LC79's A has a crossbar from 0.22 to 0.78 and the Troop Carrier's from 0.2 to 0.8, and the Camel plate's dash is shorter than the others. Each keeps its own letter through the override, so nothing was reshaped. The Troop Carrier's roof can (lying down), the Camel's (upright in a cradle, with three handles) and the Unimog's (in a cage) are different shapes and stay in their files. So do the pixel-grid lettering on the Hilux's and the 40's grilles, the HJ60's TOYOTA and script, the Sooty's dot-matrix plate, the 40's hand-drawn plate in `vehicleEquipment.ts`, and the HJ60's yellow plate, which share no geometry with these. Bull bars, roof racks and snorkels differ in shape and were left alone.

**How it was checked.** Each loop in `strokeText` keeps the arithmetic of the copy it replaced (for example `0.284 - i * 0.098 - x * 0.07` for the LC79 badge, the plate's centred form for the plates), so the floats come out bit for bit the same. A scratch script built all twelve bodies and their wheels and hashed every attribute buffer, the index and the material groups: all 24 hashes are identical before and after. `npx vite-node scripts/tri-count.ts` prints the same table (Hilux body 41,924 triangles, LC79 43,161, Troop Carrier 47,780, Perentie 48,184, Camel 53,140, Unimog 45,038; the rest unchanged too). The studio's eight views (front, side, rear, both three-quarters, top, wheel and cockpit) of the Hilux, LC79, Troop Carrier, Perentie, Camel and Unimog were photographed before and after in headless Chromium on SwiftShader: all 48 PNGs are byte-identical (two runs of the same build were byte-identical too, so the comparison is exact). Since no geometry changed, the comparison with each vehicle's reference photographs stands as recorded in its `SOURCES.md`.

**Lines.** 309 lines out of six body files, 270 in `bodyKit.ts` (most of it the glyph table, which Prettier writes one segment per line), so 39 fewer in all; the point is that a plate, a badge or a can is now changed in one place.

**Tests.** The unit suite passed before merging, on the tree merged with v1.58 (95 files, 795 tests, 475 s). The browser tests were not run: no page or HUD changed.

## v1.60: the MQ's spare clear of the rear door

The owner reported that the MQ Patrol's first spare was sticking through the back door. It was: `doorSpare()` in `src/render/mqGear.ts` placed the shared wheel geometry with no rotation, and the wheel builder draws a road wheel with its axle along x, so the spare stood edge-on with its axle across the car and its tread running about 0.4 m into the door, the load space and out behind. Every other door or carrier spare in the game (the 40's left door in `vehicleEquipment.ts`, the Camel's door in `camelBody.ts`, the Sooty 80's swing-away carrier in `sooty80Body.ts`) turns the wheel a quarter turn about y so the axle points backwards. The carrier was also drawn on the door, with a hinge post running up the door's right edge over the right tail lamp.

**What changed.**

- **The carrier.** The real MQ hardtop's spare rode on a swing-away carrier fixed to the body and bumper, not the door skin, and the Sooty 80's is built that way in the game, so the MQ's is now too: a post on a foot plate at the rear bar's right end (x 0.82, outboard of the right tail lamp), the frame hung on it by two knuckles, an upper arm over the top of the right lamp and a lower brace bent under it (so neither crosses a lens), a backing plate on the frame, and a latch arm down to a pin post on the bar at x −0.2, left of the tow ball. Nothing of it touches the door: its tubes run 70 mm off the skin, past the door's hinges (31 mm) and handle (22 mm). `MQ_SPARE` in `mqGear.ts` exports the hub position.
- **The wheel.** Turned with `rotate(0, -π/2, 0)` as the others are, hub at x 0.22, y 0.56, z `TAIL` + 0.27. Along its axle the wheel's geometry is ±0.149 m (tyre ±0.116, hub caps and the free-wheeling hub's knob beyond `wheelWidth / 2` on both faces), so the inner hub cap sits on the backing plate 0.121 m off the door skin and the inner sidewall is 0.155 m off: room for the game's dents. A centre stud runs from the plate through the hub to a wing nut past the outer cap. Seen square from behind the tyre's edge reaches x 0.61, clear of the right tail lamp (0.675), and its top (y 0.95) sits over the lower half of the rear glass, as a door spare does. The collision box for the spare in `src/data/mq.ts` moved back with it (z 2.22–2.52).
- **The rear door panel.** The close-ups showed a painted triangle across the right of the rear glass, from outside and from the cabin. The door panel was extruded between `TAIL` − 0.05 and `TAIL` with a 30 mm bevel, more than half its 50 mm thickness: Three's `ExtrudeGeometry` then ran the panel to `TAIL` + 0.011, over the shut lines, the badge, the lock and the plate, which were drawn 1–9 mm proud of `TAIL` and so were hidden, and its caps lost the window hole in one triangle. A 20 mm bevel keeps the panel inside its 50 mm and the hole whole, so the glass is clear and the door's shut lines, NISSAN PATROL badge, lock, handle and plate show again. Nothing else on the body moved.

**Compared.** Studio renders before and after (headless Chromium, SwiftShader) from straight behind (`view(0, 0.6, 5, 0, 0.55, 2.1, 30)`), both rear quarters, both sides at the tail (`view(±4.5, 0.55, 2.25, 0.2, 0.55, 2.25, 22)`), low under the rear bar, from above and from inside the cabin looking back, and the standard eight views. Before: the wheel edge-on, cut by the door, with the post over the right lamp and the painted triangle on the glass. After: the wheel faces backwards on its carrier, wholly behind the door, the lamps clear, the glass clear. No photograph of the MQ is committed yet (`SOURCES.md`, Gaps); `rear-34-right-hardtop-1986-spares.jpg` (a door-mounted pair on a Dutch car) was the only reference for a spare at the back, and the bar-mounted carrier is the game's choice, as the Sooty's is. What still disagrees: the door's handle, badge and plate layout is unchecked against a factory photograph.

**Triangles.** The whole MQ is 62.9k (body 46.4k), up from 62.6k: the carrier's tubes and plates.

**Tests.** `tests/mq.test.ts` checks that the hub sits at `TAIL` + 0.27, that the turned wheel is narrow along z (under 0.32 m) and wide across, with its inner face more than 0.1 m behind the door skin, and casts rays forwards over the wheel's disc to check nothing lies in the door panel's core or in the load space for half a metre in front of it. The raycast fails on the v1.59 body.

## v1.61: one way to mount a spare, and a test that checks every one

Two builds in a row shipped a spare wheel wrong and nobody saw it in the renders: the 40 Series ute's under-tray spare hung almost on the ground (v1.53, fixed in v1.55), and the MQ's door spare stood edge-on with its tread through the rear door (v1.55, fixed in v1.60). Sessions do not remember; the repository does. So this update puts the rule into the code, a test and the instructions every session reads.

**One helper.** `mountSpare(b, wheel, hub, facing, colour)` in `src/render/spares.ts` is now the only way a vehicle places a spare. It takes which way the wheel faces (`'back'` on a door or carrier, `'front'`, `'up'` flat on a rack, deck or bonnet, `'down'` slung under the floor, `'left'`/`'right'` against a side), applies the turn itself (the wheel builders draw the axle along x), draws the wheel and records the mount: hub, facing, the wheel's half-width along its axle (hub caps included) and its radius, both from the geometry's bounding box. `takeMountedSpares()` drains the record; `withoutSpareWheels()` builds a body with its spares recorded but not drawn, so a test can ray-cast what is behind them. `spareSidewall()` gives the tyre's half-thickness outside the rim's centre, the height a flat spare rests at. The Troop Carrier's spare cover is drawn through the helper's `cover` option, as part of the spare. Three door spares (the Troop Carrier's, the GQ's, the LC79's) were turned the other way about y; they keep that with `reversed`, which mirrors only the tread's stagger.

**The refactor moved nothing.** With all 25 spares on all thirteen vehicles moved onto the helper and nothing else changed, every body's geometry was byte-for-byte the same (a hash of every attribute of every body) and `scripts/tri-count.ts` printed the same counts.

**The test.** `tests/spares.test.ts` builds every vehicle look as `scripts/tri-count.ts` does and, for each recorded spare, checks: (a) its axle points the recorded way in the drawn geometry (across the face the tread is about one radius from the hub; along the axle the wheel ends within its half-width); (b) over the tyre and the rim's outer half (outside half the radius, where the rim's centre bears on its plate, stud or clamp), lines parallel to the axle find no part of the body inside the wheel or within 3 mm of its inner surface, and the wheel is not inside a closed part (an odd number of body surfaces outside it). On the outer face a strap or cradle bar may press up to 10 mm into the tyre's curved wall, as rubber gives; the face it is mounted against gets no such allowance. (c) Its lowest point, hub caps included, is at least 0.15 m off the ground (one wheel radius below the hubs, as the studio sets it). The test fails on the v1.53 ute spare (moved back to its old height to try) and on the v1.55 MQ spare (mounted edge-on to try).

**What the test found, and what moved.** Eleven vehicles had a spare touching or inside something. Each was a real defect, so the part moved and the test was left as it is:

- **Unimog:** the spare under the tray's front lay through the right chassis rail (its upper half 0.13 m into it). It now lies below the rail, hub at x 0.66, y −0.14, z −0.44 (was 0.64, 0, −0.44; x moved 2 cm out so the tread clears the propshaft), facing down on a new cradle: two bars along the truck under the tyre and two across outside it, 2 mm below the sidewall, hung from the rail by two hangers at z −1.04 and 0.16 with a brace from each outer corner up to the rail, and a spigot through the hub. Its lowest point is 0.32 m off the ground. `rear-34.jpg` shows the spare low under the tray's front, its top below the boxes. The collision box is unchanged (it was already thinner than the drawn tyre, on purpose).
- **Range Rover:** the first spare stood in the load bay inside the rear wheelhouse (0.15 m into it), and its strap ran through the middle of the wheel. It now stands at x −0.39 (was −0.56), facing right (inboard), its inner sidewall 6 mm off the wheelhouse's inner face, the strap down its outer face over the hub.
- **Troop Carrier:** the carrier's cross bar ran 35 mm into the covered door spare. The spare and its cover moved back 4 cm (hub `T` + 0.255) and the cross bar 2 cm forward (`T` + 0.07); the spare faces back, the cover's back clear of the bar and the uprights.
- **LC79:** the door carrier's frame stood 28 mm out from the door skin, into the tyre's inner sidewall; it now lies on the skin (`back` + 0.02). The spare did not move: facing back, hub at y 1.13, z 2.8.
- **MQ:** the toolbox on the rack ran 0.12 m into the second spare's tread; it is shorter and further forward (z 0.68–1.04, was 0.78–1.18).
- **40 Series ute:** the chassis crossmember at z 2.05 ran through the under-tray spare; it is now at z 1.8, ahead of the spare. The spare did not move.
- **Camel:** the corners of the bonnet spare's mounting block were in the tyre; the block is 0.28 m square (was 0.34).
- **Flat spares resting on their tyre walls:** the 40's rack spare on its plate (+12 mm), the ute's deck spare (+7 mm), the Perentie's bonnet spare on its plate (+11 mm), the rack spares on the 60 (+15 mm), the MQ (+15 mm) and the Range Rover (+15 mm), the Troop Carrier's (+3 mm), the GQ's (+4 mm) and the LC79's (+9 mm): each tyre's bulging sidewall sat a few millimetres inside its plate, slats or bars, because the hub was placed half the tyre's width above them and the sidewall bulges wider. Each hub is now `spareSidewall()` + 4 mm above the surface it rests on, and its clamp follows. All face up.
- **60 Series:** the slung spare's lowest hub cap was 0.146 m off the ground; it is 1 cm higher (hub y −0.21), 15 mm under the frame rails, facing down.

The Sooty 80 (back, on its swing-away carrier), the 40's door spare (back), the Camel's door spare (back), the GQ's door spare (back), the MQ's door spare (back), the Hilux's two (down under the tub, up in it), the Perentie's under-floor spare (down), the Unimog's dropside spare (left) and the ute's under-tray spare (down) passed as they were. The Unimog is 88 triangles more (the cradle); every other count is unchanged.

**Compared.** The standard studio set for all thirteen vehicles, and close-ups from behind, the side and low down of each spare that moved (Unimog under the tray from the side, low rear and front; the Range Rover's bay from behind and above; the Troop Carrier's and LC79's door spares from the rear quarter and side-on; the racks of the 40, 60, MQ, GQ, Range Rover, Troop Carrier and LC79 from above; the Perentie's and Camel's bonnets from the side; the ute's deck and its underside). The spares read as before except the Unimog's (lower, on a visible frame) and the Range Rover's (further inboard).

**The second look.** `CLAUDE.md` now asks for a reviewer subagent that sees only the renders. This update was built inside a subagent, which cannot start another, so that review could not be run here; the render paths were handed back for the session that can. Recorded here so it is not mistaken for a review that found nothing.

**The rule, written down.** `CLAUDE.md` ("Modelling a vehicle") now says: render the standard set and close-ups of every part added or moved, read every render, write where each part is attached and which way it faces, mount every spare with `mountSpare()`, render a reported defect close up before replying, and run the reviewer. `reference/vehicles/README.md` ("Close-ups of every part added or moved") has the checklist and the camera positions, and `scripts/studio-closeups.ts` takes named close-ups.

## v1.62: the independent vehicle review completed

The owner asked to finish v1.61's missing second look. Its renders had not survived into this workspace, so all thirteen vehicles were rendered again in Chromium with SwiftShader at 1280 × 720: the eight standard views and rear, side and low close-ups of all 25 spares, plus extra views where the bonnet, load or road wheel obscured a mount. Four reviewer subagents opened all 186 full-size PNGs between them. Each received only image paths and the inspection brief from CLAUDE.md, with no code or prior conclusions. This completes the review that v1.61 explicitly could not run; it does not turn the older unverified real-device checks into passes.

**Confirmed findings and changes.**

- **40 Series ute:** the two 2.2 m recovery boards reached z 2.80, through the tailgate's inner face at 2.77, leaving brown patches on its outside. Both move 0.10 m forward to the centre of the 2.4 m tray (ends at 0.50 and 2.70), still lengthways on the left of the deck with their straps around them. Neither spare moves.
- **Camel:** the rear corner panels behind the lamp stacks stopped at y 0.475, while the upper quarters began at 0.78. Both lower panels now reach the waist, attached to the side skins and facing backwards, closing the holes beside the rear door. The spare remains facing back on its door mount; the bonnet spare remains facing up on its block.
- **60 Series and MQ:** the full-width padded dashboard tops projected ahead of the binnacles, across the middle of the dial faces when viewed from the driver's higher seat. Each pad moves 0.07 m towards the windscreen and its depth becomes 0.12 m, ending behind the cluster. The dials, instrument hoods, eye positions and steering columns stay put.
- **MQ:** the diagonal rack legs passed inside the thick roof shoulder, leaving visibly separated stubs. Each now rises vertically outside the shoulder from its gutter clamp and then turns inwards to the rack rail. The deck and load stay put, with the second spare facing up and the first facing backwards on its rear carrier. The interior mirror gains a short stem joining its upper edge to the headlining; the mirror itself stays put.
- **GQ Patrol:** the roof spare's strap passed through the raised hub cap. The strap and its two ties move 0.16 m rearwards, crossing the wheel beside the cap and still returning to the rack. The spare stays facing up in the same position; its door spare still faces backwards.

**Other observations checked.** The ute's apparently hollow differential ends are dark solid spheres behind bright rings (`VehicleSuspension`), not missing faces. The solid-colour exhaust tip is an existing low-detail approximation, left for a later cosmetic pass. The 40's and 80's door spares obscure portions of their corner lamps from directly behind; their mounting and source limitations are unchanged, and this review does not establish complete lamp visibility. The last reviewer withdrew an initial LC79 tray-intersection suspicion on rechecking the perspective: no definite penetration was visible. Possible hidden mirror mounts in the Troopy and LC79 were not treated as proven defects.

**Compared and retained.** Before renders are in `/workspace/scratch/vehicle-review-v161/`, the revised standard sets and spare close-ups for all five changed vehicles in `/workspace/scratch/vehicle-review-v162/`, with camera recipes and a before/after comparison. No reference photograph was edited or added to the game. Existing 40 Series, 110-family, HJ60 and GQ source photographs were opened beside the renders for silhouette comparison; the corrected tray gear, cabin details and rack mounts are the game's additions or estimates, not newly verified factory details. The MQ still has no committed photographs and the Camel has no period Ninety set, as their SOURCES files explain. Representative files compared: `landcruiser-fj40/front.jpg`, `side.jpg`, `rear.jpg`, `front-34.jpg`, `rear-34-hardtop.jpg`; the 110 family's `front.jpg`, `side-county.jpg`, `rear-34-county.jpg`, `front-34-left-county.jpg`, `rear-34-right-county-1990.jpg`; the HJ60's `front-fj60lg-usa-1981-87.jpg`, `side-left-j60-mod-roof-tent.jpg`, `rear-j60-spare-carrier.jpg`, `front-34-right-j60-1981.jpg`, `rear-34-right-fj60-1986.jpg`; and the GQ's `front-34-1988.jpg`, `side-1992.jpg`, `rear-34-1988.jpg`, `front-34-left-1989.jpg`, `rear-34-left-1994.jpg`. Some reference filenames mislabel their angles; the pictures themselves were used. Overall body dimensions and physics tuning do not change.

**Validation.** The 76 spare checks pass without alteration. The ute's existing board check now requires clearance inside both tray walls; the Camel's model check casts rays at both formerly open rear corners; the 60's and MQ's instrument checks now cast from the actual driver's eye as well as straight in front of the faces. These vehicle tests pass. All five changed vehicles were rendered again for an independent second inspection (70 PNGs). The second reviewers found no further confirmed fit defects: the ute reviewer repeated the dark differential/solid exhaust-tip observations above, and the reviewer of the 60, MQ and GQ reported nothing found. Triangle counts remain below 80,000 for every vehicle; the MQ gains only the rack knees and mirror stem. Lint and build pass. Full unit tests run separately under the repository's publish-first workflow.

## v1.63: transfer cases, hubs and a centre lock

The next sim-audience phase from ROADMAP. L/controller triangle or Y/the phone transfer button cycles 2H → 4H → 4L → 2H on part-time vehicles. Entering or leaving low range requires a stop (under 0.5 m/s); 2H → 4H is allowed through 20 km/h, with locked front hubs. A refused shift gives its reason. Full-time vehicles switch only 4H/4L and have a centre lock in the tool menu, engaged while stopped and releasable on the move.

- **Vehicle groups.** Following the roadmap, the 40, ute, 60, Hilux, Troopy, MQ, GQ and 79 are part-time; the 80, Perentie, Camel, Unimog and Range Rover are full-time. The 79's hubs engage automatically. The others' manual hubs take 20 seconds through the existing roadside-job system, in 2H, stopped and within 30° of level; drive/actions are suppressed while the job runs, with the existing hold brake and progress display. Hubs cannot be worked while another roadside job is active.
- **Drive.** `src/sim/vehicle/transferCase.ts` owns shifting, axle requests and stored strain. 2H sends all requested drive to the rear, including the wheelspin cues, and reduces rolling resistance 3% and fuel use 2%. An open centre caps equal axle requests to the weaker axle's grip; locked retains the existing half-and-half split. All cars and labs start in 4H with hubs and centre locked, preserving the established CREB starting setup. This is a gameplay configuration following the roadmap, not a claim about each factory vehicle's historical transmission.
- **Wind-up.** Turning distance stores strain only with at least three loaded tyres on rock/concrete/timber and the axles coupled. Steering range falls by up to 22%, scrub adds resistance, and scrub gradually contributes to the existing tyre-abrasion damage. A rising strain crosses a 50% warning threshold with a creak, toast and lamp. Selecting 2H or opening the centre releases it; loose ground also unwinds it. Straight or stationary steering cannot add strain.
- **Interface and saves.** The HUD and phone button show 2H/4H/4L, with centre-lock, free-hub and wind-up lamps. Tools includes transfer/hubs/centre as appropriate; the touch menu retains its automatic grid layout. Camp checkpoints save the selected controls keyed to vehicle id; old, invalid or mismatched data uses the default. Strain resets at a camp restart. Existing coarse high/low state remains for gearing, suspension and the headless labs. No vehicle bodies were changed. The scripted driver now stops for low-range shifts and reconnects 4H after leaving low. Driving fixtures set their initial low range directly (with the usual shift delay), and high-range trials keep the recovery pilot from silently selecting low; the scored-run fixture likewise starts explicitly in low.
- **Validation.** Full type-check and 64 focused tests pass, including new shift boundaries, rear-only wheel forces, axle grip with an open/locked centre, timed hub work, wind-up and save round trips. The full unit suite and the new browser check run separately under the repository's publish-first workflow. Real phone/controller checks are in README; the browser suite's older failures remain tracked in ROADMAP.

## v1.64: fix the transfer-case test helpers

The v1.63 high-range fixture adjustment was applied too broadly: two other drive helpers referenced `high`, which exists only in `runLeg`. Remove those two references, retaining the explicit high-range guard where the caller requests a high-range trial. This fixes the full type-check and the stretch/fork helpers without changing driving physics.

The browser check passed transfer cycling, the timed hub job, free-hub status and refusal to engage 4WD, then exhausted its six-minute budget after swapping vehicles under software rendering. Give the combined check ten minutes, use a smaller viewport, and omit the dev panel so it cannot overlap phone controls. Its progress assertion now matches the prompt's text alongside its progress marker. The first full unit run used an earlier source state and its failures led to the fixture corrections in v1.63; final CI runs the corrected source afresh.

## v1.65: fixed-length live-axle articulation

The visible axle beams used to turn between independently positioned hubs with `atan2(height difference, track)`. That shortens the drawn beam's projected span without moving the hubs, so its ends leave the wheels; the tyres also stayed upright while their axle tilted. Springs and shock bottoms used the nearby hub's height even when attached well inboard of it.

**Rigid-beam geometry.** `src/sim/vehicle/solidAxle.ts` derives axle roll from the height difference and fixed hub spacing. The horizontal track contracts as the beam rolls. Both road tyres share that camber, steering turns inside the axle frame, and the wheel spin remains inside the steering pivot. Each axle uses its own track (including the 60's different front and rear tracks); the 40's previously documented drawn/physics track difference is retained. `VehicleModel.poseWheels` supplies the same placement for driving, ghosts, traffic, stranded vehicles and the studio.

**Attached running gear.** The axle tubes and differential housings face across the car between the paired hubs. Coil bottoms, damper bottoms and radius/trailing links now attach to transformed points on that beam; their top mounts stay on the chassis, with visible triangulated shock brackets and upper coil-seat plates connecting to the frame. Leaf packs are separate deforming meshes: their middle follows the axle seat while the main leaf's ends stay on fixed chassis hangers. The axle's U-bolts move with it. Leaf geometry updates only when heave or roll changes, with one mesh per pack pair rather than a draw call per leaf segment. Exterior dimensions, spare mounts and reference photographs are unchanged; the interior fit corrections below make room for the cambered tyres.

**Physics boundary.** This is the first stage of solid-axle articulation on the existing massless raycast suspension, not separate rigid axle bodies with unsprung mass or a new suspension solver. Existing spring rates, low-range droop, anti-roll bars and tyre-force calibration stay in place. The simulation derives the same rigid-beam hub positions and keeps its contact probe on the authored wheel line while that line remains inside the tyre's tread; a larger roll moves the probe back within the tread. Adding a vertical component to Rapier's lateral axle direction or extra spring preload changed the calibrated side-slope/locker behaviour and stopped the Perentie on Big Red, so those changes are excluded. The former regression tests are kept unchanged.

**Studio and review.** `scripts/articulation-shots.ts` captures the standard eight views of every vehicle and front, rear and low side close-ups of a diagonal block pose. Before: `/workspace/scratch/solid-axle-before/`; first inspection: `/workspace/scratch/solid-axle-after/`; mounted suspension: `/workspace/scratch/solid-axle-mounted/`; corrected body clearances: `/workspace/scratch/solid-axle-fitted/` (ute, MQ and Range Rover). Three independent reviewers opened all 143 original first-pass PNGs, given only image paths and the repository’s inspection brief, then inspected the revised mounted sets. They found upper shock rods with no visible chassis brackets and rear tyre intersections with the ute’s tray, the MQ’s inner wheelhouse/trim and the Range Rover’s interior. Brackets and upper spring seats were added. Matching v1.64 poses in `/workspace/scratch/solid-axle-baseline-flex/` show existing clearance defects, with camber changing their overlap. Shortening the ute’s suspension travel stopped its knoll climb, so travel stays unchanged. Instead:

- **Ute:** tray crossmembers near the rear tyres end at 1.0 m width between the chassis runners, still at y 0.29 and their original stations, facing across the car. The deck, perimeter rails and spare mounts do not move.
- **MQ:** the inner rear opening rises to y 0.43, separate from the unchanged outer arch at y 0.36. Its solid trim block becomes a thin inner wall at x ±0.49, and the rear seat plinth narrows to 0.96 m between the wheel boxes.
- **Range Rover:** the load floor and rear seat plinth narrow to 1.0 m between the rear wheelhouses. The supported rear cushion is 3 cm higher and still faces forward; lower side trim starts above the wheelhouse roof at y 0.42.

The moderate studio pose is capped at 16 cm and clears old blocks when posed again; it is an inspection rig, not a physics solver. Reviewers also noted old cabin gaps, uncertain mirror supports and a small damper/leaf-bracket gap; these do not establish new axle defects. A subsequent reviewer still described the ute’s upper tyre as cut off in the low side view. The remaining outboard perimeter rail is at x 0.82–0.90, outside the tyre’s upper sidewall envelope; expanded tyre-volume rays use its measured bulging sidewall width and show clearance. That view’s rail obscures the tyre without penetrating it. Full-travel body clearance remains a separate follow-up. The ute’s cab references (`front.jpg`, `side.jpg`, `rear.jpg`, `front-34.jpg`, `rear-34-left.jpg` in `landcruiser-fj40/`) were opened for the unchanged silhouette. The ute, MQ and Range Rover SOURCES files were read and updated: their missing original photographs remain missing, and these interior clearance fixes are not new factory matches.

**Validation.** Seventeen new regression cases cover fixed hub spacing, beam/tyre alignment, steering and resetting to level for all thirteen vehicles, plus useful load on diagonal rocks, zero support when airborne, and tyre-volume clearance against the ute, MQ and Range Rover body meshes. Focused existing articulation, climbing, rollover, towing, transfer-case and rendering checks pass without loosening their assertions. The full unit suite passed (98 files, 896 tests) on the final suspension/contact code. The three later body-clearance cases and affected vehicle/spare tests passed together (109 tests); real-device checks remain in README. The new studio capture also checks browser page errors.

**Roadmap.** Added the owner's request for more side quests and interesting discoveries off the beaten track: optional recoveries, hidden camps, abandoned equipment, local stories and useful supplies along side tracks and unmarked places.

## v1.66 — Optional jobs and discoveries on the CREB

The owner asked for interesting side missions and attractions instead of the proposed drivetrain follow-ups. This update adds two small jobs and a sightseeing trail alongside the existing CREB mission, not another episode.

**The lost logbook.** Stop at Old line camp to collect a traveller’s log, then leave it in the returns box at Wujal Wujal camp for one full 20 L jerry can. Collection must happen first. The reward is separate from the existing five supply finds, so the Troopy unlock still means collecting those same five finds.

**The last pole.** Pick up a sketch at the linesman’s hut, follow a new roughly 150 m spur past leaning poles to a turning clearing, record the last pole, then bring the sketch back to the survey box for two plugs. The spur is graded between pinned heights, from 125.5 m at the hut to 90 m at the clearing. A first ground-following profile produced a 40° local grade and failed both drive directions; it was replaced with the graded profile rather than weakening the test. The first alignment also crossed the hut and a survey sign; it was routed south of the hut and the sign moved clear of the wheel line. The new track is appended after existing tracks: terrain roughness uses their array indices as seeds, and inserting it earlier changed the chicken track’s washouts. The existing Old line climb is still the way back to the main route. New collidable returns/survey boxes and lettered signs mark the jobs; the clearing has a survey board, pole, fire ring and log seat. These are fictional locations on the shortened map, with no new real-world heritage claims.

**Falls and high country.** Roaring Meg Falls, the range lookout and Wujal Wujal Falls are optional scrapbook stops, in any order. Each adds a short fictional observation, readable again in the journal. The reward is the completed scrapbook, not supplies; the falls are viewed from the existing track ends. Existing history notes remain intact.

**Controls and progress.** Each task needs three uninterrupted seconds below 0.6 m/s total speed, upright within 35° and within 3 m of the ground. Leaving, moving or overturning resets the wait. The HUD shows the task and dwell progress. Pause → Trip journal lists directions, current stages, recorded pages and rewards; it supports controller navigation and scrolls separately from Back on a phone. The main deadline and score calculation stay as they were; the finish card lists completed trips.

**Saving.** Optional checkpoint field `sideTrips` stores completed stage keys. Older checkpoints have no progress. Jobs restore only a valid prefix; sightseeing accepts independent stops. Rewards happen only on the transition into completion. Camp entry can precede the three-second handover, so a task finished inside camp takes another checkpoint including both its reward and completed stage. Restarting restores tasks and inventory from the same camp, putting later jobs back without duplicating rewards. Partial dwell is not saved.

**Validation.** Focused tests cover collection before delivery, continuous stopping, survey out and back, sightseeing in any order, journal pages, checkpoint rollback and old-save compatibility. A headless drive checks the new spur in both directions in wet conditions and verifies each objective has track access. Browser coverage exercises journal layouts, all three jobs, delayed camp handover, reward uniqueness and restart rollback. The five mission/save cases and three new-track cases pass; the existing nine find cases also pass. Lint, full typecheck and production build pass. The full unit run finished with 906 passes and one failure: inserting the new spur had shifted the chicken track’s roughness seed and added a winch recovery. Appending the spur preserves the existing seeds; all nine fork checks and all three new-spur checks pass after that correction. The browser mission/reward/restart test passes, and a final touch-enabled smoke check of both journal layouts and the revised clearing reports no script errors. The car settles at 2.3° of tilt in the turning clearing.

## v1.67: tyre pressures in psi and recovery gear

The owner permanently ruled out steering-wheel and pedal support and asked for the psi/recovery item. That exclusion is now in CLAUDE.md and the roadmap; the hidden sim clutch implementation stays for existing automatic-clutch maths and tests, with no promise to restore a pedal option.

**Pressure.** Tools → Tyres offers 18/24/36 psi. The road and 18 psi endpoints retain the existing road/let-down grip, cut and bead-loss tuning; 24 psi interpolates between them. These are bounded game settings, not vehicle-specific tyre recommendations. Inflation health and puncture faults remain separate: per-wheel readings multiply the selected psi by the air remaining, and changing the setting never repairs a tyre. Letting down 18 psi takes 12 seconds and pumping it back takes the existing 60; smaller changes scale with the difference. Jobs exclude overlapping work and require the vehicle stopped on fairly level ground. Camp checkpoints save the selected psi; older or invalid saves use 36. Restarts restore both tyre damage and the camp’s pressure.

**Snatch block and tree protector.** A double line runs from the front winch to a fixed tree/rock pulley and back to an offset front chassis recovery point. The solver measures both legs against the same 30 m drum, reels that rope at the same 1 m/s, and applies each leg’s tension at its own chassis attachment. Maximum combined pull doubles; vehicle travel per metre of rope halves. Moving anchors and vehicle-to-vehicle pulls use a single line. Rig changes require detaching first, and double-line selection limits fixed anchors to 14.5 m. The renderer draws both cable legs, the block and broad gold webbing around tree anchors; the protector is fitted automatically. No vehicle body or spare geometry was changed.

**Rear recovery and shovel.** Stranded vehicles expose the end facing the winching car: the existing front point or a rear chassis recovery point. Both feed the same rescue completion/reward path. The shovel is a 20-second parked job on shallow mud, black soil or sand. Its relief reduces tyre sink and soft-ground rolling resistance by up to 65% in a 3 m local patch. Spinning wheels use that relief up over about twelve seconds at full spin; leaving or teleporting clears it. It does not edit terrain or water and does not persist through a camp restart.

**Controls.** Tyre choices and recovery setup have small sticky submenus, keeping the main radial menu manageable. Touch and mouse select directly; steering keys or a stick point at an option and Confirm picks it. Back cancels. The compact phone strip and four tyre gauges show psi; flat/leaking warning colours and fault descriptions remain.

**Validation.** Focused checks cover pressure timing and safe refusal, grip interpolation, fault preservation, checkpoint/old-save handling, double-line cable budget and force, rear rescue completion, shovel restrictions and actual sink reduction. Existing tools, damage, transfer-case and model geometry regressions pass. Visual checks at `/workspace/scratch/recovery-double-line.png` and `recovery-tree-protector.png` found that the first block position was inside the trunk; the strap working end is now offset clear of the bark, with a visible sheave and both cable legs. Phone and desktop menu captures are under `/workspace/scratch/recovery-*.png`. Full-suite and browser runs are in progress at commit time. Real-device checks remain in README.

## v1.68: the knoll's scored gate course

The owner asked for the scored gate course. The first one follows the existing knoll rock climb before the Daintree River: eight numbered red/blue gates at 6, 23, 41, 59, 78, 97, 115 and 130 m. The terrain, rock geometry, suspension and tyre tuning are unchanged. All gates are 3.6 m wide, wider than every vehicle's measured footprint plus the flag allowance, with room beyond gate 8 for even the Unimog's tail to clear before the summit.

**A complete attempt.** Gate 1 starts the timer; joining halfway cannot start or save a run. The existing GateCount owns crossings, skipped gates and once-per-flag contacts. Its finish waits for the car's tail, so a last-flag touch still counts. ScoredGates wraps that counter with 1000 points, deducting 25 per flag, 100 per missed gate, 5 per reverse or chassis knock, and 50 per winch attachment. Reverses require half a metre of retreat and rearm after 0.4 m gained; knocks debounce for 0.8 seconds and a held winch counts once. The score floors at zero. Missing a gate still gives a result but cannot set a personal best. A rollover past 75°, a teleport of 5 m in a step, leaving more than 3 m beyond the track edge, a vehicle change or camp restart abandons the run.

**Personal bests.** Rank fewer penalties first, then shorter time. Each vehicle has its own `gates/knoll-rock-climb/<vehicle>` key in the existing validated points/time bests store. Finishing a better valid attempt saves immediately; a partial attempt is never saved or restored. Test mode keeps the session result without writing real progress. Pause shows the best score/time for the current vehicle. The course clock follows simulation time and pauses with the game; the journey's existing deadline and finish score stay as they were.

**Finding and reading it.** The rock climb's existing signs, map feature and route hint name the eight-gate course and its scoring. The existing gate renderer draws the stakes, coloured pennants and number boards, and the map shows their red/blue dots. The live gate HUD appears only during an attempt, wraps to the phone’s width and sits clear of the dials. The finish card lists score, time and every penalty count for 15 seconds; routine discoveries cannot replace a result before it has been read. No vehicle geometry changed.

**Validation.** Pure tests cover strict starts, last-flag tail contacts, once-per-flag penalties, missed-gate best exclusion, reverse/knock/winch accounting, abandonment, score/time ranking and saved per-vehicle bests. Physical drives finish all eight gates in the short 40 and long ute; existing knoll difficulty and legacy gate checks also pass. The debug/test driver steers the real vehicle through the full climb on low range, lockers and precision crawl; it does not inject score or progress. Browser checks cover phone bounds, a physical finish, the result card and reload persistence. The v1.67 full suite finished with 101 files / 915 tests passing. Its full browser run had 36 passes and 10 failures; the instruments check still expected ROAD/TRAIL, instant deflation and percentage-only tyre readings, so it now checks timed psi settings and the puncture reading. Remaining suite results are pending at commit time.

## v1.69: gate-course test fixtures and no phone shift buttons

The owner asked what comes next and removed manual shift buttons from future plans. The roadmap, README and CLAUDE.md now record that exclusion alongside steering-wheel/pedal support. Working mirrors and cockpit head motion remain next.

**The v1.68 results.** The full unit run passed 102 files / 912 tests; `delivery.test.ts` could not load because its flat stand-in copied the CREB map, removed every track, but inherited the new rock-climb gates. The fixture now removes both `gates` and `gateCourse` with the tracks. World generation still rejects gates referencing missing tracks; the assertion was not weakened.

**Browser driver.** The updated psi instruments check passed. The scored-course browser driver reached gate 7 but sat there for over eighteen simulated minutes with both front tyres punctured, a hot engine and half engine health. It has no repair/recovery strategy. This UI/persistence test now explicitly selects damage off while still physically driving the entire climb and computing every gate and score normally. Damage, recovery and score penalties keep their own tests. The init script preserves saved bests across the reload check. Gameplay tuning and scoring are untouched.

**Validation.** Targeted supply-run, score and physical course tests and the phone/browser finish-and-reload check are rerun for this update; results are recorded in the PR. Lint, typecheck and build must pass before publication.

## v1.70: working cockpit mirrors

The door mirrors now reflect the scene behind from the actual driver's eye, including the player's bodywork, nearby traffic and trailers. The existing centre mirrors in the Hilux, 60 Series, MQ, GQ and Range Rover also have reflective glass. The camera's usual look controls move the reflected viewpoint; extra head motion remains a separate roadmap item.

**Rendering and cost.** Dedicated glazing slots identify the connected glass faces after each body's final transforms, including the ute's cab offset and the GQ's width correction. Each face retains its actual outline, including the Hilux's round discs. A Three.js planar reflector supplies a reflected eye, an oblique clipping plane and projective texture coordinates. The capture is cropped to the glass so the whole texture is useful. Only the player's cockpit allocates reflection targets; traffic and stranded vehicles keep the fallback glazing. Capture only front-facing glass in the camera frustum, at most one face per frame, oldest first. Low uses 128-pixel targets at up to 10 Hz per visible face, Medium 256 at 15 Hz, High 512 at 24 Hz, with scenery limited to 180 m. No recursive mirror captures or extra shadow-map updates. Vehicle swaps and quality changes release the targets, materials and extracted geometry. Chase view and the saved Cockpit mirrors Off setting make no capture passes.

**Parts and references.** The exterior glass stays on the existing housings and points rearward, slightly towards the driver. The interior glass sits just behind the existing black housings, facing the driver. The ute had two pairs of mirrors: inherited shortie heads plus its own side-facing pair. Its windscreen now omits the inherited heads and arms. The ute's larger heads turn rearward on their original two hinge-mounted support arms. No spare mount, body dimension or suspension geometry changed. Read the vehicle reference notes and available photographs, then inspected the standard studio views and rear/side/low mirror close-ups for all thirteen vehicles. The independent reviewer opened all thirteen full-resolution composites and found no clear physically impossible or incorrectly attached parts.

**Validation.** Geometry and lifecycle tests cover every vehicle's glass count and placement, the cropped camera, capture budgeting, disabled views and target cleanup. Save tests cover both values and older/invalid settings. The WebGL optical rig (`STUDIO_CHROMIUM=/usr/bin/chromium node scripts/check-mirrors.mjs http://localhost:4174/`) confirms asymmetric red/blue markers behind the eye, no green object behind the glass plane leaking into the reflection, and parallax after moving the eye. In the 256-pixel capture each rear marker covered over 10,000 pixels; moving the eye shifted the red centroid by 44 pixels. The full game cockpit screenshot shows the track and verge reflected. Browser checks exercise cockpit/chase transitions, the off switch and menu/save persistence; suite results are recorded in the PR. Phone frame rate still needs the owner's real-device check.

## v1.71: mirror browser checks on desktop and phone

The mirror browser test reached the gameplay capture but tried to pause the desktop using the touch-only pause button. It now dismisses any reading card and presses Escape, matching the desktop controls. A separate 390-pixel title/settings check switches mirrors off, checks that the control fits, reloads and verifies the saved choice. Gameplay and mirror rendering are unchanged. Lint, typecheck and build are rerun before publication; the browser checks and wider suites continue in the background.

## v1.72: optional cockpit head motion
