- **Lamps (item 6).** Each headlamp is inboard (0.58 m out), with the white side lamp over the amber indicator stacked outboard of it (0.775 m out). The two small lamps were side by side under the headlamp. The convoy lamp stays above the headlamp.
- **Brush guard (item 6).** One loop of tube standing on the bumper beam near its ends and running across above the lamps and grille, with a flat centre post and a cross rail under the lamps, painted with the body as both trucks' are. It was three black hoops on a black beam. The beam is still the bumper, with the towing eyes under it, the plate on its face and the winch behind it.
- The 1990 truck's grille is black; the 1988 truck's is the body's colour. The game follows the 1988 one, as the audit asked.

**The second look.** A reviewer was given 42 pictures (the standard set, six close-ups of the front and the 28 audit pictures) and found nothing broken or impossible. Two of its points were acted on:

- The winch's drum was a black cylinder half sunk into the top of the beam, which read from above as a hole. It is a steel drum with its cable, standing on the beam in two cheek plates, behind the guard's rail and in line with the fairlead in the beam's face.
- The jerry cans' stays crossed in front of the rear reflectors. The reflectors are on the ends of the rear crossmember.

Left for stage 3, all there before this stage: two grey blocks at the foot of the windscreen (its hinges), a slab ahead of each front tyre and a small cone inside each front arch. The guard's centre post hides the grille's badge from straight ahead, as the real one does.

## v1.92: the Perentie, stage 3a (wheel arches and roll bar)

Stage 3 is split in two so each part is finished and shipped. This part is item 1 and two details of item 10. The cab's width, the windscreen, the wheels and the wing intake are stage 3b. Only the Perentie changes.

**Compared with** `side-right-county-1990.jpg`, `rear-34-right-county-1990.jpg` and `front-34-county.jpg`, which are Army GS soft tops (see v1.91).

- **Wheel arches (item 1).** Each opening is flat-topped with radiused corners and sides that splay out to the sill, its top 0.46 m up, 18 cm over the tyre. The rear one is 0.86 m across the flat and 1.04 m at the sill; the front one is 0.80 m and 0.90 m, because the grille's face is only 0.50 m ahead of the axle and the wing has to reach the sill ahead of the wheel. A black moulding (the eyebrow) follows the edge from sill to sill, 28 mm proud. They were half circles 0.51 m in radius under a thick lip. Inside each is a dark box with an inner wall, a roof and a wall across each end.
- **Roll bar (item 10).** A hoop of 60 mm tube standing on the load floor 0.12 m behind the cab, inside the canvas bows and under the canvas, each foot on a plate; a 48 mm brace runs from each top corner back and down to a plate on the tub's capping rail 1.45 m back. Painted with the body.
- **Rear crossmember (item 10).** Painted with the body. It was bare galvanised grey.

**The second look.** A reviewer was given 45 pictures (the standard set, nine close-ups and the 28 audit pictures). What it found, and what was done:

- **With an axle crossed, the raised tyre met the top of the opening.** The first openings were 12 cm over the tyre and a crossed axle lifts a wheel 16 cm. They are 18 cm over it now, as the round ones were, and wider across the flat so the tyre's shoulders are not under the corners.
- **From low behind, the cab's floor and dials showed through the rear arch.** The dark boxes had no ends. Each has a wall across its front and its back.
- **At the front the opening ran to the front panel** (the nose is 0.12 m shorter since v1.91), so the eyebrow's leg ended on the bumper. The front opening is narrower, as above, and the wing reaches the sill ahead of the wheel.
- The eyebrow crossed the wing's intake grille. The grille is a low slot above the eyebrow until stage 3b settles where it belongs.
- The shovel's blade stood above the tailgate's top edge. It is 25 mm lower.
- Not changed: the roll bar's feet are on the load floor 9 cm ahead of the benches' ends, which hide them from behind. A pale strut above each front tyre is the coil spring's tower, drawn by the suspension. The blocks at the foot of the windscreen are for stage 3b.

## v1.93: the Perentie, stage 3b (the cab's width)

The last part of the Perentie's list: item 2 and most of what was left of item 10. Only the Perentie changes.

**Compared with** `side-right-county-1990.jpg`, `side-county.jpg` and `rear-34-right-county-1990.jpg`, cropped at full size at the wing, the cab and a front wheel.

- **Cab width (item 2).** The cab, the windscreen, the door tops and the canvas tilt are 1.58 m wide above the waist (`TILT_HALF` 0.79) on the 1.79 m body. They were 1.81 m, as wide as the body. A flat shelf at the waist runs along the top of each door and along the tub, from the cab's side out to the body's.
- **What stands on the shelf, and how.** The tilt's four bows stand on it 0.735 m out. The rack's four legs drop straight down outside the rolled curtains to feet on it 0.875 m out (they no longer crank inwards). The roll bar's hoop stands on the load floor 0.66 m out, inside the bows, and its two braces land on the shelf 0.76 m out. The rolled curtains hang under the canvas's edges 0.796 m out. The aerial is unchanged on the left wing.
- **Windscreen (item 10).** It leans back 10 cm in its 58 cm, about 10 degrees (it was 7 cm). The canvas's front edge moved back 3 cm to meet its top rail. The two steel cubes at the foot of the glass, which each review took for loose parts, are small painted brackets on the lower rail.
- **Wing intake (item 10).** A grille let into the top of the right wing, towards the scuttle, as `side-right-county-1990.jpg` shows. It was a louvred panel on the left wing's side. Each wing has an amber repeater on its side.
- **Arch mouldings.** Painted with the body, a shade darker so the edge reads; both photographed trucks' are camouflaged with the body. v1.92 had them black.
- **Not done:** the wheels. The model's have eight square holes; the photographs show plain dished discs with a few small round holes.

**The second look.** A reviewer was given 45 pictures (the standard set, nine close-ups and the 28 audit pictures) and found nothing floating, facing the wrong way, below the ground or gaping. Three of its minor points were acted on: the door tops stood 2 cm outside the windscreen's pillars, so the frame is now as wide as the door tops; each canvas bow's leg has a socket on the shelf; the jerry cans' straps are 8 mm narrower, inside the body sides. Left as they are: the spare's cradle is the lowest part behind the rear axle, 0.2 m up; the rear bow shows under the canvas's rear edge because the back is open; a pale strut above each front tyre is the coil spring's tower.

## v1.94: the LC79, stage 1 (the front)

The owner asked for the LC79 next. Before any change the 28 audit pictures were taken and compared with `front-34-single-cab.jpg`, `side-wagon.jpg`, `front-34-wagon.jpg`, `front-34-wagon-2.jpg`, `front-34-double-cab.jpg` and `pre-facelift-single-cab.jpg`. The list is in `docs/AUDIT-2026-11.md`; this stage fixes items 1 to 6. Only the LC79 (`lc79`) changes.

**Two photographs were mislabelled.** `side-wagon.jpg` is the 2024 wagon from in front, nearly square-on, not a profile; it is the best view of the new front. `pre-facelift-single-cab.jpg` is a double cab (four doors) with a winch bar, a snorkel and a light bar. `SOURCES.md` now says what each shows. No square-on profile of any 79 is in the folder.

- **Front wheel openings (item 1).** Flat on top with 0.17 m radiused corners and straight sides that splay out 0.3 m per metre down: the top 0.6 m above the origin as before (0.25 m over the tyre), 1.0 m across where the sides would meet it, 1.32 m at the sill. They were half circles of 0.6 m radius. The flares follow the edge, 0.11 m wide. Inside, a dark liner follows the opening just inside its edge and an inner wall closes the whole opening; body colour showed at the top corners and daylight came through from the far side before.
- **Guards (item 2).** The clip's edges round over 45 mm (were 150 mm), so the shoulders and the front corners are square, as a 70 Series' are.
- **Bonnet and grille (item 3).** A raised centre panel 0.92 m wide and 24 mm high runs from the cowl to the nose; the scoop stands on it. The grille is a black surround 1.48 m by 0.30 m whose top rises across the middle 0.8 m to just under the panel's front edge. Each headlamp (0.21 m lens, was 0.2 m) stands in a plain black pod at ±0.585 m, beside a thick upright; the hexagon mesh is only between the uprights. TOYOTA is 0.36 m across (was 0.56 m), as in `side-wagon.jpg`. The headlamp beams start at the new lamps.
- **Indicators (item 4).** On each guard's front corner, amber on the face and on the side, outboard of the surround; a small amber repeater on each guard ahead of the door. They were on the face only.
- **Winch and plate (item 5).** The winch is inside the bar's pan, as in any winch bar: what shows is its control box on the pan's top behind the face, and the roller fairlead in the face. Its drum and a stack of steel rings stood on the pan in front of the grille. The plate on the bar's face is beside the fairlead (x −0.4 m); it was behind it. The bar's centre hoop is 5 cm lower (0.95 m), with the light bar on it.
- **Cab's back pillar (item 6).** A black louvred vent just behind each door, under the swage line, and a round fuel flap on the left pillar under the vent, as `front-34-single-cab.jpg` shows.

**Where each part is attached and which way it faces.** The flares are on the guards' and sills' edges round each opening; the liner and inner wall are inside the opening. The indicators are set into the guards' front corners, facing forward and out. The repeaters, vents and fuel flap are on the body sides, facing out. The winch's control box sits on the pan's top plate. The front plate is on the pan's face, facing forward.

**The second look.** A reviewer was given 49 pictures (the standard set, the 28 audit pictures and 13 close-ups of the front) and nothing else. What it found, and what was done:

- The rear plate's lower edge was over the right-hand shackle on the rear bar. The plate is at x 0.36 m (was 0.68), clear of it.
- The spare on the canopy's door was 2 cm above the tray's deck and looked pressed onto it. It is 4 cm higher (hub at 1.17 m); its collision box moved with it.
- The cabin's rear-view mirror hung under the roof with nothing holding it. It is on a stem from a foot on the screen frame above the glass.
- The bar's outer hoop ran down in front of the new corner indicators. Each upright stands outboard of the indicator (0.93 to 0.96 m out) and ahead of it.
- Not changed: in the pictures from the rear corners above, the door spare looks wider than the canopy, and from the front quarters the side step's front end and the rear mud flap look against the tyres. In the square-on views the spare is 0.47 m in radius against the canopy's 0.93 m half width, the step ends 0.2 m behind the front tyre and the flap is 0.11 m behind the rear one; perspective puts them together.

Each fix was photographed again and read. `tests/spares.test.ts` passes. 59.9k triangles with wheels and boards (body 39.6k).

## v1.95: the LC79, stage 2 (lamps and badges)

After v1.94 the truck was photographed again from the angle of `front-34-single-cab.jpg` and beside `side-wagon.jpg`. The body now reads as a 70 Series; what still looked unlike the photographs was the lamps. Only the LC79 changes.

- **Headlamps.** The 2024 LED unit as in `side-wagon.jpg`: a dark chrome bowl behind faintly tinted glass, the bright daytime-running ring round its edge with a fainter chrome ring inside it, and a small projector in a chrome ring a little above the middle. The glass was drawn in the lens's white, so each lamp read as a white disc. The ring and the projector are the parts that light with the headlamps.
- **Driving lamps.** Two chrome reflector rings show through each lens, which is a shade greyer than before.
- **Badges.** LAND CRUISER in small chrome capitals on each front guard, behind the indicator and above the flare, reading towards the front on the right and towards the back on the left, so it reads the right way from outside. The real badge is a script; the game's stroke font has capitals only.

**The second look.** A reviewer was given 59 pictures (the standard set, the 28 audit pictures and 23 close-ups, including each lamp, each badge and the parts moved in v1.94) and nothing else. What it found, and what was done:

It is the top of the shock absorber on its bracket, drawn by the shared suspension behind the tyre, as on every coil-sprung vehicle; a real lifted truck shows its shocks there. The coil springs look like stacked rings from low in front (the shared spring, minor). The door spare is 2 cm off the canopy's door, on its carrier plate; the boards overhang the rack at both ends as v1.61 recorded.

Each fix was photographed again and read.

## v1.96: the Telegraph Track, stage 1 (the level, playable end to end)

The owner asked for a new level, "shorter but more challenging" than the CREB. A design written in another session is saved as `docs/PLAN-telegraph.md`, which also records, as built, every number below and every place the engine could not do what the design asked. No vehicle body changed.

**The episode.** Episode 2, `telegraph`, on its own map (`src/data/episodes/telegraph.ts`, `telegraphMap.ts`, `telegraphRoute.ts`, `telegraphWeather.ts`): Bramwell Junction to Gunshot Creek, 2,066 m of route on a 1024 × 2048 m map. Mission: get across Gunshot Creek before dark (3 pm start, dark at 6:15, one hour's grace, `clockRate` 7, so 13 minutes of driving uses 1.5 of the 3.25 hours of light). One camp, at the Dulhunty. Load: 16 L, no jerry cans, one spare, two plugs, at `fuelScale` 12; the scripted clean run below burns about 12 L. Four briefing paragraphs. Notes at every place, with history kept to two sources (Wikipedia on the line, Hema Maps on the crossings), recorded in `docs/HISTORY.md`. Timed sections: Palm Creek and the Chute.

**The obstacles.** Palm Creek (a 31° chute with a rut, 0.5 m of water over sand, a 27–30° clay climb out with a rock step and marked rocks, and an off-camber west exit); Ducie (30 m wide, 1.25 m deep in the middle with a current; the line runs left along the shallow near bank, round an arc and back across a bar to a soft sand bank); the sand (300 m of whoops and a washout for the planks); the Dulhunty (three rock ledges down to a shelf above the falls, holes off its downstream edge, a ledge up and a greasy clay bank, which a mud patch keeps greasy whatever the day); Bertie (70 m up the bed between holes, one between the wheels, and a dogleg out over a rock step); Gunshot (the fork and a 470 m bypass that costs 250 points, and the Chute: a band of rock and a drop of about 2.4 m into the creek, a muddy bed and a clay climb out).

**Everything that assumed one episode.** The episode and map lists and ids; every episode is open from the start (`unlocked` in `src/sim/save/save.ts`); the journey page offers the Army supply run only on the CREB, and the game starts one only there; the vehicle challenges (all set on the CREB) unlock only from CREB runs. Saves, bests, section times and the ghost were already kept per episode. An episode can now set its own numbers for the rolled track conditions (`EpisodeDef.conditions`, `conditionFor`): the Telegraph's creeks stand 0.12 m lower and run slower on a dry day and 0.2 m higher and faster on a wet one, with its own briefing lines. `scoreRun` takes fixed penalties, used for the bypass (`EpisodeDef.bypass`).

**Tyre pressure in sand.** Sand's sink and drag ignored pressure; the design needs 18 psi to matter. Now at 36 psi sand sinks a tyre 1.5 times and drags 1.6 times as much as before, at 18 psi 0.7 times (`sandPressure`). The CREB has no sand, so it does not change.

**A bug found on the way.** A weather timeline with `maxRise: 0` divided zero by zero and set every creek's level to NaN. The Telegraph's timeline (no rain) uses 0.01.

**Driven and passed** (`tests/telegraphDrive.test.ts`, scripted drives with realistic damage): every obstacle at a crawl in low range with both lockers, on the right line, in the short 40 and the LC79 on the dry day at 18 psi; the planks over the washout (and not without them); high range with open diffs stopped at Palm, Ducie, the Dulhunty and Bertie on a just-reopened day; the Chute taken fast fails; straight across Ducie floods the engine; drifting off the Dulhunty's shelf tips the car past 45°; 36 psi does not get through the sand in 90 s where 18 psi does; the Troopy grounds on the Chute's lip; and the whole route end to end in the short 40, no winch, on 12 of its 16 L (about 18 minutes at the autopilot's slow crawl through each obstacle). `tests/telegraph.test.ts` checks the layout: the route joins end to end and level, about 2 km, the first obstacle at 300 m, the creeks several metres down, the drop, Ducie's shallow line, Bertie's holes and their darker water, the bypass and the poles.

**Not done, and why (stage 2).** The engine's wheels are raycast springs, so a wheel that drops into a hole or rut is lifted out by the next ray onto the far edge: Bertie's holes tip a car straight up the middle but do not hang it, Palm's rut does not stop a crooked car, and a crawl off the Chute 20° crooked lands safely. Each is an `it.todo` and a line in the plan. On the dry day, high range gets over the Dulhunty. The Troopy cannot do the Chute (its belly grounds on the lip); other vehicles than the 40, the LC79 and the Troopy were not driven. Gunshot's Staircase and Slot, traffic, a stranded driver and surprises are stage 2.

**Looked at.** Screenshots from the chase camera, the driver's seat and the free camera at Palm Creek and Bertie, and the map from above. Bertie's holes could not be seen from the seat; this map's deep-water colour is darker now, and they show as dark patches, faint and blocky (1 m cells). The other crossings were checked as profiles rather than from the seat. The design's colours differ where the game has one colour for dirt and clay: the junction's road is pale grey like the clay, not red.

**Measured** in this container's software renderer (both maps draw at about 4 fps there, so its frame rate says nothing about the phone): Drive to Ready about 21 s on the Telegraph against about 43 s on the CREB, and about 270,000 triangles a frame against 650,000. The owner's `?bench` on the phone is in the README's checklist.

## v1.97: browser tests for two episodes

Follow-up to v1.96. Two browser tests still expected one episode on the journey page: `welcome:4` and `launch:40` now expect two (the CREB and the Telegraph Track), and `welcome:4` checks the Telegraph Track is open. Both pass. In this container the suite must be pointed at the pre-installed browser (`CHROMIUM_PATH=/opt/pw-browsers/chromium`), as the project pins its own Playwright; without it every test fails to launch. `launch:73`, `launch:147` and `launch:219` fail the same way on v1.95, so they predate the Telegraph Track. No change to the game.

## v1.98: headset play

Steve asked for the game to be playable on the VR lab's headsets (Meta Quest 3 and 3S), in the driver's seat with the headset's own controllers, not in a browser window with a gamepad. This version is the game's side of that; getting it onto the lab's headsets is the VR Lab box's job (below).

- **A WebXR session in the headset's browser.** `src/render/vrMode.ts`. Three.js's own WebXR support draws both eyes; the game's frame loop now runs on the renderer's animation loop (`GameLoop` takes a scheduler), because the page's frames stop while a headset session is on. Every headset frame is drawn: the frame cap is off while the headset is on.
- **The seat.** The camera rides in a rig placed each frame at the vehicle's driver's eye (each vehicle's `cabin.eye`) and turned with the body. Three.js applies the headset's pose under the camera's parent at draw time, so the car's movement and the head's reach the picture in the same frame. The cab is the player's still frame and the world moves outside it, which is the usual arrangement for seated driving in a headset. The screen view's head motion (v1.72) is off in the headset: the player's own head moves. Recentring follows the headset's own (hold the Meta button); the rig also recentres at the start of each session.
- **Controls.** The two hand controllers are read as one pad in the standard layout (`VrMode.virtualPad`), so the input code, the bindings and the menus' pad navigation are unchanged: triggers are the pedals, left stick steers, grips are the tool menu and winch, A B X Y as on a pad, right stick doubles as the D-pad, left stick click pauses, right stick click is the horn. The headset's buttons have their own names in prompts (`controllers.quest`, not offered in Settings). The left controller's menu button is not offered to web pages, so pause is the left stick click; the map has no button (it opens from the pause menu).
- **The dash card.** The page's HUD is not seen inside a headset. `src/render/vrDash.ts` draws what is needed to drive on a 46 × 23 cm card on the dash, beside the dials and turned to the driver: prompts, message cards (the HUD's `onToast`), history notes and lessons (`HistoryCard.words`, also the ones that stop the game, closed with A), the mission and the drive state, and the tool wheel while the left grip is held. It is redrawn only when its text changes. It is drawn over the dash (no depth test), so it is never hidden in a cab that sits closer.
- **Menus leave the headset.** The pause menu, the map and the result card are DOM panels. The headset session ends while one is open, so it shows in the browser window, where the headset's controllers point and click. Closing it is a press, which lets the page start the headset session again at once (`navigator.userActivation`). Drawing the menus inside the headset was the alternative; it means rendering the page's HTML into a picture (fonts, canvases, scrolling) and was left for later if the round trip proves annoying.
- **Graphics.** Low preset while the headset is on (the 3S's chip is a phone's), the user's setting back after. Resizing and the adaptive resolution are skipped while the headset sets the size. The terrain, trees, sky and rain use the eye's world position (the camera's own position is the head's pose relative to the rig while the headset is on). The mirrors reflect from the head's world pose (`GameRenderer.mirrorEye`); Three.js's Reflector already turns WebXR off for its own pass.
- **Testing.** `?vrsim` installs Meta's WebXR emulator (`iwer`, a dev dependency loaded only with that flag) as a pretend Quest 3. `e2e/vr.spec.ts` enters the headset from the button, checks the eye is in the cab, drives with the right trigger and left stick, brakes with the left trigger, opens the tool wheel with the left grip, and checks the pause round trip. `tests/vrPad.test.ts` checks the controller layout.

**Not tested on a real headset.** Everything above ran in the emulator in desktop Chromium. Untested: the frame rate on a Quest 3 and 3S (the 3S is the risk; Low preset, default resolution and foveation), comfort on rough ground (the world tilts round a still cab), whether the Quest browser keeps the transient activation long enough for the automatic return from a menu (the button is there if not), and the headset's controller button numbering (taken from the WebXR `xr-standard` layout).

**Getting it onto the lab's headsets** (not part of this version). The live site works in any Quest browser that can reach GitHub Pages. In the lab, the VR Lab box switches the headset browser off as a non-class app and the school filter may not let `github.io` through. The proposed route: the box serves the built game itself and each headset reaches it at `http://localhost:<port>` over `adb reverse`, which is a secure address for WebXR and needs no internet. Recorded in the VR Lab Fleet Manager's worklog.

## v1.99: the Telegraph Track, stage 2 (Gunshot's three lines, traffic)

Stage 2 of `docs/PLAN-telegraph.md`, which records as built every number below.

**Gunshot's other two lines.** From the top of the approach, beside the Chute: **the Staircase** to the east, 4 m down the bank over two 0.45 m rock ledges (ridge stones with their risers on the creek side), leaning 8° towards the creek and rutted; and **the Slot** to the west, a 2.8 m rut between steep clay walls twisting down the bank, with a hole under the left wheels and a hump under the right at the same spot, so the car twists. Both cross the creek to the bed below the Chute and its climb out. Signs at the top name all three; the Gunshot note and the briefing describe them. Both are about 30° on average, gentler than the design's 45° and 40°: the 1 m ground spreads a 4 m drop over about 7 m. Their steepest metres are past 40°.

**On the way.** A Troopy comes the other way on the sand between Palm Creek and Ducie. A HiLux is bogged beside the track before Bertie; winching it out pays one tyre plug (the design wanted a small reward). One dead tree a run comes down across the track near Ducie or Bertie, where the run's seed picks. No rockfalls: the plain has no cutting banks.

**Driven and passed** (`tests/telegraphDrive.test.ts`): the Staircase and the Slot at a crawl with both lockers in the short 40 and the LC79 on the dry day, and in the Troop Carrier, which cannot do the Chute; both stop in high range on a just-reopened day; every stage 1 drive still passes. `tests/telegraph.test.ts` checks both lines join the approach and the bed level, drop steeply, and have their ledges, lean, hole and hump; that the traffic, the stranded driver (one plug, nothing else) and one tree a run are set, the tree 22 to 60 m from a creek's water.

**Looked at.** The Staircase from the chase camera and the driver's seat at its top, both lines from the free camera across the creek, and the tree-fall stretches from above. The stretch by Ducie is open woodland: the plan's gallery forest along the creeks is not dense in the game, so a car can drive round the fallen tree. Recorded in the plan for stage 3.

**Not done.** The three engine limits from v1.96 are still open (`it.todo`): Bertie's holes do not hang a car, Palm Creek's rut does not stop a crooked one, and a crawl off the Chute crooked lands safely. They are listed for stage 3 with the play test's findings. Not driven: the traffic and the stranded driver on the Telegraph (they use the CREB's tested code); the fallen tree with the winch.

## v2.00: the Telegraph Track, stage 3 (the gauging station, the creek forest)

Stage 3 of `docs/PLAN-telegraph.md`. The owner asked for it before playing v1.99, so there was no play test to act on; the polish here is what earlier stages had noted.

**The old gauging station.** A signed faint track west off the main line just before the Dulhunty camp, downstream past the falls, over two rock ledges and boulders beside them, to a hut, a water tank and a gauge board on the south bank, with a full jerry can on a crate (a find: stop beside it to load it). A jerry can is worth the detour on a run that starts with none. No source was found for a gauging station on the Dulhunty, so the station is the game's own and its note states no history (`docs/HISTORY.md`).

**The creek forest.** The woodland circles reached past their edge over the creeks, so the gallery forest the plan asked for was open woodland. They now stop 25 m short of the water, and a new `VegetationDef.rainforestScale` (3 here, 1 by default, so the CREB is unchanged) makes the strip thick: 369 trees within 25 m of a creek, from 107. The map has about 3,000 trees in all.

**Driven and passed:** the gauging track out and back at a crawl in the 40 and the LC79 (it is easy in high range: a time cost, not a hard line); every Telegraph drive from stages 1 and 2. `tests/telegraph.test.ts` checks the side trip's junction, find, note, ledges and sign, and that trees stand more than 1.5 times as thick within 25 m of a creek as 80–200 m from one.

**Looked at:** the station from the free camera and from the track (the hut, the tank and the can on its crate, with its prompt), the rocks beside the falls, and the forest at the Ducie tree-fall stretch, which is thicker but still has gaps a car could weave through.

**Not solved:** the three engine limits (Bertie's holes, Palm Creek's rut, the crooked Chute) need wheels that a hole's edge can hold. That is a change to the vehicle model, now an item in `docs/ROADMAP.md`, not a change to this map.

## v2.01: wheels held in potholes

The owner asked to try the smaller of two ways to let a hole hold a wheel: look ahead of each tyre for ground it could not climb, and push back as if against a wall (`src/sim/vehicle/wheelContacts.ts`, `Vehicle.meetWalls`).

**How it works.** The suspension is still a downward ray per footprint sample, unchanged. At a crawl (under 3 m/s) with at least two wheels on the ground, extra rays from 1.2 m above each wheel look at the tyre's leading and trailing edges. Ground there stands as a wall when it is more than 1.5 radii above the tyre's bottom, or, for a tyre hanging free, more than 0.3 radii above it _and_ more than 2 radii above the ground under the hub (a hole's floor). A wall takes away the corner's velocity into it, along the tyre's rolling direction; a tyre against a wall ahead or behind is also walled at its sides, so it cannot slide round the edge. The car can still back out or be winched.

**What it took.** The first version (any ground ahead 1.5 radii above the tyre) broke 21 tests: over logs and ledges a wheel lifts briefly and the log ahead read as a wall. Requiring the floor under the hub to be far below the edge separated holes from logs, but Bertie's holes were smooth bowls whose floor rises to meet the edge, which reads like a log. So **Bertie's holes are now sheer-sided potholes** (two new options on erosion ruts, `wall` and `ramp`), with a flat floor right to a 0.2 m wall. A sideways wall from a boulder beside a tyre on Granite Spur's chute tipped it 0.1° past its limit; side walls now count only with a wall ahead or behind.

**Driven and passed.** Straight up the middle of Bertie, the short 40 and the LC79 now stop at the first pothole (the test that was an `it.todo`); the weaving line still passes in both; the full suite passes (1,039 tests), including the CREB's rock climbs, Roaring Meg's ledge, the logs, Granite Spur and the crawl courses. Palm Creek's rut (0.5 m) is too shallow to tell from a log, so a crooked car still gets out of it, and the crooked Chute is a landing, not a wall: both stay `it.todo`.

**Looked at.** Bertie from above and from the driver's seat: the potholes show as before, as dark water; their sheer sides are under it.

## v2.02: the headset game at its own address

The owner reported that the **Drive in VR** button (v1.98) sat over the controls, and said it does not belong on the screen: a setting at most, or better nowhere, with the headset game a build of its own.

- **The button is gone.** The ordinary address never offers a headset, in any browser, so nothing for one is on its screen or in its settings.
- **The headset game is its own address:** `/vr` (a small page, `public/vr.html`, that leads to `/?vr` and keeps any other options in the address). `VR_PAGE` in `src/core/buildInfo.ts` is the one switch. It is the same script as the ordinary game, not a second bundle: a separate bundle would be a second copy of everything to download and keep in step, and would differ only in this switch. The address is what makes it a separate thing to open, bookmark and hand to the VR Lab box.
- **Getting into the headset without a button.** A browser starts a headset only on a press. On the headset page the **Drive** press on the briefing is that press (`Game.start`, if the browser still counts it). When the headset is off with the drive running and no menu open (after Continue, whose press was too long ago; after leaving the headset view; if the return from a menu was refused), a cover over the whole window, a card reading **Over the Cape VR**, takes the press. The headset page is not meant to be played in the window, so covering it is right there. In a browser with no headset the card says so.
- **The service worker** serves `/vr` from its stored copy of `vr.html` (Workbox's clean addresses) and `/?vr` as the game's page with the option intact, so the headset address works offline like the ordinary one.

**Tested** in the emulator (`e2e/vr.spec.ts`): the headset page's cover starts the headset and the rest of the v1.98 run follows; the ordinary page with a headset present has no cover or button. **Not tested on a real headset:** whether the Quest browser still counts the briefing's Drive press when the game starts (the cover is there if not).

## v2.03: back into the headset at the click

A follow-up to v2.02 from its browser test. Resuming from the pause menu with a click did not go back into the headset: the cover showed instead.

- **Cause.** The game asked for the headset at the next frame after the menu closed. A browser counts a press for about five seconds. The test machine's software renderer takes 2.5 to 6 seconds a frame (measured), so the press had lapsed by then and the game gave up on the return. A headset draws far faster than that, so it would rarely happen there, but nothing should depend on it.
- **Fix.** The headset page now asks at the click itself, after the menu's own handler has closed it (`window` click listener in `addVrPrompt`), as well as each frame. A menu closed from a controller button still returns at the next frame.
- **The test.** `e2e/vr.spec.ts` resumes with the Resume button (a click, as a headset controller's trigger on the window is), then leaves the headset view with no menu open (`leaveHeadset` on the test hook, standing in for the headset's own quit), checks the cover shows, and takes the seat again from it. The old step closed the menu with Escape, which a browser does not count as a press; it passed before only when one of the test's own calls happened to land first (Playwright's page calls count as presses).

**Tested:** the headset drive test passes with the fix; a run that logged the browser's calls showed the headset asked for at the click and granted. Unit tests: 1,039 passed on v2.02 (the local run also reported a worker timeout while the browser tests shared the machine; no test failed).

## v2.04: the Perentie's last details, and the GQ Patrol, stage 1

The owner asked whether the Perentie and the LC79 were finished and, if so, for the GQ next. Both were photographed again (the 28 audit pictures each) and put beside their photographs. The LC79 showed nothing to fix. The Perentie had the one item v1.93 left open, its wheels, and two details nobody had listed. Those are fixed here, and the GQ's first comparison follows. The Perentie (`perentie`) and the GQ Patrol (`patrol`) change.

### The Perentie

**Compared with** `front-34-county.jpg` (the 1988 truck from its right rear) and `side-right-county-1990.jpg`, cropped at full size at the rear wheel and the body side behind it.

- **Wheels (the last of item 10).** A plain disc dished in two steps, with no holes, five studs and the hub. Eight square holes were drawn round it.
- **Lockers.** A stowage locker door with round ends, 0.32 m by 0.26 m, on each body side behind the rear wheel, 9 mm proud of the panel, with its hinge along the bottom edge and a catch at the top. The 1990 truck has another ahead of its right rear wheel; the 1988 truck, which the game follows, does not.
- **Mud flaps.** One behind each rear wheel, 0.26 m wide and 0.32 m long, hung from a strip bolted under the body side's edge, its foot 0.25 m off the ground.

**Where each part is attached and which way it faces.** Each locker door lies on the body side between the wheel arch's moulding and the tail, facing out. Each mud flap hangs from its strip under the body side, 16 cm behind the tyre, facing the tyre.

### The GQ Patrol

Before any change the 28 audit pictures were taken and compared with `rear-34-1988.jpg`, `rear-34-left-1994.jpg`, `side-right-1989.jpg`, `front-34-1988.jpg`, `front-34-left-1989.jpg` and `side-1992.jpg`. The list is in `docs/AUDIT-2026-11.md`; this stage fixes items 1 to 6.

**A photograph was mislabelled.** `side-1992.jpg` is a front three-quarter view, not a profile, and both it and `front-34-1988.jpg` were captioned with rectangular headlamps; they are round lamps in square recesses. `SOURCES.md` now says what each shows. No profile of a GQ is in the folder.

- **Rear doors (item 1).** They were the mirror image of the real ones. The doors now meet 0.16 m left of the centre line (`DOOR_SPLIT` −0.16, was +0.16): the narrow door is on the left with the handle, a black moulding against the split, and the plate under it; the wide door is on the right with the spare.
- **Door spare (item 2).** Its hub is 0.27 m right of the centre line and 0.6 m above the origin (was 0.27 m left and 0.5 m), so it stands over the foot of the right-hand window, as on all three photographed cars, and 8 cm clear of the rear bar. Its collision box moved with it.
- **Winch and driving lamps (item 3).** The winch is inside the bar's pan: what shows is its control box on the pan's top and the fairlead in the face. Its drum, drawn as a stack of rings, stood on the pan in front of the grille. The centre hoop is 0.92 m wide (was 0.84 m), and the two driving lamps stand on the pan inside it 0.32 m out (was 0.19 m), one each side of the NISSAN letters, which they covered. The high-beam lights start at the new lamps; they were set 16 cm above the lamps.
- **Snorkel (item 4).** A flat moulded body 0.44 m long lies along the left guard's side under the bonnet's edge, and the tube climbs from its back end up the windscreen pillar to the ram head above the roof, held to the pillar by two stays. It left the guard low down and ran to the roof as one diagonal.
- **Quarter vents (item 5).** A black louvred vent on each D pillar at the rear side window's lower back corner.
- **Mud flaps (item 6).** One behind each rear wheel, hung from a strip under the body's edge.

**Where each part is attached and which way it faces.** The spare is on its carrier plate on the wide right-hand door, its face to the rear, placed with `mountSpare()`; `tests/spares.test.ts` passes. The handle and the plate are on the narrow left-hand door's face, facing back. The winch's control box sits on the pan's top between the hoop's legs. Each driving lamp stands on a stem on the pan's top, facing forward. The snorkel's body is against the left guard's side; its tube is tied to the pillar. Each vent is set on the D pillar's side, facing out. Each mud flap hangs under the body's edge 4 cm behind the wheel arch.

**Not done (items 7 to 9).** The two colours meet at the body-side moulding; on the photographed two-colour cars they meet just under the door handles. The body sides have no crease, and there are no PATROL 4x4 badges. The snorkel is on the left guard; the photographs are stock cars with no snorkel, so they do not say which side a GQ's goes, and it was not moved from memory.

### The second look

A reviewer was given 57 pictures and nothing else: the standard set of each vehicle, five close-ups of the Perentie, eight of the GQ and the GQ's 28 audit pictures. It found nothing floating, facing the wrong way, gaping or below the ground. What it did find, and what was done:

- **GQ: the tailpipe ended 4 cm in front of the left rear tyre's tread** (there before this version). It now turns out and ends inboard of the tyre, 11 cm ahead of it.
- **GQ: the left door mirror was hard against the snorkel's tube**, 2 cm apart. The tube climbs 3 cm further forward, 5 cm clear of the mirror's head.
- **GQ: with an axle crossed, the rear mud flap's foot met the block under the wheel.** The flaps are 0.28 m long (were 0.34 m), 0.23 m off the ground. The Perentie's were shortened the same way, to 0.32 m.
- **GQ: the rear door's handle was 5 cm from the spare's tyre.** The spare is 2 cm further right, 7 cm from the handle and 5 cm from the right-hand tail lamp.
- **GQ: nothing visible holds the door spare.** Not changed: its carrier plate is behind the hub, between the wheel and the door, as on the real car, and the tyre stands 3 cm off the door's skin.
- **Perentie: the spare's cradle is the lowest thing at the tail.** Not changed: 0.2 m off the ground, as the real one is (recorded since v1.90).
- **Perentie: the cans' stays cross the tail lamps.** Not changed: the two stays are either side of the lamp (4 cm outboard of it and 13 cm inboard) and stand up to 0.2 m off the panel, so from an angle it passes in front of the lens; from straight behind both lamps are clear.
- **Perentie: the tailpipe runs close beside the spare under the floor.** Not changed: it passes 7 cm from the tyre and 4 cm from the cradle's front hanger.
- The reviewer also noted that the GQ's snorkel is on the left with the steering wheel on the right (item 9 above, open), and that a pale post shows above each front tyre on both vehicles: the top of the coil spring's tower or the shock absorber, drawn by the shared suspension.

Each fix was photographed again and read. `tests/spares.test.ts` passes.

## v2.05: the GQ Patrol, stage 2 (the snorkel's side, the two colours, badges)

The owner asked for the snorkel on the right and for the GQ to be finished: items 7 to 9 of its list in `docs/AUDIT-2026-11.md`. Only the GQ Patrol (`patrol`) changes.

**Compared with** `front-34-left-1989.jpg` and `side-right-1989.jpg` (the two-colour 1989 ST, blue over silver), `side-1992.jpg` (the guard badge) and `rear-34-1988.jpg` (the rear door badge).

- **Snorkel (item 9).** On the right-hand guard, the driver's side. This was the owner's call: the photographs are stock cars with no snorkel. It is the v2.04 snorkel mirrored: the flat body along the guard's side under the bonnet's edge, the tube up the windscreen pillar, two stays to the pillar and the ram head above the roof. The intake the simulation floods the engine at (`intake` in `src/data/patrol.ts`) moved with it, 0.9 m right of the centre line instead of left, at the same height. From the driver's seat the pillar hides it.
- **The two colours (item 7).** They meet 0.57 m above the origin: 4.5 cm under the door handles and 2.5 cm over the flares, on one line along the guards, the doors, the rear quarters and across the back doors. They met at the body-side moulding, 0.27 m lower. The lower colour is silver-grey (`#9A9FA1`); it was charcoal, which at this height made half the car dark and hid the black flares. The 1989 car is silver below. The moulding stays where it was, now on the silver.
- **How the line is drawn.** Each panel that crosses the line (the front clip, the side skins, the rear face) is built once and drawn twice, cut along the line, each half in its own colour (`cutAtSplit` in `src/render/patrolBody.ts`). Splitting the panel into two separately rounded pieces, as the old line at the moulding was, would have cut a deep groove along each guard, because the clip's edges are rounded 11 cm.
- **Crease (item 8).** A narrow bead along each body side on the line, from the nose to the tail, a shade darker than the lower colour. The sides already swell out most at this height.
- **Badges (item 8).** PATROL 4X4 in dark stroke capitals on the left guard above the flare, and on the narrow rear door above the handle. The real badge is a decal in outlined letters. The right guard has none: the snorkel's body lies there.
- **Fuel flap.** In the lower colour and 7.5 cm lower, wholly under the line, as on `side-right-1989.jpg`.

**Where each part is attached and which way it faces.** The snorkel's body is against the right guard's side; its tube is tied to the right windscreen pillar by two stays; its head faces forward. The guard badge lies on the left guard's side, facing out, reading from the front of the car to the back. The door badge lies on the narrow left-hand rear door's face, facing back. The crease beads lie on the body sides.

**The second look.** A reviewer was given 46 pictures and nothing else: the standard set, the 28 audit pictures and ten close-ups (the snorkel from the side, the front, behind and low down, the left guard, the rear, the door badge, the rear quarter, the fuel flap and a front corner). It found nothing floating, facing the wrong way, gaping or below the ground. What it did find, and what was done:

- **Daylight between the snorkel's head and its tube** from behind. The tube runs 5 cm further up, into the head.
- **The snorkel's head 2.5 cm from the end of the rack's wind deflector.** The deflector is 1.5 m wide (was 1.6 m), 7.5 cm clear of the head. The gap was the same on the left before the snorkel moved.
- **The crease bead bridged the gap between the guard and the door.** It is laid in four lengths that stop at each shut line, and stands 3 mm proud (was 5 mm), so its front end no longer reads as a small block on the guard.
- **Crumpled facets at the top back corner of each front guard** (there before this version). The clip's corner there was rounded 2 cm in profile, less than the 6.6 cm its edges are drawn in by, so it folded over itself. It is sharp in profile now, and the corner is clean. The cowl panel under the windscreen is 1.56 m wide (was 1.72 m): its ends stood out through the guards' rounded shoulders.
- **A slit to see through between each wheel's disc and its rim** (there before). A ring fills the rim's well.
- **The fuel flap's outline was uneven and its corner looked against the flare.** The flap stands 2 mm proud of its outline (was 5 mm) and is 4 cm further back, 6 cm from the flare.
- **The jerry can on the rack stood on one crossbar** (there before). It stands on a steel tray across three crossbars, strapped as before.
- Not changed: a short post above each front tyre is the top of the shock absorber, drawn by the shared suspension; the gap between each guard and front door is wide because the guard's back edge is rounded; faint dotted seams inside the rear arches and along the guard tops; with an axle crossed the mud flap is 7 cm above the block and the raised tyre is close under the flare. A small dark block under each guard's front lower edge is the end of the bull bar's wing, which wraps round the guard's corner.

Each fix was photographed again and read. `tests/spares.test.ts` passes.

77.5k triangles with wheels and boards (body 56.6k), up from 71.6k: the panels cut along the two-colour line, the badges and the rim rings.

## v2.06: nothing locked

The owner asked for nothing in the game to be locked. Two things still were: eleven of the fifteen vehicles, each opened by a challenge since v1.06, and the **Conditions** choice (time of day and weather) on the journey page, shown only once the episode had been finished. Both episodes were already open (v1.96).

- **Vehicles.** The challenge system is removed rather than hidden: `src/data/unlocks.ts` and its test are gone, the save no longer keeps an `unlocked` list (an old save's list is ignored when it loads), and the game no longer checks the challenges or shows "Vehicle unlocked" cards. The journey page lists every vehicle as a choice with no "To unlock" list, and Pause → Vehicle has no locked buttons. The saved vehicle is always kept at launch (a locked one used to fall back to the 40 Series).
- **The Army supply run** stays as a Start option on the CREB. Delivering both drums used to open the Perentie; it now shows a **Supply run · Drums delivered** card once per run. `supplyRun` moved to `src/sim/supplyRun.ts`.
- **Conditions** are always offered under Vehicle & difficulty, and a chosen time and weather is used whether or not the episode has been finished.
- The other challenges (all five finds, the section times, winching out the Patrol and the LandCruiser, finishing without the winch) still happen in the game; they just open nothing. The finds and the stranded drivers keep their own toasts and rewards.
- The episode list's "Finish episode N to unlock" path, never shown since v1.96, is removed with `unlocked()` in the save code.

## v2.07: phone settings first, and the three failing title-screen tests

The owner asked for the three failing tests in `e2e/launch.spec.ts` to be fixed. They had been failing since before v2.06. Two were out-of-date tests; the third found a real fault.

- **Phone settings out of sight (a game fix).** On a sideways phone (844 × 390), **Phone driving** and **Joystick side** were below the bottom of the title Settings page, pushed down when v1.70 and v1.72 added Cockpit mirrors and Cockpit head motion above them. On a touch screen they now come first, above Graphics, and are first in the order a controller or the arrow keys move through. Other screens don't show them, as before.
- **The pretend controller had no sticks.** The controller test's fake gamepad reported buttons but no `axes`. The menus read the left stick every frame, so the fake made that code throw before it handled the D-pad. A real controller always reports its sticks; the fake now does too (`axes: [0, 0, 0, 0]`).
- **Phone settings checked on a computer.** The Settings persistence test looked for Phone driving on a desktop page, where it has deliberately been hidden since v1.84 made the phone settings touch-only. It now checks they are absent there. The phone test checks that a Phone driving choice is saved and comes back after a reload. It also looks for each phone setting by its heading, because there are two sections with the class it used.

In a cloud session the project's Playwright can't find its own browser; run the browser tests with `CHROMIUM_PATH=/opt/pw-browsers/chromium`.

## v2.08: the Troop Carrier, stage 1 (the cab and roof)

The owner asked for the Troop Carrier next. Before any change the 28 audit pictures were taken and compared with `side.jpg`, `front-34.jpg`, `front-34-b.jpg` and `rear-34-ambulance.jpg`. The list is in `docs/AUDIT-2026-11.md`; this stage fixes items 1 to 8, which are the body's shape. Only the Troop Carrier (`troopy`) changes; the LC79, which shares its cab's parts, is unchanged.

**What was wrong.** The model was a tall box with an upright windscreen to the full height of the roof, a high flat bonnet and three windows in a row at one height: it read as a Land Rover station wagon. On `side.jpg` the screen leans back 37 degrees to a header 0.23 m below the roof, the roof stands up behind the header, the bonnet slopes down to a low nose, and the door's glass is lower and larger than the two windows behind it.

**How it was measured.** `side.jpg` is nearly square on but tilted 1.6 degrees, so heights were taken from the line through its two hubs, at 238 px a metre (its 2.98 m wheelbase), and set 1 cm higher for the game's springs. The table of each height and station, before and after, is in the vehicle's `SOURCES.md`.

**A photograph was mislabelled.** `rear-34-ambulance.jpg` is a straight-on rear view with both doors open, not a three-quarter. `SOURCES.md` now says so.

- **Windscreen (item 1).** From the cowl 0.79 m above the origin and 0.70 m behind the front axle to a header at 1.31 m, 1.09 m behind the axle: 37 degrees back. It stood 12 degrees back, from 0.84 m to 1.48 m.
- **Roof (item 2).** From the header it stands up 12 cm and then slopes up to the full height (1.54 m, unchanged) 0.3 m behind the driver's eye line, as a shell across the car (`BROW`). A gutter runs over each door at the header's height and along the raised roof behind it.
- **Bonnet and nose (items 3 and 5).** The nose's skin is 0.62 m ahead of the front axle (`F` −2.11, was −2.2) and its top 0.61 m up (was 0.745); the bonnet rises to the cowl at 0.785. The grille, the lamps and the corner indicators are 10 cm lower (centre 0.46 m), the bull bar 9 cm further back with its hoop 12 cm lower, and the headlamp beams, the winch's rope (`winchMount` z −2.33) and the bar's collision box moved with them. The centre rail across the bar's hoop is gone: at the new height it crossed the grille's letters.
- **Doors and windows (item 4).** The door runs from 0.64 to 1.75 m behind the front axle (it was 0.54 to 1.57) and stops at the header; its glass is from 0.805 to 1.25 m up. The sliding window and the fixed window behind it are from 0.97 to 1.43 m up, 0.89 m and 0.85 m long, with 6 cm corners.
- **The cab inside (item 6).** The Troop Carrier has its own cab layout (`troopyCabin`, and `TROOPY_CAB_FIT` for the parts): the dash 0.15 m lower and 0.2 m further back, behind the foot of the leaning screen; the seats and gear levers 0.12 m lower; the driver's eye 0.13 m lower, 0.12 m under the header; the steering wheel, dials, warning lamps and handbrake moved with the dash and seat; the door trims fit the new door; the visors are under the header; the headlining starts behind the brow. The LC79 keeps the cab as first drawn (`seventyCabin`), which `tests/lc79.test.ts` now checks against.
- **Winch (item 7).** Inside the bar's pan, as on the LC79 and the GQ: its control box shows on the pan's top and the fairlead in the face.
- **Roof rack (item 8).** On the flat of the raised roof only: 2.0 m long (was 3.1 m), from 0.4 m behind the centre to the tail, on seven feet a side. The boards (centred on it, 0.19 m over each end), the jerry can (now ahead of the spare) and the spare are on it; its collision box is shorter to match.
- The cowl panel under the screen is 1.48 m wide (was 1.76 m): its ends stood out through the guards' rounded shoulders. The front clip's top corners no longer fold over themselves (the fault found on the GQ in v2.05).

**Where each part is attached and which way it faces.** The rack stands on fourteen feet on the edges of the raised roof. The roof spare lies flat on the rack's two bearers, face up, under two straps, placed with `mountSpare()`; the door spare is unchanged. The jerry can lies on the rack's crossbars ahead of the spare. The winch's control box sits on the pan's top between the hoop's legs.

**Not done (items 9 to 13).** The grille's bars, the wheel openings' black lips and height, the rear quarter's vent and filler flaps, the tail lamps, stripes, mud flaps and steps. No photograph shows which rear door carries the spare, so it was not moved.

**The second look.** A reviewer was given 47 pictures and nothing else: the standard set, the 28 audit pictures and eleven close-ups (the cab from each side, the roof's brow from above, the side and behind, the rack from the front, the side and below, the nose from above and below, and the side windows). It found nothing facing the wrong way or below the ground. What it did find, and what was done:

- **A wall stood up across the back of the bonnet, and its ends stuck out past the guards.** It was the cab's footwell bulkhead and the dash's padded top, which the lower cowl and shorter scuttle had left outside. The bulkhead is behind the clip's back edge and under the cowl; the dash is 6 cm further back, behind the foot of the glass.
- **Ground showed between the cowl panel's end and the pillar's foot.** Each end of the cowl has a rounded block that closes the corner between the guard's shoulder and the pillar.
- **A dark slot between each guard and its door.** The side skin runs forward over the clip's rounded back edge, up to its shoulder line.
- **The cab's rear-view mirror hung in the air.** It is on a stem from a foot on the header.
- **The oil gauge hung off the edge of the instrument housing** (there before, and in the LC79). The housing is 6 cm wider, in both.
- **The rear wheel wells showed body colour inside** (there before). The load space's wheel boxes stood 3 cm outboard of the dark wheelhouse wall; they are 5 cm further in.
- **The aerial's base sat on the bonnet's shut line.** It is on the guard, 6 cm further out.
- **The boards overhung the back of the rack by 0.4 m.** They are centred on the rack and overhang each end by 0.19 m.
- Not changed: a pointed post over each front tyre and a small one in each rear arch are the tops of the shock absorbers, drawn by the shared suspension. The exhaust seems to start in the air behind the right rear wheel: from the side its front length is behind the chassis rail. The roof spare is 5 cm from each board. The tyres stand inboard of the body sides, as a 75's narrow track does. The door spare covers a corner of the right tail lamp and most of the right door's window from straight behind; the rear is the next stage (item 12).

Each fix was photographed again and read. `tests/spares.test.ts` passes.

## v2.09: the Hilux single cab, rebuilt against its photographs

The owner asked for another vehicle that had not yet been brought up to its photographs. The Hilux single cab was chosen: it is one of the game's two original vehicles, its folder has nine photographs, and it had never been compared with them picture by picture. Before any change the 28 audit pictures were taken and compared with `front-34-left.jpg`, `front-34-right.jpg`, `rear-34-right.jpg`, `side-1980-4wd.jpg`, `front-34-1980-brochure.jpg`, `front-utility-2wd.jpg` and `rear-utility-2wd.jpg`. The list is in `docs/AUDIT-2026-11.md`; this version fixes all fifteen items (the fifteenth came from the second look, not the photographs). Only the Hilux single cab (`hilux`) changes; the SR5 Xtra Cab has its own body and is untouched.

**What was wrong.** The model was a tall glass box on a shallow body: 0.62 m from the sill to the waist and 0.67 m of glass and roof above it. The real truck has 0.74 m under the waist and 0.5 m above it. The bonnet was level; the real one falls about 0.27 m to a low nose. The body was 1.67 m wide with plain sides; the real body is 1.61 m wide and the 4WD's flares stand out to the published 1.69 m.

**How it was measured.** The folder has no profile (five of its photographs were mislabelled, and `SOURCES.md` now says what each shows), and Wikimedia refused the container again, so none could be added. The heights were read off `front-34-left.jpg` instead: on the near side of that truck the body side and the tyres' outer faces are nearly one vertical plane, and on an upright line in that plane a photograph keeps the proportions between heights. The white truck sits higher than standard, so the body was set on the wheels by the published roof height (1.765 m). The table of each height, read and modelled, is in the vehicle's `SOURCES.md`. Stations along the body were not moved: the white truck is a short bed, and the door and cab back agree with it once its wheelbase is allowed for.

- **Waist and cab (item 1).** The waist, where the door glass stands, is 0.74 m above the origin (`WAIST`, was 0.6) and the roof's crown 1.25 m (was 1.28). The door glass is 0.41 m tall (was 0.59). The cab leans in above the waist to 1.45 m across the drip rails. The doors have their painted window frames, a black weatherstrip along the waist, and the handle just under the upper pressed line.
- **Bonnet and nose (item 2).** The bonnet's shut line falls from 0.74 m at the cowl to 0.47 m at the nose (`shoulder`), and the guards' sides fall with it. The headlamps are 5.6 cm lower (centre 0.36 m) in smaller bezels, and the bonnet's edge sits directly on the grille panel. Two pressed lines run down the bonnet.
- **Width, flares and wheel openings (item 3).** The body sides are 0.805 m out (`SIDE`, was 0.835). Each wheel opening is 0.9 m long (was 1.03 m), its top unchanged at 0.42 m, with a flare round it that swells to a lip 4 cm proud and fades into the side at each foot (`flare`). The tyres' outer faces are now flush with the body sides, under the flares. The cab's inside was narrowed to fit: floor, dash, bench and door trims.
- **Grille (item 4).** Black, in four panels of slats between three uprights, with TOYOTA in white strokes on a plate in the panel on the truck's left. The bezels are dull silver with a chrome ring round each lamp.
- **Winch (item 5).** Inside the bar, as on the LC79, the GQ and the Troop Carrier: its control box shows on the beam's top between the hoop's legs and the roller fairlead on a plate in the face.
- **Tub and tailgate (item 6).** The tub's rail is level with the waist, with a rolled outer lip and four rope hooks a side under it; the floor is 3 cm higher, so the tub is 0.41 m deep. TOYOTA runs across the tailgate in raised off-white letters 0.15 m tall, with the two latches at its top corners as on the Australian ute.
- **Bull bar (item 7).** 1.80 m wide (was 1.96 m). The centre hoop is 0.6 m up (was 0.7 m) and its legs 0.475 m out, between the grille and the lamps; the branch over each headlamp is 0.5 m up. The collision box was already 1.70 m wide and is unchanged.
- **Tail lamps (item 8).** 0.41 m tall on each corner post, amber over clear over red.
- **Mirrors (item 9).** A black rectangular head 0.15 m wide on a short arm from a foot on the door, under the vent window. They are still working mirrors.
- **Cab vent, fuel flap, pressed lines, badge, hooks, mud flaps (items 10 to 13).** A black louvred vent on the cab's side behind each door, just under the waist. A square flap on the tub's right side ahead of the wheel opening, in place of the round cap on the left. The upper pressed line at 0.665 m along the doors and tub, falling along each guard to the nose, and a lower one at 0.24 m. 4WD in chrome strokes on each guard behind the wheel opening. A mud flap behind each rear wheel.
- **Windscreen (item 14).** Its foot is at the door's front edge (it stood 7 cm ahead of it) and it leans back 34 degrees to a header 1.175 m up.
- **From the driver's seat.** The cab's inside was already laid out for a dash at this height, so only the eye moved: 3 cm lower (1.03 m), 0.145 m under the header. The bonnet now falls away out of sight, so the track shows closer to the nose. The visors and headlining moved down with the roof, and the rear-view mirror hangs on a stem from the header.
- **Chassis, under-tub spare and tailpipe (item 15, from the second look).** Behind the cab each chassis rail kicks up 0.155 m over the rear axle (`KICK`), as a real ladder frame does; the rails ran straight. The spare under the tub hangs against the underside of the raised rails, so its cradle is 0.35 m off the ground (was 0.19 m, below the axle), and the angle from the rear tyres' contact to the lowest point at the tail is about 19 degrees (was about 10). The tailpipe climbs over the axle inside the frame, crosses over the right-hand rail under the tub's floor and comes down behind the wheel; it ran straight back at axle height, through the rail. The kick-up's height is an estimate: no photograph in the folder shows the chassis from the side.
- **Collision boxes.** The cab and tub boxes are 1.6 m wide (were 1.64 m), the lower cab and tub boxes reach the waist at 0.74 m (were 0.66 and 0.6 m), and the upper cab box is 1.48 m wide. The rail boxes follow the kick-up, and the under-tub spare's box is 0.15 m higher, so the tail drags on a step later than it did. Mass, balance and inertia are unchanged.

**Where each part is attached and which way it faces.** Each flare is part of the guard or tub side round the wheel opening, facing out. Each door mirror stands on a foot on the door's skin 0.2 m behind its front edge, its glass facing back. Each cab vent lies on the cab's side between the door and the cab's back corner, facing out. The fuel flap lies on the tub's right side, facing out. The rope hooks stand out of the tub's side under the rail's lip. The tailgate's letters are raised on its face, facing back, reading left to right from behind. Each mud flap hangs from a strip under the tub's lower edge, 7.5 cm behind the wheel opening, facing the tyre, its foot 0.27 m off the ground. The winch's control box sits on the beam's top between the hoop's legs. The 4WD badges lie on the guards' sides and read front to back on the left, back to front on the right (the right way from outside). The under-tub spare lies flat, face down, against the underside of the raised chassis rails behind the axle, on two cradle bars, each hung by a rod at either end from the two rear crossmembers. The tub spare lies flat on the tub's floor between the wheel boxes, face up, under two straps, one each side of the hub, that run down to the floor. Both are placed with `mountSpare()`. The jerry can stands on the tub's floor behind the rack's headboard, held by a strap round the can and the headboard's lower rail. Each shock absorber hangs from a bracket under the roof of its wheel well.

**The second look.** A reviewer was given 54 pictures and nothing else: the standard set, the 28 audit pictures and eighteen close-ups (the grille, the nose from the side, above and below, both sides of the cab, the windscreen pillar's foot, a mirror, the fuel flap, the tail from behind, the side and low down, the cab's back, both wheel arches, the tub from above, the cab inside, and a far side view). It found no wheel or spare edge-on or facing the wrong way, nothing touching or below the ground, and every rack post, bar, mirror, lamp and flap attached. What it did find, and what was done:

- **The rack headboard's lower rail ran through the jerry can** (there before). The can stands 10 cm further back, behind the rail, and its strap goes round the can and the rail.
- **A black rectangle on each wheel box inside the tub** (there before, smaller). It was the dark wall of the wheel well, standing 5 mm inside the tub through the box's painted wall. The painted wall is 4 cm further in and the dark wall behind it.
- **The spare under the tub hung very low**, 0.19 m off the ground and below the axle (there before). Fixed with the chassis kick-up above (item 15). Its cradle bars had free ends; each end now hangs by a rod from a crossmember.
- **Rods in the wheel arches ending in mid-air.** They were the shock absorbers' towers, drawn by the shared suspension. The Hilux now uses that code's bracket mounts (as the FJ40 has since v1.89): each shock hangs from a bracket under the roof of its wheel well.
- **The wipers lay on the glass with nothing joining them to the scuttle** (there before). Each has an arm up from a spindle on the scuttle.
- **The tub spare's strap passed through its hub cap** (there before). Two straps, one each side of the hub.
- **The rack's front feet overhung the front of the tub's rail.** The front frame is 2 cm further back (7 cm behind the tub's front wall), and the boards' clamps moved with it.
- **A dark gap under the windscreen pillar's foot, and a black nub near its top.** The pillar runs 4 cm further down, into the corner of the guard and door; the vent window's divider stops 2.5 cm lower, inside the frame.
- **The white corner lamp's lower edge sloped.** The guard's skin swelled out through it; the lamp stands 7 mm further out.
- Not changed: **a faint dashed line on each door** in the far side views only, running down from the foot of the glass past the lock. It is in the pictures taken before this version too, at the same place, and does not show in a close or a long-lens view of the door. It was not traced to a part.

Each fix was photographed again and read. While checking them, the headlamp beams' positions (`lamps.head` in `src/render/vehicleLooks.ts`) were moved to the new lamp centres. `tests/spares.test.ts` passes.

56.1k triangles with wheels and boards (body 40.2k).

## v2.10: the Hilux's bonnet line

The owner looked at v2.09 from the side and saw a wedge, and asked how the bonnet compares with the real truck's. Only the Hilux single cab (`hilux`) changes.

**What was wrong.** v2.09 drew the bonnet's shut line as a straight ramp from the waist at the door (0.74 m above the origin) to a nose 0.47 m up: 0.27 m of fall, with the headlamps 0.36 m up. The real line leaves the cowl nearly level and rolls down towards the nose, 0.21 m in all, and the lamps are 0.40 m up.

**Why.** v2.09 read the bonnet off `front-34-left.jpg`, the white 4WD, and took its camera to be at about the waist's height. It is at about the roof's: the tops of the roof and the bonnet both show. For the cab that made no difference, because those heights were read on one upright line, where a photograph keeps proportions whatever the camera's height. For the bonnet it did, because those points run away from that line towards the nose. With the camera at the roof's height the same picture gives 0.735 m at the cowl, 0.67 m over the front axle and 0.53 m at the nose.

**A lower photograph.** Commons' category for this truck has 108 files and none named as a side view, but `Toyota Hilux N30 001.JPG` (Tennen-Gas, CC BY-SA 3.0) is a three-quarter view of a Japanese two-wheel-drive RN35 taken from 0.11 m above the waist, so it sees the bonnet nearly side-on in height. It is now in the folder as `front-34-left-rn35-2wd.jpg`, the original file. Its horizon was found from the tub's rail and the sill, its scale at each wheel from the tyre, and heights from the hubs: sill 0, waist 0.735, roof 1.235 (which confirm v2.09's cab), shut line 0.73 at the cowl, 0.68 over the front axle and 0.53 at the nose, headlamp centre 0.40 m. Two more low views there could not be fetched at full size from this container; `SOURCES.md` links them.

- **Bonnet line.** `shoulder()` in `src/render/hiluxBody.ts` is a curve: 0.74 m at the door, 0.68 m over the front axle, 0.615 m 0.35 m ahead of it and 0.53 m at the nose (it was 0.74, 0.62, 0.545 and 0.46). The guards' sides, the bonnet, the wing tops, the bonnet's two pressed lines and the guard's pressed line all follow it.
- **Nose.** The headlamps, grille, bezels, corner lamps and side lamps are 4 cm higher (lamp centre 0.40 m), and the bonnet's edge is 4 cm above the bezels at the sides and about 7 cm in the middle. The bonnet's crown at the nose is 2.4 cm (was 3.5). The panel under the grille is 4 cm taller.
- **Bull bar.** The centre hoop is 0.66 m up and the branch over each lamp 0.56 m (were 0.6 and 0.5), 0.1 m over the bonnet's edge as before.
- **Headlamp beams** start at the new lamp centres.

**Where each part is attached and which way it faces.** Nothing was added. The bull bar's hoop still stands on the beam's top, its legs between the grille and the lamps; the lamps face forward in their bezels on the front panel.

**Compared again.** The model was photographed from the camera position worked out for `front-34-left-rn35-2wd.jpg` and put beside it, and from square-on to each side.

**The second look.** A reviewer was given 17 pictures and nothing else: the standard set and nine close-ups (the bonnet square-on from each side, the front three-quarter, the grille, the nose from the side, above and below, the windscreen pillar's foot, and the bonnet from the driver's seat). It found nothing floating, no wheel or spare facing the wrong way and nothing on or below the ground. What it did find, and what was done:

- **A dark pocket at each front corner, behind the bull bar's bend.** With the nose higher, the corner was open between the grille panel's top and the wing's top, outboard of the bonnet's front lip. The lip now runs under each wing's front edge to the guard's corner. Both corners were photographed again, closed.
- **A short post in the front wheel arch with a cut-off top** (there before v2.09, and noted by that version's reviewer). It is the front spring's hanger, drawn by the shared suspension 0.48 m out; the Hilux's front chassis rails are 0.35 m out, so its top stood in the air. An outrigger from the rail now carries each of the four front hangers. The rear hangers already lie against the rear rails.
- **The door's window frame ended in a square-cut end on the guard's top.** Its foot runs 2 cm down into the guard.
- Not changed: **a grey sliver at the end of each wheel box in the tub**, in the picture from above. It is the box's end wall, seen at a grazing angle with the sky reflected in the paint; a close picture from above shows the box whole. **A spring hanger stops a few pixels short of the spring's top leaf**, seen from the ground under the bumper: the shared suspension's hanger and leaf, the same on every leaf-sprung vehicle. **The bull bar's outer uprights stand in front of the corner lamps** from dead ahead, as they did in v2.09. Square black corners behind the centre hoop in the straight-on view are the door mirrors.

`tests/spares.test.ts` passes.

56.5k triangles with wheels and boards (body 40.5k).

## v2.11: the Troop Carrier, stage 2 (grille, wheel openings, rear and sides)

The owner asked to keep going with the vehicle models. The Troop Carrier's list in `docs/AUDIT-2026-11.md` had items 9 to 13 open after v2.08; this version does them, which closes it. Before any change the standard studio set and side, front and rear-quarter close-ups were taken, and a studio side view from 40 m with an 8 degree lens was laid over `side.jpg` (scaled from the hubs, 271 px a metre, and turned 0.96 degrees to their line). The photographs compared were `side.jpg`, `front-34.jpg`, `front-34-b.jpg`, `detail.jpg` and `front-34-ambulance.jpg`. Only the Troop Carrier (`troopy`) changes; the LC79's guard badge font moved to the shared `bodyKit.ts` unchanged, so the Troop Carrier's stripe could use it.

- **Grille (item 9).** One dark grey panel across the face from indicator to indicator, 1.46 m wide and 0.27 m tall, holding the round headlamps in raised squares at its ends. Between them the grille is a black recess in a frame with three horizontal bars (it was 27 vertical slats between separate lamp surrounds). TOYOTA stays in letters on a black plate on the middle bar: the 1992 Australian ambulance (`front-34-ambulance.jpg`) shows letters, the Peruvian car the oval badge, and the game's car is Australian.
- **Wheel openings (item 10).** The black lips are gone; the openings are pressed into the body and painted. Their tops are 1.5 cm lower (`ARCH.y` 0.42, was 0.435): on the overlay the black lips had made the opening's visible top 0.495 m, 7 to 8 cm above the photograph's. Each front guard's lower half is now a flare, as on `front-34.jpg`: it stands 18 mm out from the guard, its top crease 0.53 m up (`FLARE.top`), running from under the indicator over the opening and back to the door. The rear openings have a narrow lip, 3.5 cm wide and 1 cm proud. The wheelhouse roofs are 1.5 cm higher relative to the openings and reach into the side panels, so the tyres keep the 3 cm they had at full bump.
- **Rear quarter (item 11).** A black louvred vent on each side under the stripe's end, 0.27 by 0.09 m, where `side.jpg` and `front-34-b.jpg` have it. On the right side only, two square fuel flaps (0.19 by 0.15 m, shut line and a lock at the back edge): the main tank's behind the rear wheel under the vent, the sub-tank's behind the door. The round caps behind each rear wheel are gone. A grey and gold stripe in two bands (0.67 to 0.785 m) runs from each door's front edge to the rear quarter, with LAND CRUISER in black in the wide band at its end, reading from the front on the left and from the back on the right. On both photographed cars the stripe also runs along the front guard; the model's guard rounds over at the shoulder where it would lie, so it starts at the door. The upper swage line (0.72 m) went with it, and the lower one moved from 0.5 to 0.54 m, under the vent and in line with the flare's crease, now running unbroken from the door to the tail. The door handle is chrome and 4 cm lower (0.625 m), under the stripe, as on `side.jpg`.
- **Tail lamps (item 12).** A tall lamp on each rear corner, 0.21 to 0.57 m up: red over clear over amber (`side.jpg`). It stands 4 cm out behind the rear face, 0.1 m wide and 3 cm in from the side, so from the side it shows as a strip behind the body's edge, as on the photograph. The small low lamps on the rear face and the lamps in the rear bar are gone; the corner lamps' red lights with the brakes. The doors' lower hinges are 6 cm higher (0.68 m), clear of the lamps' tops. The rear bar is 1.86 m wide (was 1.78) and its ends wrap forward along the body sides to the vent's front end, as on `side.jpg`. Which rear door carries the spare is still not shown by any photograph.
- **Mud flaps and steps (item 13).** A rubber mud flap behind each wheel (0.3 m wide, 0.3 m tall, its foot 0.24 m off the ground), and a black step under each door, 1.19 m long and 0.2 m wide, standing 3.5 cm out from the body (`front-34.jpg`, `front-34-b.jpg`).

**Where each part is attached and which way it faces.** Each front flare is part of the guard round the opening, facing out; each rear lip is part of the body side round the opening, facing out. The grille panel lies on the front clip's face, facing forward, the lamp squares and grille frame on it. Each vent lies on the rear quarter, facing out. The fuel flaps lie in the right side's skin, facing out. The stripe bands and the lettering lie on the body sides, facing out. Each tail lamp is fixed to the rear face at the corner, its lenses facing back and out. Each rear bar end is part of the bar, running forward along the body side below the sill. Each mud flap hangs from a strap that runs from the chassis rail out under the body's lower edge, just behind the wheel opening, and faces the tyre (its face is across the car, so it shows edge-on from the side). Each step lies on two arms bolted to the chassis rail, under the door, tread up. The door spare (moved, below) still faces back from the wide right door on its carrier; the roof spare is unchanged. Each shock now hangs from a bracket under the roof of its wheelhouse.

**The second look.** A reviewer was given 74 pictures and nothing else: the standard set, the 28 audit pictures and 38 close-ups (the grille from ahead, the side, low and above; each front flare from the side, ahead, behind, above and low; the rear lips; both vents; the fuel flaps from afar and close; the stripe and both sides' lettering; the tail lamps from behind, the side, a rear quarter and low; the rear bar's ends; each mud flap from behind, the side and low; the steps from the side, low, behind and above; the door handle). It found both spares facing the right way, nothing on or below the ground, and the flaps, steps, hooks, bar uprights, mirrors and aerial all attached. What it did find, and what was done:

- **The black shut line at each front door's rear edge broke where the swage line crossed it.** The swage ran unbroken across the door's rear edge and covered the line. The swage and each stripe band now stop either side of it, as a pressing and a decal do.
- **A thin bright strip across the top of each front wheel opening.** Light came straight across the car between the wheelhouse's inner wall, which stopped at 0.40 m, and its roof at 0.43 m (the black lips had hidden it). The inner walls reach the roofs now, front and rear.
- **The door spare's carrier uprights stood over the foot of the right door's window** (there before). They stop 2 cm under the glass (0.45 to 0.93 m; they ran to 1.02 m).
- **The door spare covered the inner top corner of the right tail lamp from close behind** (there before, when the lamps sat lower and further in). The spare and its carrier are 3 cm further in (`SPARE_X` 0.30, was 0.33), 4 cm clear of the lamp. From a camera 2.4 m behind, the spare, 0.4 m nearer, still just touches the lamp's corner in the picture; from further back there is a gap.
- **A strut in each rear wheel opening and a cone in each front one, ending in the air** (there before; v2.08 noted them as the shared suspension's shock towers).
