The new `recovery:9` passed.

## v2.26: no wheel spin in neutral, seat backs that lean back, the Range Rover's roo bar

The owner reported three things: "the tyres spin when revving in neutral", "all vehicles' seats tilt forwards rather than lean back", and "Range Rover bullbar sticks out a lot, is that accurate?". Each was looked at first (a failing test for the first, renders for the other two), and all three were right.

**1. Revving in neutral** (`src/sim/vehicle/vehicle.ts`). A standing car on loose ground with the throttle in is taken to be bogged: its wheels spin, throw dust and dig in. That test read the pedal alone (`driving`), so it fired whenever the engine was running, including in neutral, between gears and with the clutch in. It now also needs the drivetrain engaged (not neutral, clutch more than 5% home). `tests/neutralSpin.test.ts` holds a Hilux on the handbrake at the test camp, in Manual: full throttle in neutral for two seconds (the revs rise, no wheel spins, the car stays put), then in 1st (the wheels spin). Before the change the first half failed with a spin of 0.25. Automatic has no neutral and is unchanged.

**2. Seat backs** (every cabin). The frame is −z forward, so a seat back leaning back is a positive turn about x. Every cabin that leaned its backs turned them by a negative angle, about a point at floor level, which also carried the whole back forward: in a section through the cabin every back leaned over its cushion towards the wheel. `VehicleBuilder.seatBack(x, y, z, lean)` (`src/render/vehicleGeometry.ts`) now places a back about its own middle and says which way is back, and all fifteen vehicles use it. Where each back is attached and which way it faces:

- **Seats with room behind** (the front seats of the FJ40 and the code-built 40, 60 Series, MQ, Range Rover, Troop Carrier and LC79, Xtra Cab and 80 Series, and the 60's and MQ's rear benches): the foot stays where it was, on the rear of the cushion, and the top leans back 0.1 to 0.2 rad. Headrests lean with the back (the 80's stood upright on its own).
- **Backs against a rear wall, or already behind their cushions** (Hilux bench, Unimog, 40 Series ute, GQ Patrol front and rear): the rearmost point stays where it was, so the top now rests where the foot did and the foot comes forward onto the cushion. The Hilux bench's back is 8 cm longer at the foot (the wall showed under it once it leaned the right way) and its pleats are on the face you sit against (they were on the face against the wall).
- **Range Rover rear bench:** the first spare stands in the load bay right behind it. Leaning back about its foot put the back into the tyre (`tests/spares.test.ts` failed: "fouled at these points across its face"). It keeps its rearmost point and sits 2 cm further forward; the test passes.
- **Land Rovers** (Camel Trophy 90 front and rear seat, Perentie): their backs stood upright. They now lean 0.1 rad with the top where it was.

**3. The Range Rover's roo bar** (`src/render/rangieGear.ts`, `src/data/rangie.ts`). Measured in the studio's side view: the pan was bolted on ahead of the factory bumper (pan from 0.10 m to 0.39 m ahead of the grille), which stood the hoops 0.34 m and the fairlead 0.44 m ahead of the grille. A winch bar takes the bumper's place rather than hanging in front of it. The pan now starts where the bumper blade does (0.02 m ahead of the grille; it is 4 cm wider and covers the blade, and the blade's wrapped ends show behind it as its wings), so its face is 0.25 m ahead of the grille, the hoops 0.2 m and the fairlead 0.3 m: 14 cm less. `winchMount` moved back with it (z −2.46 to −2.32), as did the two driving lamps on the centre hoop. The hoops stand on the pan's top, the cross rail runs through them, the lamps are on the centre hoop's top rail facing forward, the control box is on the pan's top behind the centre hoop, and the plate and the two shackles hang from the pan's face and underside. The car is 4.84 m over the bar and tow ball (4.98 m). **Not compared with a photograph:** Wikimedia again refused the green car's pictures (HTTP 429), so the new reach is reasoned from where a winch bar mounts, not measured; `SOURCES.md` says to check it when the file can be had. The 60 Series' and GQ Patrol's bars reach about as far ahead of their grilles as the Range Rover's did; they were not asked about and are unchanged.

**The studio can cut a section.** `studio.view()` takes an eighth argument, `near`: everything closer to the camera than that is cut away, so a side camera with the cut just inside a door shows the cabin in section. `scripts/studio-closeups.ts` passes it through (`name:x,y,z,tx,ty,tz,fov,near`). This is how the seats were looked at.

**Looked at.** Before: a section of all the cabins and the Range Rover's nose from the side, from above and from the front quarter. After: the same, the standard studio set for all fifteen vehicles, a section and a look across the seats for each, and six close-ups of the Range Rover's bar (side, above, front quarter, low, the corner where the pan meets the bumper's end, and down onto the pan).

**The reviewers** (three, five vehicles each, given only the 157 render paths). On the parts changed here:

- **GQ Patrol: the driver's headrest stood through the roof.** Real, and older than this change (the headrest's top was at 1.29 with the headlining at 1.215; moving the back only made it show in the section). The back is now 8 cm shorter and the headrest 4 cm, with its top at 1.18. Rendered again: it is under the lining.
- **Range Rover: the load-bay spare meets the rear seat back in profile.** They were 8 mm apart; the back moved 2 cm further forward. Rendered again: a gap shows.
- **Range Rover: a separate black block at the bumper's corner, set back from the bar.** That is the factory bumper's wrapped end behind the pan, as intended.
- Nothing else on any seat, cockpit view or the bar ("the hoops stand on the bumper top, the cross-rail runs through the hoops, the driving lights sit on the centre hoop, and the plate and shackles are attached and clear of the ground").
- Not faults: `rangie/loadbay.png` was a bad camera (inside the roof); the MQ's "wedge panel" is its sun visor close to the camera; thin outlines in the sections are the cut.

They also listed things on parts this change did not touch, **not fixed here**: the code-built 40's rack legs do not reach its gutter blocks, its boards overhang the rack's ends and its jerry can stands in front of the right rear door; the FJ40's rear spare shows only a post under its tread; the Camel's snorkel runs along the wing top, and a green triangle shows beside the box by its winch; the Perentie's under-floor spare cradle is the lowest thing on the car; the Unimog's steering wheel rim rests on the driver's cushion, its roof hatch lid hovers above the roof from the side, a pale drum's pipe stub ends at the rear tyre, the side spare shows no carrier, and a loose bar lies on the canvas; the Troop Carrier's front arch shows a pale panel behind the tyre; the 80's headrest is right at its headlining.

**Tests.** Unit: 121 files, 1,119 passed and 2 to do, run before the Patrol and Range Rover rear-bench follow-ups; `tests/spares.test.ts` (85) was run again after them.

## v2.27: the vehicle faults the v2.26 reviewers listed on other parts

The v2.26 reviewers listed seventeen things on parts that update did not touch, on seven vehicles. Each was rendered close up before anything was changed (`scripts/studio-closeups.ts`, with the section cut where the part is inside a cabin), the code for the part was read to see what it is, and each was then fixed or left with a reason. The before and after close-ups have the same names, so they pair up.

### The code-built 40 (`forty`, `src/render/vehicleEquipment.ts`)

**1. Rack legs on the roof skin: real, fixed.** Each post sank 3 cm into the roof skin, and its outrigger ran straight to its gutter clamp through the corner of the roof cap, so only a nub showed and the three clamps a side sat on the gutter with nothing reaching them. The posts are now stubs under the deck rail that stop 16 mm above the roof (their feet went from y 1.54 to 1.59). Each outrigger goes out over the roof's edge to a knee (x 0.85, y 1.59) and straight down outside the cap, 22 mm clear of its side, to the clamp. The clamps are where they were (x 0.835, y 1.465, on the gutter strip). The legs moved from z −0.29 / 0.66 / 1.52 to −0.20 / 0.68 / 1.56: the gutter runs from z −0.30 to 1.66 and the old front clamp hung 4 cm past its front end. **Attached:** three clamps a side on the gutter, an outrigger up from each to a post under the deck rail. The rack touches the roof nowhere.

**2. Boards overhanging both ends of the rack: real, fixed.** The 2.4 m boards (z −0.61 to 1.79) lay on a 2.02 m rack (−0.40 to 1.62): 0.21 m out at the front, 0.17 m at the rear, and through the top 1 cm of both end rails. The car now carries the 2.5 m deck the FJ40 already had (end rails at z −0.53 and 1.97, twelve slats, six uprights a side), and the boards lie at z −0.48 to 1.92, 28 mm inside each end rail. The rack overhangs the roof by 0.15 m at the windscreen and 0.06 m at the tail, and does not show from the driver's seat. The 0.13 m shift that only the FJ40 gave its boards is now part of `buildRackPlank`, and `FJ40_PLANK_SHIFT` is gone; the FJ40's boards are where they were. The two straps on each board moved to z −0.03 and 1.37, where both cars have a cross slat, so each strap goes round the board and the slat under it. **Attached:** the boards lie flat on the slats, lengthwise, down the left and middle of the deck, strapped to two slats.

**3. Jerry can on the rear bumper in front of the right door: real, fixed.** The can stood end-on in a cradle whose floor (y 0.2475 to 0.2825) was almost wholly inside the bumper's top (y 0.285): it stood on the bumper and the door could not have opened. It now has the FJ40's holder (`fj40RearCan` became `doorCan(b, door, x, y, stay)`), all of it on the right door and swinging with it: two flat bars bolted to the door skin, a tray, a stay under each side back to the door, and a strap. The can lies flat against the door with its broad side facing back, centre x 0.33, y 0.64, z 1.93 to 2.09 (it reached 2.125 end-on). The tray's underside is at y 0.375, 9 cm above the bumper; the stays meet the door at y 0.31; the cap is at 0.925, under the window (1.0); the can sits between the door handle (to x 0.145) and the door's hinges (from x 0.50).

### The FJ40 (`shortie`, `src/render/vehicleEquipment.ts`)

**4. The rear spare showing only a post under its tread: real, fixed.** A section with the tyre cut away showed that the swing-away carrier was all there (the arm along the sill, the post, a plate and a boss behind the hub), but everything above the sill was behind the tyre. Two things were added. An upper hinge bracket on the fixed left rear corner (x −0.69, y 0.60), with an upper arm from it to the post, in the clear band between the left door's two hinges: it shows from the side and the left rear quarter. And a clamp on the wheel's face: a stud from the boss out through the wheel's centre, a 0.44 m bar across the rim and a tee handle, the same clamp the roof spare has. **Attached:** the carrier is hinged at two brackets on the body's left rear corner and latched to the rear bar; nothing is fixed to a door. The spare faces back, hub at (−0.365, 0.78, 2.05), 40 mm off the door skin. It is the Blender model's own wheel (moved by `SPARE_SHIFT` in `fj40Glb.ts`, not touched), not a `mountSpare()` one, and `tests/spares.test.ts` builds the code body for `shortie`, so that test does not check this wheel: the renders are the check. The car's rearmost point is now the tee handle at z 2.198 (it was the tyre at 2.16): 4.30 m overall, from 4.27 m.

**5. Snorkel head tight against the rack's front-left leg: real, fixed.** The head's rear end was at z −0.255 and the front clamp began at z −0.22, level with it and 35 mm behind. The rack's legs moved from z −0.17 / 0.70 / 1.55 to −0.02 / 0.76 / 1.55, which leaves 185 mm between head and clamp. The snorkel did not move: it follows the pillar. **Attached:** the clamps are on the gutter as before (x 0.778).

Not changed, seen while looking: on the code-built 40 the right door's handle is partly behind the door spare's tyre from straight behind, as before. No photograph in `landcruiser-fj40/` covers the rack, the can holder or the carrier's details (they are the game's fittings); `rear-34-left.jpg` only confirms a swing-away carrier hinged on the left corner with the spare across the left door. A centre clamp bar on the door spare is the game's fitting: a real carrier holds the wheel by its nuts.

### Unimog (`unimog`, `src/render/unimogInterior.ts`, `unimogBody.ts`, `unimogCanvas.ts`, `src/data/vehicles.ts`)

**6. Steering wheel rim on the driver's cushion: real, fixed.** In section the rim's lowest point was at y 0.98 and the cushion's top at 0.97, with the rim 3 cm behind the cushion's front edge. The seat was also too high for the cab: with the cushion at 0.97 the driver's eye (the camera, y 1.52) was 0.55 m above it and a seated head would have been in the lining at 1.71. Both seats are 0.15 m lower: cushion top 0.97 to 0.82 (0.28 m above the cab floor, 0.70 m under the eye), base 0.32 to 0.17 m high, back 0.62 to 0.68 m long with its rearmost point on the rear wall unchanged. The wheel is flatter: the column is 25° from vertical (it was 45°), the wheel's centre moved from (0.5, 1.15, −1.45) to (0.5, 1.13, −1.48), and the column's foot from (0.5, 0.78, −1.82), where it hung under the dash, to (0.5, 0.57, −1.74), on the floor. The rim's lowest point is now y 1.02: 0.20 m above the cushion, from 0.01 m. The wheel could not go higher: the line from the eye to the speedometer crosses the wheel 0.10 m above its hub, and any higher puts the hub over the dials. From the driver's seat all three dials still show through the wheel's upper half; the rim's top arc sits a little lower in view, just under the dash rail. The pedals moved so the column stands between clutch and brake (x 0.36 / 0.52 / 0.68 to 0.34 / 0.60 / 0.74; the brake was right behind the new column foot). **Attached:** the seats stand on the cab floor against the rear wall, backs leaning back 0.12 rad; the column stands on the floor behind the dash; the wheel faces the driver, 25° off flat. Knee room is still tight, because the cab is short.

**7. Roof hatch lid hovering: real, fixed.** The lid sat on a bare black seal ring, which read from the side as a dark gap under it, and its two hinge blocks did float, 14 mm above the roof skin. A painted flange (radius 0.285, 14 mm proud) now sits on the roof skin, and the lid (radius 0.257, 26 mm thick) sits on the flange with a thin seal bead at the joint. **Attached:** two hinges at the back and a latch at the front each stand on the roof and reach over the lid's edge, a steel pin runs through both hinges, and the grab handle stands on the lid. The hatch is over the passenger seat (x −0.48), hinged at the rear.

**8. A pale drum's pipe stub ending at the rear tyre: real, fixed.** The drum is the silencer. Its tailpipe ended at z 1.35, 2 cm from the tread and pointing at it; the silencer also ran through the left chassis rail, and its inlet pipe ended inside the rail. The silencer now hangs across the truck outside the rail at (−0.80, 0.02, 1.05), 0.5 m long, radius 0.12 (it was at (−0.62, 0.15, 1.15), radius 0.13). The inlet pipe runs inside the rail under the crossmembers, dips under the rail beside the fuel tank and enters the silencer's front near its inner end (its first route went through a suspension link: see the reviewers, below). The tailpipe is a short stub from the outer end, turned out and down, ending at (−1.14, −0.07, 1.09) under the tray's edge, about 0.2 m ahead of the tread, as `side.jpg` shows the real one. **Attached:** a bracket from the rail's outer face to the silencer's inner end, and a hanger from a band round its middle up to the tray's cross bearer at z 1.12. Its underside (y −0.10) is 0.58 m off the ground.

**9. The side spare with no carrier or strap showing: real, fixed.** The carrier (two rails, two uprights and a hub plate on the left dropside) is all behind the tyre. Added, with the spare still placed by `mountSpare()` and not moved: a spindle from the hub plate out through the wheel's centre, a collar round the hub cap with three arms whose foot pads bear on the rim's flange, and a steel T-bar across the cap. **Attached and facing:** the spare stands against the left dropside facing left (outer face to −x), hub at (−1.39, 0.95, 0.85), held to the carrier's hub plate by the spindle and clamp. A first clamp stood 6 cm proud of the wheel and failed `tests/unimog.test.ts` (body width 3.256 m against a 3.2 m limit); it was made again inside the wheel's own cap and the test passes unchanged. The spare still shows the road wheel's hub cap and nuts (it is the shared wheel).

**10. A loose black bar on the canvas: real, fixed.** There were two: the fore-and-aft rails of the board frame (x ±0.64, y 2.32), 1.62 m long, each stopping 0.11 m short of the cross bars at both ends, so each lay over the canvas held by nothing. They are now 1.935 m and join the two cross bars into one frame. **Attached:** the frame stands on four posts with feet on the canvas over tilt bows 2 and 4; the two boards lie flat, strapped across the cross bars. The feet have no fastening drawn through the canvas (as before).

Not changed, recorded in `unimog-ul1700l/SOURCES.md`: the under-tray layout may be mirrored. `side.jpg` is the truck's right side and shows the tank, a box and the exhaust stub there, and `rear-34.jpg` seems to be the left side with the spare; the model has the spare on the right and the tank and exhaust on the left.

**Also fixed, found in the cab close-ups: no firewall.** A tan patch showed under the dash beside the gearbox hump. A section and a look into the passenger footwell showed what it was: the cab had no front wall below the screen, so both footwells opened into the engine bay and the ground showed through. A firewall (`unimogInterior.ts`) now stands across the cab's front from the floor to the bonnet's top at the cowl (y 0.52 to 1.10, z −1.985), between the side skins, 2 cm thick, painted like the cab's inside. Nothing of it shows from outside.

### Camel Trophy 90 (`camel`): both intended, nothing changed

**11. The snorkel running along the top of the wing: intended.** The equipment account that `camel-trophy/SOURCES.md` cites says of the 1986 cars: "A LR Parts raised air intake ran up the LH windscreen pillar to roof level and entered at the front of the LH wing." A pipe from the front of the wing to the pillar has to run along the wing, and v2.18 built it that way on purpose. The close-ups (from the side, above, the front and the rear quarter) show a box on the front corner of the left wing's top covering the tube's end, the tube strapped to the wing top twice, and two clamps to the cage's left A leg: nothing floats and nothing passes through a panel. No photograph of a 1986 car is in the repository, so this rests on the written account.

**12. A stray green triangle beside the tall black box by the winch: not a fault.** It is the grille's green oval LAND ROVER badge (at x −0.23, y 0.60 on the grille's face). The upright Warn 8274's motor housing (x −0.245, y 0.55 to 0.65) stands in front of it, so from dead ahead a sliver of the oval shows beside the box. From the front right the letters LAND read beside the box.

### Perentie (`perentie`, `src/render/perentieBody.ts`, `src/data/perentie.ts`)

**13. The under-floor spare's cradle the lowest thing on the car, behind the rear axle: real, fixed.** v1.90 and v2.08 left it as "0.2 m off the ground, as the real one is". The photographs do not show that: `front-34-left-county.jpg` (the 1988 truck from directly behind) shows only a few centimetres of tread under the rear crossmember, about on the centre line, and nothing under the tyre; `front-34-county.jpg` shows nothing hanging below the crossmember either. The model had 0.23 m of tyre below the crossmember and a cradle 0.045 m under that, 0.195 m off the ground, with the drawn differential at 0.296 m and the axle tube at 0.348 m.

- **Why it sat so low.** On the centre line the tyre (0.816 m across) cannot go higher: there is 0.783 m between the drawn differential housing (a 0.13 m ball, 0.08 m right of centre, in the shared `vehicleSuspension.ts`) and the crossmember's front face.
- **The spare.** Its hub moved from (0, −0.17, 1.93) to (−0.17, −0.048, 1.89): 0.17 m left, 0.12 m up and 0.04 m forward, beside the differential, with 1.5 cm to the housing and 1.2 cm to the crossmember. The tyre's underside is 0.36 m off the ground (0.24 before), the lowest point of the assembly is the hub plate at 0.31 m (the cradle was at 0.195 m), now above the differential, and 0.11 m of tread shows below the crossmember (0.23 before). It has 5 to 6 cm to the left rear tyre and the left mud flap, and is 8 cm behind the left coil.
- **Chassis rails.** They ran straight at y −0.06 to 0.12, and the axle on full bump stood into them. Each now climbs 0.095 m between z 0.95 and 1.25 and runs back 0.11 m deep (y 0.07 to 0.18) under the load floor (0.185), with a drop bracket from its end to the front of the crossmember. The climb's height is an estimate (no photograph shows the chassis side-on), as the Hilux's is (v2.09).
- **What holds it.** The cradle (two rails, two crossbars, four hangers) is gone. A crossbar between the rails above the wheel, a winding rod down through the wheel's centre and a 0.2 m plate under the hub hold it. That is an assumption: the photograph does not show what holds the real one.
- **Exhaust.** The tail pipe ran back on the left to z 2.4, through where the spare now is. It now turns out under the left rail behind the silencer and ends under the left sill's edge at (−0.84, −0.15, 0.70), 0.29 m ahead of the left rear tyre. `side-county.jpg` shows the real outlet under the right sill ahead of the right rear wheel, with no pipe at the rear; the model's exhaust has always been on the left and stays there.
- **Attached and facing.** The spare lies flat against the underside of the raised rails behind the rear axle, outer face down, 0.17 m left of centre, placed with `mountSpare(…, 'down')`. Its crossbar spans the two rails, the rod hangs from the crossbar through the hub, and the plate is under the hub, facing down. The rails' drop brackets stand on the front face of the rear crossmember. The exhaust's outlet points out to the left under the sill, ahead of the left rear wheel.
- **The collision box follows.** The spare's box in `src/data/perentie.ts` was centred, with its underside 0.46 m off the ground: since v1.90 the drawn tyre hung 0.21 m below it. It is now the drawn tyre (`cx −0.17, cy −0.045, cz 1.89`, half-height 0.115: underside 0.37 m off the ground), which takes the angle the tail clears from about 24° to about 22.5°. So the car touches where the tyre is drawn, and that is a little sooner than before.
- **Still disagrees with the photograph.** About 6 cm more tread shows than on the real truck, and the spare is 0.17 m left of centre where the real one looks central. Getting closer needs a smaller differential in the shared axle drawing or a thinner crossmember. The exhaust is on the left; the photograph shows the right. The tail pipe passes about 2 cm under the left rail and the left trailing link with the car parked (see the reviewers, below), and was not checked with the axle drooped.
- `land-rover-110-perentie/SOURCES.md` has the new figures, and two rows of its photograph table are corrected: `front-34-county.jpg` and `side-county.jpg` show the truck's right side, not its left.

### Troop Carrier (`troopy`, `src/render/troopyBody.ts`)

**14. A pale panel showing through the back of the front wheel arch, behind the tyre: real, fixed.** It was the cab's footwell bulkhead (`troopyInterior.ts`, the body colour at 0.7), seen through the open back of the front wheelhouse. The rear wheelhouses were closed at both ends; the front ones were open at the back. A dark panel in the wheelhouse's colour now closes the back of each front wheelhouse. **Attached and facing:** it lies against the opening's sloping back edge, 1.2 cm behind it, from the sill (y 0.04) to the wheelhouse roof (0.45) and from the inner wall (x 0.34) to the side skin, facing forward into the arch. `side.jpg` shows the real arch dark all round.

### 80 Series (`sooty80`): a camera artefact, nothing changed

**15. The driver's headrest right at the headlining: not a fault.** Measured by casting rays up through the built body: the headrest's top is at 1.20 m and the ceiling above it at 1.287 m over the seat's middle and 1.278 m over the headrest's outer edge, 8 to 9 cm clear. The driver's eye is at 1.12 m, so the top of a head (about 1.235 m) has 5 cm. In a side section the headrest lines up with the far side's cant rail, and in a section looking forward with the windscreen header; both are lower than the roof over the seat. From inside the cabin, open space shows above it.

### Looked at

Before: the standard studio set and close-ups of each reported part on all seven vehicles (128 pictures). After: the standard set for the five vehicles changed (code-built 40, FJ40, Unimog, Perentie, Troop Carrier) and the same close-ups again (109 pictures), every one opened, and 16 more close-ups of the parts the reviewers raised. The Camel's and the 80's "before" pictures stand, since nothing on them changed. Triangle counts (`scripts/tri-count.ts`): both 40s 58.9k, Unimog 65.8k, Perentie 68.8k, Troop Carrier 78.9k.

### The reviewers

Two reviewers were given only the paths of the 109 "after" pictures (one the two 40s and the Troop Carrier, one the Unimog and the Perentie). Every finding was checked against the code or a new close-up. A third was then given the 23 pictures taken after the first round of fixes below, and what it found was fixed and rendered again as well.

**Real, fixed:**

- **Unimog: the intake's cap floated above its pipe.** The tube stopped 3 cm under the cap (older than this update). It now runs up into the cap. Rendered again: the cap sits on the pipe.
- **Unimog: the hatch's hinge pin lay on top of the hinge blocks.** It is 14 mm lower and runs through both blocks, 5 mm proud at each end. Rendered again from behind the hatch.
- **Unimog: a suspension link ending at the exhaust pipe.** The new inlet pipe crossed under the left rail at the chassis end of the left rear trailing link (x −0.42, z 0.975) and passed 7 mm into it. The pipe now comes out under the rail by z 0.8, ahead of the link, and enters the silencer's front near its inner end, 0.15 m clear of the link. The silencer itself is 9 cm outboard of the link; the reviewer's "tubes against the muffler" is the links seen behind it.
- **Perentie: nothing holding the tail pipe, and the silencer against the rail.** The silencer's side was 5 mm inside the left rail's inner face (older than this update): the pipe and silencer are 2 cm further in. The tail drops a little more, which puts it 2 cm under the rail and the trailing link. A rubber hanger joins the tail to the rail where it passes under it, and a second hangs its end from a bracket under the body's sill.
- **Third reviewer, Perentie: the tail pipe stopped under the body, short of the side, and its hanger had no bracket at the top.** The pipe ended 0.15 m inboard of the sill's edge. It is 0.1 m longer and ends at x −0.84, just inside the body's side, and the hanger has a bracket under the sill.
- **Third reviewer, Unimog: the inlet pipe's end lay half off the silencer's end, the silencer's hanger was hard to see, and nothing joined the hatch's lid to its hinge pin.** The pipe enters the silencer 6 cm further along it (x −0.66), wholly inside its front. The hanger is thicker (12 mm) and comes up from a steel band round the silencer's middle. A hinge leaf on the lid's top reaches back over its edge to the pin.

**Looked at, not changed:**

- **Perentie: the spare hard against the differential and the rails, left of centre and below the crossmember.** As built and recorded above: it lies against the underside of the rails (that is what it is wound up to), beside the differential's housing with 1.5 cm to it, not through it.
- **Perentie: the rear axle's details from underneath** (a toothed ring at the axle's end, nuts on the wheels' inner faces, links ending on the axle tube), and **the bonnet spare in the driver's view.** The shared axle and wheel drawings and the Perentie's own bonnet spare; none is part of this update.
- **Unimog: a second wheel lying flat under the tray on the right.** The under-tray spare, in its cradle with a brace at each outer corner (looked at again, close up). The truck carries two.
- **Unimog: the upright spare against the dropside and the tilt, and its lowest lugs below the top of the box under the tray.** The spare is 0.26 m outboard of the fuel tank (the lugs line up with it only in side view). It stands on a carrier on the left dropside, so that dropside cannot be lowered with the wheel on it; the game's fitting, unchanged.
- **Unimog: one hanger rod on the silencer.** There is also a bracket from the rail to its inner end.
- **Unimog: the board frame's feet on the canvas.** They stand on the tilt over bows 2 and 4 with no fastening drawn through the canvas, as before.
- **Unimog: a pale rectangle on the passenger's side of the dash.** A placard.
- **Unimog (third reviewer): a bright sliver in the dark panel beside the exhaust, the mirror arms crossing the intake stack.** The sliver is the sky reflected in the bevelled lower edge of the fuel tank (black metal), not a hole. The two rods at the stack are its own stays to the windscreen pillar.
- **Perentie (third reviewer): a small dark piece on the pipe behind the silencer; the bonnet spare close to the windscreen; parts close above the front tyre.** The first is the new hanger between the rail and the pipe. The others are not part of this update.
- **Code-built 40: the door spare laps onto the right door, and nothing visible holds it.** A plate on the left door behind the hub carries it (seen from above with the roof cut away). It overhangs the door seam by 0.12 m, 0.2 m behind the doors, as the factory carrier's spare does in `rear-34-left.jpg`: the left door opens first. Unchanged.
- **Code-built 40: a black cylinder under the rear.** The rear differential's housing.
- **FJ40: no visible gap between the spare and the body.** Seen from above with the roof cut away there is a gap, with the carrier's arm crossing it.
- **FJ40: the spare stands out past the body's left side, sits 4 cm off the post's centre, and the carrier's arm crosses the door seam.** The Blender model's wheel on the v1.89 carrier, hinged at the left corner and latched to the rear bar; not part of this update.
- **FJ40: the jerry can over the right door's shut line.** Seen square-on its edge is at the shut line, not over it; where it was before this update.
- **FJ40: a small pale shape on the bonnet from the driver's seat**, and the **Troop Carrier's** shock mount, mirror glass, aerial, covered door spare and boards: none is part of this update.
- **Troop Carrier: a pale hairline inside the arch's rear edge, and flecks at its top corner.** Close up it is the body-coloured inner edge of the arch's lip, ahead of the new panel. Widening the panel did not change it, so the panel was left as built. At any ordinary distance the arch is dark all round.

### Tests

Unit: 121 files, 1,119 passed and 2 to do, run with the Perentie's new collision box in place and before the reviewers' follow-ups; `tests/spares.test.ts`, `tests/unimog.test.ts`, `tests/perentie.test.ts` and `tests/troopy.test.ts` (107) were run again after them. `tests/spares.test.ts` is unchanged and passes (85).

## v2.28: every engine builds to a howl at its rev limit

The owner likes how the Range Rover's engine howls at high revs and asked for the other engines to give that sound at the limiter (spec: issue #247; this is its first ticket, #248). More revs and more power at the top are the second ticket.

**What the Range Rover does, measured.** A script revved each engine against its limit and reported what the sound code sees. The Range Rover's sound limiter (5200 rpm) sits 4% above the rev limit its physics cuts at (5000 rpm). So held flat out every cylinder still fires, and the limiter character it inherits from the 40 Series' six adds intake roar, valve noise and harshness as the revs near the limit. With eight cylinders at 5000 rpm, that is the howl. It does not stutter with the throttle held: the stutter and pops come only if the revs are pushed past 5200.

**What the others did.** Only the 40 Series' six and the LC79's diesel had limiter character, and theirs sits at the physics limit, so they bounce there (as built in v0.89 and since). Every other engine had none: where its sound limiter was close to its rev limit, half its firings were dropped at random (the Hiluxes, the Perentie, the Unimog, the Troop Carrier, the Patrol, the 80 Series); the HJ60 and the MQ never reached theirs, so nothing changed at the top. The Camel Trophy Ninety used the Perentie's sound, with its limiter at 3650 rpm, but revs to 4200: it dropped firings at random over its last 550 rpm.

**What changed.**

- Every engine now has limiter character of its fuel's kind: the petrol fours take the petrol six's (ignition cut), the diesels the LC79's (fuel cut). A diesel with no turbo gets no extra turbo dump on a lift-off; the 80 Series, which is turbocharged, keeps it.
- Each of these engines follows the Range Rover's arrangement, through one helper (`howl()` in `src/data/audio.ts`): the sound limiter 4% above the vehicle's rev limit, and the build-up starting 6% below that limit, so it is about 60% of the way in when the physics cuts. Held at the limit the engine keeps its full note and gets brighter; it bounces only if pushed past the limit, as on a steep descent in gear.
- The Ninety has its own entry, with the limiter where its own rev limit puts it.
- The Range Rover, the three 40 Series and the LC79 are unchanged: their sound settings compare equal before and after.

**The owner was told something different, and why.** In the planning conversation the petrol engines were to get "the Range Rover's stutter and pops". That was read from the settings the Range Rover inherits, before the measurement showed its revs never reach the point where they apply. What was built is the sound he asked for, the Range Rover's. The 40 Series and the LC79 keep their bounce at the limit, because it was built on purpose; whether they should howl instead is his call after hearing this.

**How much.** Rendered offline at the rev limit, the sound above 2 kHz is 11–26% stronger than the same engine without the character (the Range Rover: 11%), and the level stays inside full scale at the limit and past it.

### Tests

`tests/limiter.test.ts`: every vehicle has limiter character of its fuel's kind; every vehicle's sound limiter sits where its rev limit puts it (at the limit for the 40 Series and the LC79, 4% above for the rest), which is the check that would have caught the Ninety; the build-up has not started a tenth below the limit and is under way at it. `tests/engineSynth.test.ts`: each engine rendered at its limit is brighter than without the character, the same a tenth below, and inside full scale at the limit and past it. These three files and `tests/audio.test.ts` pass (43). The full unit run was started after publishing, as the shipping rule says.

Not checkable here: whether it sounds right. That is the owner's test.

## v2.29: engines rev a tenth further and pull a little harder at the top

The second ticket of the engines spec (issue #247, ticket #249). The owner asked for every engine other than the Range Rover's to rev a bit more, and for each vehicle to have a little more power at maximum revs.

**What changed, per vehicle.**

| Vehicle               | Rev limit was | Now  | Full-throttle upshift now | Unchanged up to (rpm) | Extra air at the limit |
| --------------------- | ------------- | ---- | ------------------------- | --------------------- | ---------------------- |
| 40 Series (all three) | 4300          | 4700 | 4150                      | 2800                  | 10.4%                  |
| Camel Trophy Ninety   | 4200          | 4600 | 4150                      | 2400                  | 12.3%                  |
| Hilux single cab      | 5500          | 6100 | 5550                      | 3600                  | 11.1%                  |
| 60 Series             | 3900          | 4300 | 3850                      | 2400                  | 6.1%                   |
| LC79                  | 4100          | 4500 | 4150                      | 2600                  | none                   |
| MQ Patrol             | 3900          | 4300 | 3750                      | 2200                  | 10.2%                  |
| GQ Patrol             | 4000          | 4400 | 3950                      | 2000                  | 10.8%                  |
| Perentie              | 3600          | 4000 | 3650                      | 1900                  | 8.0%                   |
| Range Rover           | 5000          | 5000 | 4600                      | 2500                  | 5.8%                   |
| 80 Series             | 4000          | 4400 | 3950                      | 2600                  | none                   |
| Troop Carrier         | 4000          | 4400 | 4050                      | 2200                  | 10.7%                  |
| Unimog                | 2800          | 3100 | 2900                      | 1800                  | 6.9%                   |
| Hilux SR5 Xtra Cab    | 5500          | 6100 | 5550                      | 2800                  | 10.6%                  |

- **Revs.** Each rev limit rises by a tenth, rounded to a hundred; the Range Rover's stays at 5000. The tachometers' red zones, the HUD and engine wear already read the limit from this data. The automatic's full-throttle upshift rises in the same proportion, so the extra revs are used without switching to manual; light-throttle shifts are where they were.
- **The torque curve** is stretched above a pivot (the first point of the curve at or above both peak torque and half the old rev limit): every point above it moves up the revs in proportion, so the fall-off that used to come at the old limit comes at the new one. Points at and below the pivot keep their values. The Range Rover's curve is not stretched: its points above 2500 rpm are raised, by 8% at the limit.
- **The airflow limit was in the way.** The engine model also caps torque by the air the engine can draw (`airflow / rpm`), and measuring what is delivered at full throttle showed that this cap, not the curve, already set the top-end pull of seven engines (the 40 Series delivered 163 Nm at its limit where its curve says 190). Stretching the curve alone would have lowered the power delivered at the new limit on most of them. Raising `airflow` would have fixed that but changed every part-throttle position at every speed, crawling included. So the engine data has a new optional setting, `topEnd`: the air the engine can draw rises in a straight line from `airflow` at the pivot to `airflow × (1 + air)` at the rev limit. Below the pivot nothing changes at any throttle opening.
- **The target** for each vehicle: torque delivered at the new limit such that power there is 8% above the power delivered at the old limit. `air` is set to give exactly that where the cap governs; where the curve governs, the curve's point at the limit is set to it. The LC79 and the 80 Series have air to spare and need none.
- **Sound.** Each sound limiter follows its vehicle's new limit through `howl()` (v2.28); the 40 Series' moves to 4700 and the LC79's to 4500, so they still bounce where the physics cuts. `tests/limiter.test.ts` holds the two together.

**What it does on the road** (flat ground, full throttle, automatic, measured before and after):

| Vehicle          | 0–60 km/h was | Now   |
| ---------------- | ------------- | ----- |
| Range Rover      | 7.2 s         | 6.9 s |
| 40 Series        | 6.6 s         | 6.3 s |
| Hilux single cab | 9.4 s         | 8.8 s |
| Troop Carrier    | 10.9 s        | 9.8 s |
| GQ Patrol        | 10.5 s        | 9.5 s |
| LC79             | 4.4 s         | 4.2 s |
| Unimog (0–40)    | 8.5 s         | 8.0 s |

The Unimog's top speed goes from 68 to 70 km/h.

**Side effects, kept.** Engine heat works on the share of the rev limit, so at the same revs an engine with a higher limit runs a little cooler; flat out at the limit it heats as before. The rev limits no longer match the real engines' (the Unimog's real governor is at 2800 rpm, the Xtra Cab's brochure power is at 4800): the owner asked for this.

### Tests

New: `tests/engineRevs.test.ts` holds each engine's v2.28 figures (rev limit, peak power, upshift, torque delivered at full throttle at each curve point) and checks that every engine but the Range Rover revs about 10% further to a round hundred; that each delivers 6–10% more power at its limit than at its old one; that the bottom half of the revs pulls exactly as it did; that nowhere does an engine pull less; that peak power is not down and not up by more than 10%; and that the full-throttle upshift moved with the limit and stays under it.

Changed, because they pinned figures this update moves on purpose: `tests/unimog.test.ts` (rev limit 3100; 40 km/h in 7.5–13 s, under 73 km/h flat out), `tests/camel.test.ts` (rev limit 4600), `tests/xtracab.test.ts` (the brochure's power point is now at 5250 rpm on the curve), `tests/engineHeat.test.ts` (the hard climb is 93% of the revs, not a fixed 4000 rpm).

These and `tests/limiter.test.ts`, `tests/engineSynth.test.ts`, `tests/drivetrain.test.ts`, `tests/manualGearbox.test.ts` and `tests/audio.test.ts` pass (10 files, 109 tests). The full unit run was started after publishing, as the shipping rule says; the scripted drives are the part it will tell us about.

## v2.30: mud flies as small flecks, the way the wheel is turning

The owner's report (issue #252): the mud flicked up from the tyres is too chunky, and often spits out in the wrong direction when bogged.

**Wrong direction: the cause.** The spray took its direction from the sign of the car's speed (`dir = v.speed >= 0 ? 1 : -1` in `src/render/particles.ts`), not from which way the wheels turn. A bogged car's speed sits near zero and reads a little forwards one moment and a little backwards the next, so the mud flipped; in reverse while stuck it went out the back. Reproduced in the browser before looking at the fix: in the long drive's bog, first gear, full throttle, the car read a speed of −0.001 m/s, which the old rule throws forwards.

**The fix.** `wheelTurning()` in the new `src/render/mudSpray.ts`: a wheel that is spinning (wheelspin above 0.15) turns the way its gear drives it, whatever the car is doing; a wheel that is only rolling turns the way the car is going (so rolling back down a climb in a forward gear still throws mud up the hill). The flecks start on the side of the contact the tread leaves and go that way, faster the harder the wheel spins, as before.

**Too chunky.** Each piece was a 20-sided lump with a radius of 6 to 15 cm, so 12 to 30 cm across. Agreed with the owner: mostly flecks 2 to 5 cm across, about one in fourteen a clod of up to 8 cm, each drawn out along its path (1.6 to 2.8 times as long as it is wide, the small ones the most) and pointed the way it is going, so it reads as flung mud. The particle pool gained a per-piece `stretch`; water droplets, which share the pool, are unchanged (stretch 1, tumbling as before).

**More of them.** 100 a second per wheel at full wheelspin (45 before) and 30 from rolling speed (14 before), living 0.5 to 1 s (0.7 to 1.3 before), so four wheels at full spin still fit the pool of 520. The quality preset scales the rate as it did.

**Looked at.** Side-on, close, in the long drive's bog: in first gear the flecks leave behind each wheel; in reverse, ahead of each wheel. On the Low preset (the screenshots' setting) they are sparse; Medium and High throw more. They read as flecks, not lumps.

**Also in this update:** `tests/mq.test.ts` allowed the MQ Patrol 11 to 18 s to reach 60 km/h, and v2.29's engines take it there in 11.0 s; the full unit run on v2.29 found it. The bound is now 10 to 18 s.

### Tests

New `tests/mudSpray.test.ts`: a bogged car in a forward gear throws mud backwards whichever way its speed reads, and forwards in reverse; a rolling wheel turns the way the car goes; harder spin throws faster; flecks are 2 to 5 cm across with fewer than one in ten up to 8 cm, all drawn out; more pieces than before, inside the pool. It and `tests/mq.test.ts` pass. The full unit run on v2.29, in a separate checkout, was half way through when this was published and had found only the MQ bound; the next update's notes record how it ended.

## v2.31: signs that can be read from the driver's seat

The owner's report (issue #253): the signs are basically unreadable by the player.

**Why.** Every sign was one line on a board 1.45 m by 0.36 m, drawn on a 256 × 64 texture, with long wording shrunk until it fitted. A short name was lettered 0.17 m high at best; "Nolans Brook — in, right to the big rock, then across" came out 0.07 m high. The hand-lettered face is also thin, so at a distance its strokes faded into the board.

**What changed.**

- **The wording is set out by `signLayout()`** (`src/sim/world/signLayout.ts`, pure): it breaks at each dash, slash or dot, then between words, over up to three lines of lettering 0.30 m high. Wording that does not fit in three lines comes down in steps to 0.24 m and no further. An arrow stays on the line it points from.
- **The board is as big as the wording needs:** 1.2 to 2.2 m wide and 0.54, 0.87 or 1.21 m high for one, two or three lines, with its top edge where it was (2.05 m, just under the top of the post). Its width stops at 2.2 m because a post may stand 1.2 m from a track's edge (`tests/episodeSigns.test.ts`), and a wider board would hang over the track.
- **The lettering is bolder and darker:** each stroke is painted thicker and the board is a little paler, so the lettering stays dark when the board is only a few dozen pixels wide on the screen. The texture is 160 pixels to the metre and is kept sharp at a slant.
- **Read from behind, the post no longer crosses the lettering.** A double-sided sign had one board on the front of its post with the back lettering between board and post, so the post stood across the middle of it. It now has a second board on the back of the post. A sign with an arrow is one-sided, as before.
- **No sign had to be reworded.** The longest, Nolans Brook's, fits in three lines at 0.28 m. (That sign goes in the next update for another reason: it says where to steer.)

**Looked at from the driver's seat** (the 40 Series, 1280 × 720, the Low preset, which is the hardest case): "Eliot and Twin Falls" (two lines) and Nolans Brook's three lines can both be read at 12 to 14 m, and not at 22 to 26 m. Seen from behind, the lettering is clear of the post. On a sharper screen or a higher preset they read from further off. If 12 m is still too close at the speeds the owner drives, the next step is taller lettering on a wider board, which needs the posts moved further from the tracks.

**Where each part is.** The front board is on the front of the post, the back board on its back, both centred on the post and both with their top edge 5 cm below the post's top; the lettered faces lie half a centimetre proud of each board's outer side.

### Tests

New `tests/signLayout.test.ts`: a short name is one line at full size, over 1.7 times the old best; long wording wraps at its dash, then between words; arrows and slashes; the board's size; and every sign on every track (over 40) is lettered at or above the smallest size allowed, in three lines or fewer, on a board no wider than the limit. `tests/signs.test.ts` (both faces lie flat on the board at every heading; one face with an arrow) passes unchanged.

## v2.32: Nolans Brook can be read from the bank

The first ticket (#255) of "ground that tells the story, beyond Canal Creek" (spec: issue #254). The owner chose to carry Canal Creek's tools to the next places without waiting for the uncoached play test, which stays on the roadmap. At Nolans Brook he also chose: the two steering signs go (he reports the signs could not be read anyway), the big rock stays, the words say how to read the brook, and a late arrival finds the brook harder to read.

**Nothing a wheel meets has changed.** The bed, the depths, the shoal, the holes, the soft bottom, the current and the rise are what they were. The scripted drives across the brook pass unchanged.

**1. Clear water** (`clear: true` on the brook). As at Canal Creek: the shoal, 0.55 m down, counts as shallows in full, so its bed shows through and its surface breaks as the brook runs; the brook's ordinary 1.4 m and the dug-out straight line are at the darkest colour.

**2. A dark bed under the deep** (new, `src/sim/world/terrainGen.ts`). Switching clear water on was not enough: looked at from above and from the seat, the deep water was mid-grey, not dark, because the brook's bed is pale sand and a third of it shows through even deep water. Canal Creek did not have this problem because its bed is dark gravel and only its shelf is pale. So in a clear creek over sand, the bed under water deeper than 0.9 m, off the bars, is now drawn in the dark creek-bed colour. The shoal and the shallows along both banks keep their pale sand. Only the tint: the surface a wheel meets is the same.

**3. It fades with the rain, as agreed.** The clear look already fades as a creek rises and is gone at 0.3 m of rise (`clearWater.siltedAt`, now named and tested). At Nolans, which takes the whole of the storm's rise, that is about two thirds left at 4 pm, an eighth at 5 pm ("still shows faintly until about 5 pm", as the owner asked) and none from about 5:15 pm on the commonest day. Two things let the shoal show through the mud all the same, and were changed for every creek: muddy water is now drawn nearly opaque (it was two thirds opaque whatever its silt, so the dark and pale beds showed through it), and browner (0.7 of the way to mud at full silt, from 0.55). Looked at from above at 5:40 pm the brook is brown from bank to bank.

**4. Worn wheel lines** along the brook's own track, which are drawn on dry ground only: down the near bank to the water, and from the far water's edge up to the camp. The track goes into the water at x = 0 and comes out at x = 18, so the far-bank lines are well to the right of where the near ones go in: that, and the pale band, are the clue.

**5. The signs and the rock.** "Turn across here" (above the rock) and "Aim here from the rock" (on the far bank) are removed. The big rock stays at the water's edge on the near bank, where the shoal's line across starts: it is what is left to go by when the water is brown. The approach sign, "Nolans Brook — in, right to the big rock, then across", now reads "Nolans Brook — deep crossing".

**6. The words.** The hint was "Into the water, hard right to the big rock, then straight across to the sign. Crawl"; it is "Read the water: pale and broken is the shoal, dark is deep. Far-bank wheel lines show the way out. Crawl". The note keeps the history (Bridge Creek, the biggest obstacle south of the Jardine, a soft sandy bottom, the old line dug out, one line shallower and firmer) and then tells the driver to stop and read the water, says what the wheel lines and the rock are for, and that the rain takes the pale water away. The map note and the briefing no longer say "hard right". After a recovery, the cue back to the line carries the new hint.

**Looked at** (the game in a browser, Low preset, the short 40, the commonest day). Midday from above: a pale, broken band from the near bank across to where the far-bank wheel lines come out, dark water either side, the dug-out hole dark beside it with the drowned HiLux at its edge, pale shallows along both banks. Midday from the driver's seat on the bank: the pale band between dark water, leading right towards the far-bank wheel lines, the HiLux in the dark. 5 pm from above: the band only faintly paler. 5 pm from the seat, in the rain: grey-brown water with little to choose, the far-bank wheel lines still plain. 5:40 pm from above, with the lights on: brown bank to bank. The first midday look, before the dark bed, is what showed the deep water was not dark.

**If it is too hard late in the day,** the lever is `clearWater.siltedAt` (a larger figure keeps the pale water longer) or putting one sign back.

### Tests

New `tests/readableNolans.test.ts` (8): the brook asks for clear water and is marked for it; the shoal is shallows in full and under half-way to the deep colour, the deep at the darkest; the pale bed on the shoal and in the shallows and the dark one under the deep, with other sand creeks unchanged; how much of the clear look is left at 12, 3, 4, 5 and 5:30; depths and surfaces as they were; the wheel lines (dry ground, a wheel track either side of the line, the near bank to within 3 m of the water, the far bank out at x > 15); the rock kept and the two signs gone; no "left", "right" or "straight" in the hint, the map note or the note. Changed: `tests/readableCreek.test.ts` (two creeks are clear now, and Nolans' track has wheel lines) and `tests/telegraphNorth.test.ts` (the rock without its signs). These and the other Telegraph North tests, the drives included, pass (7 files, 79 tests).

**The full unit run on v2.29** (in a separate checkout): 122 files, 121 passed; 1,128 tests passed, 2 to do, and 1 failed, the MQ Patrol's 0–60 bound that v2.30 corrected.

## v2.33: the long drive's bog shows who went in and who went round

The second ticket (#256) of "ground that tells the story, beyond Canal Creek" (spec: issue #254). The owner's choice: two sets of wheel lines at the bog, deep cut-up ruts running straight into it and a fainter line skirting it on the firm side, with the note changed to say what cut-up dark ground means and not where to steer. One set alone would either hide the way round or mark it like a route.

**Nothing a wheel meets has changed.** The bog, the sand, the winch rocks and the scripted drive are what they were.

**The wheel-line tool can do more** (`TrackDef.wheelMarks`, `src/sim/world/wheelMarks.ts`). A stretch was a pair of distances along a track, and its two lines lay a wheel track either side of the track's own line, all equally worn. A stretch can now also be a `WheelMarkStretch`: a strength (under 1 is fainter and drawn thinner; over 1 is a deep rut, drawn wider with its marks overlapping more, which makes it darker), an offset to one side that can change along the stretch, and `cutUp`, which lays a second mark beside each one, scattered either side of the line, so the ruts look churned and not ruled. The old pairs work as before (Canal Creek's and Nolans Brook's are unchanged).

**At the bog.**

- **The deep ruts** start 40 m before the bog's middle, at strength 1.3 and cut up by 0.22 m, move 0.8 m towards the bog's side as they near it, run through the dark patch and stop 14 m past its middle. (1.6 was tried first and looked like black paint from the seat.)
- **The faint pair** start 36 m before the bog's middle on the track's own line, at strength 0.45, swing 2 m out to the firm side by 10 m before the middle, hold that past the bog, and are back on the track's line 30 m past it.
- **Where the firm ground is.** The surface was mapped across the track at seven places along the bog: the black soil reaches 1 m past the track's line on the firm side at its widest (the bog's nominal edge is half a metre). So the faint pair's bog-side line is a quarter of a metre clear of the mud and its other line is on the grass beside the track. The first position tried (1.3 m out) had its bog-side line in the mud, which the test caught. By the two winch rocks, which stand 17 m either side of the bog and 3.6 m out with a radius of 0.8 m, the lines are back in to 1.3 m out, so a car on them clears the rocks by about 0.65 m.

**The words.** The hint was "Soft sand: 18 psi. At the bog, keep the left wheels on the firm edge; a rock to winch to at each end"; it is "Soft sand: 18 psi. Dark, cut-up ground is soft: see which wheel lines go in and which go round. A rock to winch to at each end". The note says the same at more length and no longer says which side the rocks or the firm edge are on.

**Looked at** (the game in a browser, Low preset, the short 40, 1 pm). From above: two thick dark ruts run up the track into the dark patch; a thin pair leaves them, passes the patch on the side the rocks are on, over the edge of the grass, and rejoins the track beyond. From the driver's seat 32 m before the bog: the dark ruts under the bonnet running to the dark patch ahead, the thin pair veering off beside them, a rock beside the track. From the chase camera at the same place: the same, with the hint showing.

### Tests

New `tests/readableBog.test.ts` (6): two sets, strong and faint, with nothing in between at the bog; the strong ones reach the bog's middle on black soil, on its side of the track's line, scattered; the faint ones are never on mud or black soil, pass on the rocks' side, and are back within 2.4 m of the track's line by the rocks; the two part company more than 25 m before the bog and join more than 25 m after; the bog itself is as it was; no "left", "right" or "straight" in the hint or the note. `tests/readableCreek.test.ts` now lists the long drive among the tracks with wheel lines. These, `tests/readableNolans.test.ts`, `tests/telegraphNorth.test.ts`, `tests/telegraphNorthStage2.test.ts` and `tests/telegraphNorthDrive.test.ts` pass (6 files, 79 tests).

## v2.34: the CREB's three fords with a line to pick can be read

The third ticket (#257) of "ground that tells the story, beyond Canal Creek" (spec: issue #254): the full treatment at the Daintree, the Little Annan and the middle creek on the Bloomfield Track.

**Nothing a wheel meets has changed.** Depths, beds, bars, holes and currents are what they were.

**A count to correct.** The owner was told the CREB has ten creek crossings, three with a line to pick and seven without. Mapping every place a CREB track goes under water found eleven wet stretches, and one of them is the Bloomfield River under its bridge, which is not forded. The three with a line to pick take four of the rest (the Little Annan has two: the road's ford and the bar on the Shallow crossing fork). That leaves six plain fords for the next ticket, not seven: the two paddock creeks, Roaring Meg, Donovan's, and the southern and northern creeks on the Bloomfield Track.

**1. The Daintree** (`clear: true`). The gravel bar (0.6 m) is pale and broken, with a pale gravel bed as at Canal Creek; the river beside it (1.2 to 1.6 m) and the two holes scoured into the bar (about 1.1 m) are dark. Wheel lines along the whole crossing track, which follows the bar: down to the water and out on the far bank.

**2. The Little Annan** needed depths of its own. It is a small river: the road's ford, "where it is deepest and fastest", is 0.55 m deep and the bar downstream is 0.28 m. By the usual depths both count as shallows in full, and the whole river would have been drawn pale and broken with nothing to choose. `CreekDef.clear` can now be an object giving the depths the creek is drawn by; the Little Annan's are darkest from 0.6 m, shallows in full to 0.32 m and none from 0.5 m. So the bar is pale and broken and the road's ford is dark and smooth. Wheel lines either side of the road's ford and either side of the bar on the Shallow crossing track.

**3. The middle creek on the Bloomfield Track** (`clear: true`). The ford (0.55 to 0.64 m) is pale and broken; the scour hole 5.5 m downstream (over 1 m) is a dark, smooth patch just below it. Wheel lines either side of the ford.

**How a creek gives its own depths.** `features.creekClear` holds 0 for ordinary water, 1 for clear water at the usual depths, and 2 + k for a creek with depths of its own, which are entry k of `features.creekClearDepths`. `shallowsOf()` and `depthTone()` in `src/render/water.ts` take the depths.

**The words.** The Daintree's note said "Don't drive straight across: a gravel bar curves downstream"; it now says a bar keeps the water shallower and does not run the shortest way across, and to read the water: pale and broken over the bar, dark and smooth where deep, the holes dark patches in the pale. The Little Annan's note keeps its description of the two ways (they are a signposted fork) and adds what each looks like from the bank. The creek-crossings note adds the middle creek's hole. "Slow right down" became "Slow to a crawl", only because the test for directions looks for the word "right".

**Looked at** (the game in a browser, Low preset, the short 40, 9 am). The Daintree from above: a pale band across the river, dark blue water either side on the upstream side, the two holes dark spots on it, boulders, wheel lines on both banks; from the driver's seat at the water's edge: pale broken water ahead with the boulders standing in it and darker water beyond. The Little Annan from above: the river dark along its length, the road crossing it in the dark, and the bar pale and broken where the river widens. The middle creek from above: a pale, broken creek with one dark patch beside the ford's line and wheel lines on both banks; from the driver's seat: the pale band and the wheel lines, the hole hard to pick out at that low angle. Two of the first seat photographs were taken with the camera still above the creek and were taken again.

### Tests

New `tests/readableCreb.test.ts` (7): clear water at the three creeks and no others; the Daintree's bar shallows in full with a pale bed, its holes and the river beside it dark; the Little Annan's own depths, its bar pale and its ford dark; the middle creek's ford pale and its hole dark; depths as they were; wheel lines within 4 m of the water on the way in and out at all four wet stretches, on dry ground; no "left", "right" or "straight" in the three notes. It, the other three readable-ground tests, `tests/crebForks.test.ts` and `tests/crebStretches.test.ts` pass (6 files, 44 tests). The CREB's scripted drives are in the full unit run, started on v2.33 in a separate checkout and checked in the next update's notes.
